PREFACE
This book has been written as a text for a course in digital and
sampled-data control systems. However, since it was prepared
with the needs of modern scientists and engineers in mind, it should
also prove useful as a reference book for scientists and engineers
working in control systems and related fields. The author has put
much effort into preparing a book that may be studied with a
minimum amount of guidance.
The bulk of the material presented here has been taught by the
author since 1958 in a graduate course at the University of Illinois.
However, with the recent advances in the theory and practice of
sampled-data systems, more than one semester is now needed to
cover the entire up-to-date subject. If the book is used as a text in
a one-semester course, some of the chapters will have to be skipped
for lack of time.
The author assumes that the reader has a knowledge of the basic
theory of feedback control systems, although he need not have had
prior contact with the theories of sampled- or digital-data systems.
The reader should also have adequate training in ordinary dif-
ferential equations, the Laplace transformation and its applica-
tions, and the complex variables theory. In order to comprehend
the material in Chapter 11, he would need some background on the
theories of proability and statistics.
Both the z-transform and the state transition techniques are
emphasized in this book. The z-transform method is straightforward
vii

in its application to sampled-data systems. Through the use of the
z-transform method, many of the well-known analysis and design
techniques of continuous-data systems can be modified and applied
to sampled-data systems. The state transition approach permits a
unified study of a great variety of sampled-data systems. It utilizes
the signal flow graphs and the state transition flow graphs to give a
unification between computer and analytical solutions.
The book deals with the following topics: linear analysis, linear
design and synthesis, statistical approach, and nonlinear systems.
Chapter 1 serves as an introduction; the historical background of
sampled-data systems and the importance and advantages of sam-
pling in control systems are the highlights of this chapter. Chapter
2 gives the physical interpretation and a comprehensive mathe-
matical description of the sampling process. Chapter 3 describes
how a signal may be recovered from its sampled version and in-
cludes problems related to the process. Chapter 4 introduces the
theory and basic applications of the z-transformation and presents
the theorems and the limitations of the z-transforms. The analysis
of sampled-data systems begins in Chapter 5. This chapter covers
such topics as the block diagrams, signal flow graphs, matrix rep-
resentation, and the state transition method. Chapters 6 and 7
discuss time and frequency responses of sampled-data systems.
Stability studies are carried out using analytical and graphical
methods. Chapters 8 and 9 are devoted to the principles and tech-
niques of systems design. Chapter 10 deals with multirate, skip,
nonuniform, and nonsynchronized samplings. Chapter 11 presents
statstical analysis and design. The final chapter is on nonlinear
systems; these are studied through use of the state transition,
phase-plane, incremental phase-plane, and describing function
methods.
Although most of the material in this text has been derived from
the literature of sampled-data systems, some original ideas have
been included. In a book of this nature reference cannot be made to
all of the original work and contributors in the vast and active field
of sampled-data systems. The author wishes to apologize to those
contributors in the field whose works and names are not mentioned
through either oversight or ignorance.
The author wishes to acknowledge with gratitude the encourage-
ment and help he received from his colleagues at the Electrical
Engineering Department of the University of Illinois. The author
is also indebted to his graduate students, whose questions and class-
room discussions have assisted in many ways in the preparation
of the manuscript. The encouragement and interest of Dean W. L.
Everitt, editor of this series, and Dr. E. C. Jordan, Head of the
viii

Electrical Engineering Department of the University of Illinois,
have been invaluable in the preparation of the manuscript. Finally,
the author's special thanks go to his wife Margaret, whose patience
and understanding made the tedious writing of this book an enjoy-
able undertaking.
B.C.K.

INTRODUCTION
1.1 WHAT ARE SAMPLED-DATA AND DIGITAL
CONTROL SYSTEMS?
Within the past few years much progress has been made in the
development of sampled-data and digital control systems. These systems
differ from the conventional continuous-data systems in that the
signal in one or more sections of the system is in the form of either
a pulse train or a numerical code such as the binary code. Usually,
sampled-data systems refer to a more general class of systems whose
signals are in the form of pulsed data, whereas the term digital
control systems implies the use of a digital computer or digital

sensing element in a system. In general, a sampled-data system is
defined as one that receives information or data only at discrete
instants of time. The signals in a sampled-data system may be of
the form of a periodic or an aperiodic pulse train, with no infor-
mation transmitted between two consecutive pulses. Figure 1-1a
illustrates in a simple manner how an open-loop sampled-data system
operates. A continuous input signal e(t) is sampled by a sampling
device, and the output of the sampler is a sequence of pulses. If the
continuous input e(t) is represented as shown in Fig. 1-1b, the
output of the sampler, e(t) is shown in Fig. 1-1c. In this case, the
sampler is assumed to have a uniform sampling rate. On the basis
of the signal configurations of Fig. 1-1, the sampling may be regarded
as a pulse-amplitude modulator. The sampling schemes exist in many
variations; some of these are the periodic, cyclic-rate, multirate, skip,
random, and pulse-width modulated samplings. The most common
variations are single-rate and multirate samplings.
A digital control system is defined as a control system in which
the signal at one or more points of the system is expressed in a
numerical code for digital computers or digital transducers in the
system. The appearance of digital signals (such as binary numbers)
in part of the system requires the use of digital-to-analog as well as
analog-to-digital converters. The block diagram of a typical digital
control system using a digital computer is shown in Fig. 1-2. A
sample-data system can also include a digital computer or a digital

Although the use of sampled-data or pulsed-data in control systems

was recognized quite early in the history of feedback control systems,
only recently has significant progress and emphasis been made in the
development of the analysis and design techniques of sampled-data
systems. The complexities that have arisen in the area of automatic
tracking systems and the increased use of digital computers in
control systems have caused considerable activity in the field of
sampled-data and digital control systems.
The exact origin of the engineering adoption of the sampling
concept is difficult to trace. However, a basic form of the sampled-
data system was described by Gouyt some sixty years ago. Gouy

Another classical example of a physical system employing sampled-
data was described by Guanella, who suggested the use of sampling
in electronic feedback amplifiers, electro-acoustical converters, and
radio and other transmitters or receivers for the purpose of eliminating
instability conditions or improving stability conditions. Fig. 1-6a
shows a light control system with sampled-data which was devised
by Guanella. The block diagram of the system is shown in Fig. 1-6b.
The brightness of the light source is assumed to depend upon the
current or voltage supplied by the amplifier A. In addition to the
reflected light, a small part of the light emerges from the source and
is directed upon a photocell. A feedback signal B, proportional to the
output of the photocell is subtracted from the reference signal R
through the transformer T₁. The block S is made up of the elements of
the sampling device that is to sample the signal RB at periodic
intervals. The circuit in S is essentially a double-triode series gate.‡
The triodes conduct and cut-off simultaneously and periodically in
accordance with the control pulse train applied to the grids of the
triodes. Therefore, the impedance between the points 1 and 2 in Fig.
1-6a varies between a small and an almost infinitely large value accord-
ing to the control pulse train. The voltage on the controlling grid of the
pentode is zero whenever the triodes are cut-off and is equal to R-B
during the pulse duration p of the control pulse. Therefore, the block
S functions as a pulse-amplitude modulator or sampler, and its input-
output relations may be described by the waveforms of Fig. 1-1.
The pentode and the condenser C act as an integrating or hold
device. When a voltage pulse of amplitude RB and pulser width.
p is applied to the grid at point 2, a current pulse proportional to
RB with equal duration is passed through the condenser C and
causes a corresponding change in the voltage of C by an amount
proportional to RB. Thus the voltage at the input to the amplifier
A is changed by the amount R(nT) B(nT) at the n-th sampling
instant t = nT. If the pulse signal at point 2 is represented by
en = K[R(nT) - B(nT)] at

used here is not commonly used in the modern sampled-data control

systems. Normally, the function of a hold device is to reconstruct
from the pulsed-data the original continuous signal. The pentode
integrator described in the system does not fulfill this purpose.
However, the present sample-and-hold scheme can be shown to
improve the stability condition of the feedback system, whereas the
conventional hold device, such as a zero-order hold (one that holds
the amplitude of a pulse until the next occurs), used in common
sampled-data systems is known to have adverse effects on system
stability.
1.2 IMPORTANCE OF SAMPLING IN CONTROL SYSTEMS
The examples given in the preceding section on sampled-data
systems merely illustrate the early application of sampling in feed-
back systems. However, in order to fully understand the merits and
the reasons for using sampling in control systems, we should ask
the natural question: Why sampled-data? In other words, what are
the advantages and characteristics of sampling that have made
sampled-data and digital control systems so important in modern
control technology? To answer this question, a survey of the
available class of sampled-data and digital control systems will be
helpful. In general, the applications of sampled-data systems can
be divided into two main categories: systems with inherent sampling
and systems with intentional sampling. These systems are discussed
as follows:
1. Control Systems With Inherent Sampling
In this class of systems, the signals are available only in sampled
form. Examples of these systems are:
1. Radar Tracking System. The signals sent out and received by
a radar tracking system are in the form of pulse trains. The scanning
operation of the radar performs the function of a sampler converting
both the azimuth and the elevation information to sampled-data.
2. Time Sharing Systems. Because of economic factors in the cost
of information transmission, a data transmission link is often shared
among several control systems. For instance, in the multichannel
drift stabilization system shown in Fig. 1-7, one stabilizing amplifier
is used to stabilize a group of d-c amplifiers. With the aid of a
sampler, which samples the error voltage of each amplifier in turn,
† P.G. Pantazelos, "Sampled-Data Analysis of a Drift-Stabilization System," A.I.E.E.
Conference Paper No. CP 56-961, October 1956.

the stabilization amplifier is time-shared among several d-c amplifiers.
Thus the cost, size, and maintenance of equipment is reduced.
3. Digital Computer in Control Systems. Digital computers have
become very important as elements in control systems. There are
several important reasons for this use of digital computers. First of
all, digital computers are light in weight when compared to analog
computers of the same capacity; and conversely, for a given weight,
the capacity of digital computers is far greater than that of analog

8 INTRODUCTION
Chap. 1
computers. In some cases digital computers used as compensators
for control systems are preferable to the continuous type in the
improvement of static and dynamic system characteristics. As an
example, a digital control system used for the control of drill table
position so that a number of specified holes can be drilled in a
work piece is shown in Fig. 1-8. Digital computers are also used
often in modern hot plate mills, with preset programming systems
using devices such as plugboards or punch cards to store the
sequential roll-screw-down settings required to roll an ingot to a
finished plate or slab. Such digital systems simplify the mill operator's
job considerably.
One also frequently finds many other applications of digital com-
puters in control systems: fields such as guidance control for missile
navigation, air-surveillance and weapon control systems, and many
others.
2. Control Systems with Intentional Sampling
There is a class of system in which the sampling operation is
purposely introduced. This is one of the recent trends in the design
of feedback control systems. Some of the reasons for this inten-
tional sampling are given below:
1. Sampling Improves Sensitivity. In servo applications a load must
sometimes be driven by a relatively low-power-level signal. By the
use of sampling in control systems, tremendous amounts of power
can be controlled by sensitive control elements without excessive
power amplification. Also, the loading effect upon sensitive instru-
ments may be minimized by the sampling operation. A classical
example of this improvement in sensitivity through sampling is the
chopper galvanometer described by Oldenbourg and Sartorius, the
system is shown in Fig. 1-9. In this device a small signal is applied
to the galvanometer coil. The chopper bar is lowered periodically and
the projected needle of the galvonometer causes the load to rotate
in proportion to the signal strength. The torque which is applied
to the load shaft is thus determined by the chopper-bar drive rather
than just by torque developed in the galvanometer coil.
† D. J. Gimpel, “Sampled-Data Systems," Control Engineering, February 1957, pp.
99-106.
C. Burdick, "Digital Computer Runs Hot Plate Mill," Control Engineering, January
1960, pp. 126-130.
$R. C. Oldenbourg and R. Sartorius, The Dynamics of Automatic Controls, Chap.
5, publication of the American Society of Mechanical Engineers, New York.

2. Digital Transducers. A transducer in its broadest sense is any
device which can couple unlike systems, transferring energy from
one to the other and converting units. It is generally more useful
for the control engineers to consider that a transducer is a device
that transmits information, regardless of energy level and dimensions.
A digital transducer is any coupling device that transmits data along
in some form of discrete code to other unlike systems or subsystems.
The most important reason for using a digital transducer is the
relative immunity of digital signals to distortion by noise and non-
linearities. Furthermore, digital transducers and sensing devices,
particularly rotary and linear-position sensors, are available with
accuracy and resolution difficult to achieve by analog techniques.
3. Digital Coded Signals. Sampled-data in the form of digital
coded signals can be stored for any length of time, transmitted over
any distance, retransmitted, and detected or used as many times
as necessary with no loss in accuracy. These properties permit
elaborate command signal programs to be formulated and executed
with extremely high accuracy.
4. Behavior. In control systems design, under certain conditions a
sampled-data system with variable sampling intervals or with variable
pulse widths behaves better than a continuous-data system. This is
particularly true in the compensation of systems having plants with
transportation lags.
+ E. J. Kompass, "What About Digital Transducers?" Control Engineering, July
1958, pp. 94-99.

10 INTRODUCTION
Chap. 1
1.3 A SURVEY OF THE METHODS OF ANALYSIS AND
SYNTHESIS OF SAMPLED-DATA SYSTEMS
Very little work was done on the analysis and design of sampled-
data systems prior to 1950. Although early textbooks on servo-
mechnisms written by MacColl (1945), James, Nichols and Phillips
(1947), and Oldenbourg and Sartorius$ (1948) all cover some of the
topics of sampled-data systems, the treatments have mostly been
limited to stability analysis and the derivation of transfer functions
of pulsed systems.
Since the early 1950's when digital computers were first used in
control systems and when the need arose for complex automatic
tracking systems, sampled-data and digital control systems have
enjoyed a most fruitful period of development and resesarch. Not
only have numerous papers been published in the United States and
abroad, but several books devoted solely to the subject of sampled-
data and digital control systems have also become available.
In this section some of the available methods in treating sampled-
data and digital control systems will be indicated, and the major
topics of current interest that need further investigation will be
pointed out along with new problems to be tackled. In general, the
studies of sampled-data and digital control systems can be put into.
the following categories: Linear theory, nonlinear theory, statistical
theory, time varying theory, and digital control theory.
1. Linear Theory
In linear sampled-data theory, the system is assumed to consist
of linear components only. A pulse-amplitude-modulated sampler is
considered a linear device, since its input-output relations satisfy the
superposition principle. A majority of the literature on linear theory
† L. A. MacColl, Fundamental Theory of Servomechanisms, Chap. 10, (Princeton,
New Jersey: D. Van Nostrand Company, Inc. 1945).
H. M. James, N. B. Nichols, and R. S. Phillips, Theory of Servomechanisms, Chap.
5, (New York: McGraw-Hill Book Company, 1947).
§ R. C. Oldenbourg and H. Sartorius, The Dynamics of Automatic Controls.
|| J. R. Ragazzini and G. F. Franklin, Sampled-Data Control Systems, (New York:
McGraw-Hill Book Company, 1958). E. I. Jury, Sampled-Data Control Systems, (New
York: John Wiley & Sons, Inc., 1958). Y. Tsypkin, Theory of Pulse Systems, (Moscow:
State Press for Physics and Mathematical Literature, 1959). J. T. Tou, Digital and
Sampled-Data Control Systems, (New York: McGraw-Hill Book Company, 1959).
E. I. Jury, "Recent Advances in the Field of Sampled-Data and Digital Control
Systems," Proc. of the 1960 Conference of the International Federation of Automatic
Control, Moscow.

Sec. 1.3
INTRODUCTION 11
assumes that the output of a sampler is a train of impulses with
the strength (area) of the impulse equal to the height of the pulse
at the sampling instant. This major assumption is necessary in order
to simplify the mathematics involved in the analysis and design of
sampled-data systems. However, the assumption is justified only if
the actual pulse width of the sampler is small when compared with
the dominant time constant of the system and if the sampler is
followed by a hold device. In the event that the pulse width is wide,
the impulse approximation can at best give a rough estimate of the
actual behavior of the system, and often may even produce mislead-
ing results. However, if the sampler is not followed by a hold device
or smoothing filter, the impulse-modulated approximation is not a
correct description and represents an entirely different system. In fact,
if the hold device is absent and if the pulse width is small so that the
amplitude-modulated pulses are replaced by flat-topped pulses, the
impulse approximation of these flat-topped pulses actually represents
a system with loop-gain equal to 1/p of that of the actual system,
where p is the pulse width.
Once the pulse-amplitude modulated sampler is replaced by an
impulse-amplitude modulated sampler (or ideal sampler), the analysis
of the system can be carried out by use of any of the following
methods:
(1) Difference equation method
(2) Impulse response method
(3) Frequency response method
(4) Signal flow graphs
(5) z-transform and modified z-transform methods.
(6) Root locus and root contour methods
(7) State variable method
These methods are discussed in various degrees of detail in the
ensuing chapters. Although each of the above listed methods has its
advantages and disadvantages, the published works indicate that the
z-transform method is the most popular. The use of the z-trans-
formation for sampled-data systems is entirely analogous to the
application of the Laplace transformation to continuous-data systems.
Sampled-data systems are ordinarily described by equations in the
s-domain with complex coefficients such as e. The z-transformation,
given by the relation z = est, makes possible the description of
sampled-data systems by equations in z with constant coefficients,
and the transfer functions become rational functions in the variable
z. Therefore, most of the widely used techniques for solving linear
continuous-data systems, such as the Nyquist criterion, Routh-Hurwitz
criterion, root locus diagram, or Bode diagram can be modified and

extended to the studies of linear sampled-data systems.
The design and synthesis of linear sampled-data systems can be
divided into two general catagories: continuous-data compensators
and digital compensators. Typical system configurations of continuous-
data compensation and digital compensation are shown in Fig. 1-10.
In general, other configurations such as feedback compensation and
inner-loop compensation are possible. In control system theory usually,
design refers to a procedure which uses the compensator, whether
it be digital or continuous in nature, as the starting point. In other
words, the designer simply has the task of finding a suitable and
physically realizable compensator so that the compensated system
will satisfy the specified performance criterion. In doing so, the
designer usually has to rely to a great extent on his past experience
in choosing the proper compensating network, and usually some
degree of trail and error is involved. Therefore, the philosophy of
design of linear sampled-data control systems follows directly from
the design of linear continuous-data systems, and the conventional
methods such as the Bode diagram, Nyquist plot, or root locus
diagram can be used.
The synthesis of linear continuous-data systems was first intro-
duced by Aaron and Truxal. The philosophy of synthesis is such
† M. R. Aaron, "Synthesis of Feedback Control Systems by Means of Poles and Zero
Location of the Closed-loop Function," Trans. A.I.E.E., 70, Part II, 1951, pp. 1439-1445.
J. G. Truxal, "Servomechanism Synthesis Through Pole-Zero Configuration," MIT
Research Laboratory of Electronics Technical Report, 162, August 25, 1950; and J. G.
Truxal, Automatic Feedback Control System Synthesis, Chap. 5, New York: McGraw-
Hill Book Company, 1955.

Sec. 1.3
INTRODUCTION 13
that the over-all system transfer function C/R is first determined
to satisfy the design criterion and simultaneously satisfy the physical
realizability of the compensator; then the transfer function of the
compensator is determined from C/R. In contrast to the design
procedure, which works from the inside toward the outside, the
synthesis procedure works from the outside toward the inside of the
system. Synthesis procedure similar to that of Truxal's may be
derived for linear sample-data systems.
In general, the following performance criteria are considered for
the design and synthesis of sampled-data control systems with deter-
ministic input:
(1) Frequency domain criteria: bandwidth, peak reasonance M,,
gain and phase margins.
(2) Time domain criteria: For sampled-data systems, deadbeat
responses can be achieved that have no overshoots and have minimum
response times for aperiodic inputs such as step, velocity or para-
bolic. Other conventional criteria are overshoot, peak time, setting
time, steady-state response, and rise time.
(3) Performance indices: Specifications of minimization of error
criteria such as the integral error, mean-squared error, and integral
square error.
2. Statistical Theory
In many applications of sampled-data systems (as well as con-
tinuous-data systems), the actual system inputs or disturbances are
not deterministic but are random in nature. These random signals
or disturbances can often be described only by their statistical prop-
erties in the form of correlation functions or power spectral density
functions. Therefore, statistical analysis and design of sampled-data
control systems are necessarily important.

14
Chap. 1
INTRODUCTION
The statistical design and synthesis of sampled-data control systems
are best illustrated by the block diagram of Fig. 1-11. The main
essence of the design procedure is to obtain an optimum filter (or
system) that will render a minimum error between the actual and
the desired output signals. The design criteria are based mainly
on the minimization of the mean-square error originally suggested
by Wiener. A system designed from the minimization of the mean-
square error has the characteristic of emphasizing the large errors
and passing over many small errors. A large class of control systems
definitely exists for which minimization of the mean-square error
certainly is not the ideal design criterion. However, if the minimi-
zation of the mean-square error is used as the design criterion, the
random signals or disturbances are readily described by the correlation
functions or the power spectral density functions. Most of the
statistical studies of sampled-data systems are based on the assumption
that the random processes are stationary and the systems subjected
to the random signals are linear and time-invariant. However,
intensified efforts on the statistical studies of nonstationary, time-
varying, as well as nonlinear sampled-data systems should also be
emphasized; these subjects definitely are the present and the future
major topics of many investigators in the field.
3. Nonlinear Systems
The studies of nonlinear sampled-data systems can be divided
into two general catagories: (1) Systems with inherent nonlinearities,
and (2) Systems with intentionally introduced nonlinearities. The
first catagory includes the systems with nonlinear behavior purely
due to physical limitations and imperfections of system components,
while in the second catagory, nonlinear elements such as relays
with dead zones and hysteresis are purposely inserted to improve
the performance of the system. A typical relay-type sampled-data
control system is shown in Fig. 1-12. Pulse-width control and
amplitude quantization in sampled-data systems are also regarded as
modified versions of the ordinary relay control. A pulse-width

modulated signal can be thought of as the output of a relay by
controlling the duration of the full positive or negative relay cor-
rection. The amplitude quantizing process is a nonlinear approximation
in the sense that only a discrete set of amplitude levels are produced
for any input signal. The block diagram of the amplitude quantizer
and its input-output characteristics are shown in Fig. 1-13. It is
apparent that the quantizer can be regarded as a relay with multiple-
level corrections. In fact, if the amplitude of the input signal is
small, the quantization effect becomes much more emphasized
because of few levels, and the quantizer becomes an on-off relay.
The nonlinear theory of sampled-data systems has not been
developed fully. The main reason for this lies in the fact that
nonlinear difference and differential equations which described non-
linear sampled-data systems are usually very difficult to solve, and
little has been done in the solution of these equations. The studies
of nonlinear sampled-data systems have been influenced mainly by
the use of the nonlinear theory of continuous-data systems. The
following significant methods of analysis and design have been
developed and investigated:

16 INTRODUCTION
Chap. 1
(1) Phase plane method
(2) Incremental phase plane method
(3) Describing function method
(4) Discrete describing function method
(5) Difference equation method
(6) Matrix method
(7) Second method of Lyapunov
4. Time-Varying and Adaptive Systems
In most control systems the dynamic characteristics of the
controlled process generally vary under actual operating conditions.
This variation may be caused by normal deterioration of the system
components, or drifting of parameters, or other reasons. Usually,
slight variations of system parameters can be counteracted by using
feedback. However, should the variations become large, the system
must be considered to have time-varying characteristics. This initiated
the studies of time-varying systems and adaptive systems. In sampled-
data control systems, a sampler with time-varying rate may be
introduced purposely for the sake of improving system performance.
Adaptive sampled-data systems are increasing in practical applications.
These systems are designed so that the time characteristics of the
compensator or the sampling scheme may vary according to the
time-varying characteristics of the controlled dynamic process.
The methods of analysis of time-varying and adaptive sampled-
data systems are generally of the following types: (1) matrix and
difference equation (with time-varying coefficients) and (2) statistical
methods.

periods separating the information carrying pulses. Mathematically,
the pulse-data signal can be represented by a sampling operation that
may be adequately described by a fictitious component called the
sampling switch or sampler, as shown in Fig. 2-1a, or a pulse modu

2.2 MATHEMATICAL ANALYSIS OF THE SAMPLING
PROCESS
For the purpose of introducing the mathematical description of the
sampling operation, we shall discuss only uniform-rate sampling at
present. As described in the preceding section, the sampling process
may be represented by a fictitious pulse-amplitude modulation process
that converts a continuous signal into a train of amplitude-modulated
pulses. In the uniform-rate sampling the carrier of the pulse-modulator
is a unit-pulse train consisting of a train of pulses of unity height.
The interval between two consecutive pulses is the sampling period
T, and the pulse width p is often referred to as the sampling dura-
tion. The unit-pulse train used as the carrier of the pulse modulator
p(t)

device is a continuous function of time, the output of the device is
a train of pulses whose envelope is identical to the input signal
during the sampling durations.
We shall now investigate the frequency spectrum of the sampled
output e(t). In general, we may ask: "If the input function e(t)
contains no frequency component higher than we radians per second,
what frequency components will be contained in e(t)?" By inspec-
tion of the time response of Fig. 2-2, we shall expect to find much
higher frequency components in e(t) than in e(t) because of the
discontinuity characteristic of the pulse train. We can describe the

The frequency spectrum of the periodic pulses p(t) is simply a plot
of the Fourier coefficient Cn as a function of w. The amplitude plot
of Cn is depicted in Fig. 2-5a. We see that the amplitude spectrum
of C is not continuous, but rather it is a line spectrum at discrete
frequencies of zero and nw, rad/sec, where n is a series of integers.
The envelope of the spectrum is described by Eq. (2-16) and the
amplitude of the envelope goes to zero at frequencies of 2πk/p, where
p is the pulse width of p(t) and k = ±1, ±2, ±3, . . .. Because of
the discrete property of Cn we can now write Eq. (2-17) in the

spectrum of the sampled signal is constructed in Fig. 2-5c. It is
interesting to note that |E(jw) | consists of not only the fundamental
component of E(jw) but also an infinite number of spectra called
the complementary components. The nth complementary component
in the output spectrum is obtained by multiplying |E(jw)| by its
corresponding Fourier coefficient |C| and shifting it by nws, where
n = ±1, ±2, ±3, . . .. Therefore, we may visualize the sampling
device as a harmonic generator whose output contains the weighted
fundamental component plus all the weighted complementary com-
ponents at all frequencies separated by the sampling frequency. The

The Sampling Theorem
(2-40)
The simple physical reasoning made in the preceding section about
the minimum sampling frequency for signal recovery actually answers
a very basic yet important question in regard to the proper rate of
sampling. In general, if sampling is used in a given system, we may
ask the following question: What are the limitations on the rate of
sampling? It is easy to see that theoretically there is no upper limit
on the sampling frequency. As a matter of fact, when the sampling.
frequency approaches infinity (if this is physically possible for a
sampler), we approach the continuous case. As far as the lower
limit is concerned, intuitively, we know that, if the continuous
signal changes radically with respect to time, the signal's bandwidth
must be relatively high, and sampling the signal at too low a rate
may lose vital information on the signal between the sampling in-
stants, and consequently, we may not be able to reconstruct the

28
Chap. 2
THE SAMPLING PROCESS
continuous signal from the information contained in the sampled data.
From the amplitude spectra shown in Fig. 2-5, the lowest sampling
frequency for possible signal reconstruction is 2we, where we is the
highest frequency contained in e(t). Formally, this is known as the
sampling theorem. The theorem states that if a signal contains no
frequency higher than we radians per second, it is completely charac-
terized by the values of the signal measured at instants of time
separated by T = {(2π/wc) second. Note that in practice system
stability of closed-loop systems and other factors also dictate the
choice of the sampling frequency and may make it necessary to sample.
at a rate much higher than this theoretical minimum. Furthermore,
strictly speaking a band-limited signal does not exist physically in
communication or control systems. All physical signals do contain
components of a large frequency range. In general, because the
amplitudes of the higher frequency components are greatly diminished,
a band-limited signal is assumed. Therefore, in practice these facts,
plus the unrealizability of an ideal low pass filter, make impossible
the exact reproduction of a continuous signal from the sampled signal
even if the sampling theorem is satisfied.
An interesting note on the sampling theorem should be added
here. A signal can still be defined completely by sampling it at a rate
less than 2. radians per second provided that the derivatives of the
signal are known at the sampling instants as well as the function
amplitude sample information. To be more specific, L. J. Fogel has
proved that if a signal contains no frequency higher than we radians
per second, it is completely characterized by the values e(*) (nT),
e(k-1) (nT), . . ., e'(nT), and e(nT), (n = 0, 1, 2, . . .) of the signal
measured at instants of time separated by T = (k + 1)(2π/wc)
į
second, where e*) (nT) = d e(t)/dt = nr. This means that when the
values of the first derivative of e(t) at t = nT, e'(nT), for n = 0, 1,
2,..., are given in addition to the values of e(nT), the maximum.
allowable sampling time is increased to T = 27/wc, which is twice
the time required when e(nT) alone is measured. The addition of
each succeeding derivative allows the time between samples (sampling
time) to become larger according to T = (k + 1)(2π/we), where k
is the order of the highest derivative when all lower ordered deri-
vatives are observed for each sample.
Further discussions of the signal-recovery and the sampling
frequency are presented in the following sections.
R. M. Oliver, J. R. Pierce, C. E. Shannon, "The Philosophy of Pulse Code Modu-
lation," Proc. I. R. E., 36, No. 11, November 1948, pp. 1324-1331.
L. J. Fogel, "A Note on the Sampling Theorem," Trans. on Information Theory,
March 1955, pp. 47-48.

Sec. 2.3
THE SAMPLING PROCESS 29
2.3 MATHEMATICAL DESCRIPTION OF THE IDEAL
SAMPLING PROCESS-THE IDEAL SAMPLER
In the preceding section, mathematical analysis of an actual
sampling process is performed by the methods of Fourier transform
and complex convolution. The characteristics of the sampling opera-
tion are described in both the time domain and the frequency domain.
However, exact analysis of sampled-data systems with finite pulse-
width considerations is quite complex. In general, to simplify the
analysis and synthesis techniques, the sampling device may be replaced
by an "ideal sampler" whose output signal e* (t) contains a train of
impulses. This approximation is justified if the pulse-width of the
sampler output is very small when compared with the dominant time
constant of the continuous signal e(t), and to the sampling period.

of the output signal of the ideal sampler contains all the complemen-
tary components in addition to the primary component and that the
amplitudes of all components are equal and unattenuated.

again a harmonic generator. The sampler reproduces the spectrum of
the continuous input time function e(t) as well as the sidebands
(complementary components) at integral multiples of the sampling
frequency; and all components have the same amplitude of 1/T.

Some s-plane Properties of E* (8)
We have shown in the preceding section that the most significant

meaning of Eq. (2-68) is that it clearly indicates the effect of an
ideal sampler in terms of the frequency domain behavior of e(t) and

2.4 SUMMARY
The nature and the operation of the sampling process have been
discussed in this chapter. The sampling operation may be considered
as either a fictitious sampler or a pulse-amplitude modulation process.
The sampled signal contains all the sidebands (complementary
components) about integral multiples of the sampling frequency.
Theoretically, if the continuous signal is assumed to be band-limited,
then Shannon's sampling theorem states that the necessary condition.
for recovery of the continuous information from the sampled signal
is to have the sampler sample at least once every (27/w) second,
where we is the highest component frequency contained in the con-
tinuous signal. An extension of the sampling theorem is also given
that allows for fewer samples, but additional information is necessary
concerning the derivatives of the continuous signal at the sampling
instants.
For mathematical convenience the actual sampling process may

38
Chap. 2
THE SAMPLING PROCESS
be approximated by the operation of an ideal sampler, whose output
signal is a train of amplitude-modulated impulses. Based on this
simplification, four basic forms of the Laplace transform of the
sampled signal E*(s) are derived and the conditions of validity of
these expressions are discussed.

3.1 INTRODUCTION
Although the envelope of the sampled signal e*(t) corresponds
to the values of the continuous signal e(t) at the sampling instants,
the characteristics of the two functions in the frequency domain
have been shown to be quite different. In most feedback control
systems employing sampled-data, the high frequency sidebands in
e*(t) that resulted from the sampling operation must be removed
before the signal is applied to the continuous part of the system.
This step is absolutely necessary, since the continuous signal must
be reconstructed from the sampled-data; moreover, signals containing
high sidebands may cause excessive wear to the controlled elements.
Therefore, a component which may be considered as a data-recon-
struction device or a filtering device must be employed to remove
the sidebands from the sampled signal e* (t) before the signal is
applied to the controlled elements.

signal is first processed or modified by a digital network D*(s); the
output of D*(s), which is in the form of a pulse train, is then
smoothed out by the data-reconstruction device before the signal
is applied to the components of the controlled process.
For the purpose of studying how data reconstruction may be
accomplished, let us assume that a sampler is sampling at a sampling

desirability of a close approximation of the continuous signal.
3.2 DATA RECONSTRUCTION BY POLYNOMIAL
EXTRAPOLATION
The problem confronting us is that we have a sequence of
numbers e(nT) like that shown in Fig. 2-8c and the original
continuous signal e(t) shown in Fig. 2-8a must be reconstructed
from the information contained in this pulse sequence. Essentially,
this data-reconstruction process may be regarded as an extrapolation
process, since the continuous signal is to be constructed based on
information available only at past sampling instants. For instance,
the original function e(t) between two consecutive sampling instants.
nT and (n + 1)T is to be estimated based on the values of e(t) at
all preceding sampling instants of nT, (n - 1)T, (n-2)T, . . ., 0;
that is, e(nT), e(n − 1)T, e(n − 2)T, . . ., e(0). A well-known
method in generating this desired approximation is based on the
power series expansion of e(t) in the interval between sampling
instants nT and (n + 1)T; that is
―
| ex(t) = e(nT)| + e'(nT)(t − nT) +
where
zch
e' (nT) (t − nT)² + . . . (3-1)
2!
en(t) = e(t) for nT <t< (n + 1)T
(3-2)
[In Eq. (3-1), the primes indicate the derivatives of e(t) with respect.
to time.]
In order to evaluate the coefficients of the series given by Eq.
(3-1), the derivatives of the function e(t) must be obtained at the
sampling intants. Since the information concerning e(t) is available
only at the sampling instants, these derivatives must be estimated
from the impulse sequence. A simple expression involving only two
data pulses gives the estimate of the first derivative of e(t) at
t = nT as

From these approximated values of e'(nT) and e"(nT), we see that
the higher the order of the derivative to be approximated, the larger
will be the number of delay pulses required. In fact, we can easily
show that the number of delay pulses required to approximate the
value of e(nT) is k + 1. Thus the extrapolating device described
above consists essentially of a series of time delays, and the number
of delays depends on the degree of accuracy of the estimate of the
time function e(t). The adverse effect of time delay on the stability
of feedback control systems is well known. Therefore, an attempt
to utilize the higher order derivatives of e(t) for the purpose of
more accurate extrapolation usually meets with serious difficulties
in maintaining system stability. Furthermore, a high-order extra-
polator also requires complex circuitry and results in a high cost in
construction. For these two reasons quite frequently only the first
term of Eq. (3-1) is used in practice. A device generating only e(nT)
of Eq. (3-1) is normally known as the zero-order extrapolator, since
the polynomial used is of the zeroth order. It is also commonly
referred to as the zero-order hold device because it holds the value
of the previous sample during a given sampling period. Similarly, a
device that implements the first two terms of Eq. (3-1) is called a
first-order hold, since the polynomial generated by this device is of
the first order. Details of the zero-order hold and higher order hold
devices are discussed in the following sections.
3.3 THE ZERO-ORDER HOLD
When only the first term of the power series given by Eq. (3-1)
is used as shown in the last section, the extrapolator is called a
zero-order hold. Then, Eq. (3-1) is simply

Fig. 3-13 Gain and phase characteristics of a first-order hold.
The frequency response curves for the first-order hold are sketched
in Fig. 3-13. From the gain characteristic we see that the first-order
hold cuts off at w = ws, just as in the case of the zero-order hold
but at a sharper rate. The fact that the amplitude curve peaks up
between zero frequency and w, shows that higher ripple may be
contained in the output if the sampling frequency is low. In Fig.
3-13 the phase shift of the first-order hold is compared with that
of the zero-order hold. Note that at low frequencies, the phase lag
produced by the zero-order device exceeds that of the first-order
hold but, as the frequency becomes higher, the opposite is true.
However, in feedback control systems, the high frequency charac-
teristics of the open-loop transfer function normally control the
system stability; therefore, application of the first-order hold does.
complicate the problem of closed-loop systems stability. Consequently,
although the first-order hold in general does a better job in signal
recovery than the zero-order hold, the fact that it is quite complex
to construct and its adverse effect on system stability have made
it seldom practical.
3.5 THE FRACTIONAL-ORDER HOLD
From the discussions given in the last two sections, we see that
the amplitude characteristic of a zero-order hold drops off rapidly at
low frequencies and the amplitude characteristic of a first-order
hold exhibits an overshoot before cutting off sharply at w = ws. An
amplitude characteristic which falls between the zero-order and the

50
Chap. 3
RECONSTRUCTION OF SAMPLED SIGNALS
first-order characteristics would come close to approximating the
ideal filter response. Such a filter characteristic may be realized by
use of a so called fractional-order holdt and may be regarded as
an extension or modification of the first-order hold. The output
signal of a zero-order hold has a zero slope between two consecutive
sampling instants, whereas that of a first-order hold has a constant
slope determined by the difference in values of the two preceding
samples. For a fractional-order hold the output signal between two
consecutive sampling instants has a slope which is equal to k times

where Gho(s) and Gh(s) are the transfer functions of the zero-order
hold and the first-order hold, respectively. From the three preceding
equations, we clearly see that when k = 0, G(s) = Gho(s); the device
is identical to a zero-order hold. On the other hand, when k = 1,
Ghk(s) is identical to the transfer function of the first-order hold.
The frequency response in magnitude and phase of G(jw) is
plotted in Fig. 3-15 for five different values of k. It is observed from
this plot that a flat response is obtained when k is approximately
0.3. In general, an optimum value of k is determined based on the
frequency distribution of the input signal. A particular value of k
should be selected that will result in extrapolations having the least
error for this known range of frequencies.

Evidently, the z-transform of the time function e(t) is still the same
as the Laplace transform of e*(t), though disguised by the change
in variable from s to z. Since the z-transform of e(t) is derived from
the Laplace transform of e*(t) with a change in variable, we can
say in general that, in view of the relationship between E*(s) and
E(s) given by Eq. (2-68), any continuous function that possesses a
Laplace transform also has a z-transform.

indicating that if E(z) has a finite number of simple poles, the z-
transform of e(t) is a rational function in z. Furthermore, the degree
of the denominator of E(z) in z¹ is equal to the degree of the
denominator of E(z) in s.
One of the disadvantages of the z-transform is that it carries in-
formation about the corresponding time function only at the sampling
instants; that is, E(z) defines e*(t) only. If the sampling frequency
is high (at least twice as high as the highest signal frequency), the
continuous signal e(t), if it is band-limited, may be recovered from
e*(t) by means of an ideal filter. Then the z-transform E(2) carries
virtually all the information contained in the Laplace transform E(s).
However, if nothing is known about the characteristics of e(t), the
inverse z-transformation is usually not unique, since the discrete
time function e* (t), obtained from E(z), may represent any continu-
ous function e(t) that has the same values as e*(t) at the sampling
instants. In Fig. 4-1 two entirely different time functions with the

several methods have been developed for the evaluation of signals
between sampling instants. Among these methods the submultiple
sampling method and the modified z-transform methods are the most
common. These methods are discussed in detail in later sections of
this chapter. Regardless of the fact that the inverse z-transform is
not unique and of the other limitations of the z-transform method
to be brought out later, the modern design and analysis of sampled-
data systems still rely heavily on the z-transform technique because
of its simplicity and unique manner of application.

unique solution for e(t).

4.4 THE INVERSE Z-TRANSFORMATION
Just as in the method of Laplace transformation, time domain
response must often be obtained from the z-transform expressions.
Since the inverse z-transformation is not unique, we must remember
that the inversion from z to time domain referred to here is the
determination of e(t) or the sampled values of e(t) at the sampling
instants enT), but not e(t), from the z-transform E(z).
In general, the inverse z-transformation can be effected by one of
the following methods:
(1) The Partial Fraction Method. The z-transform E(z) is manip-
ulated into a sum of functions by partial fraction expansion, and
the z-transform table is used to find the corresponding time functions
of the expanded terms. Note that, although the z-transform tables
in this book and elsewhere have the time functions tabulated in the
form of continuous functions e(t), the inverse transform of E(z) is
not necessarily equal to e(t) (and most likely it is not). The inverse
z-transform of E(z) is simply a time function that has the same
values as the corresponding e(t) at the sampling instants of t = nT
(n = 0, 1, 2, . . .).
(2) The Power Series Method. The z-transform E(z) is expanded
into a power series in powers of 21. The coefficient of the term 2-"
corresponds to the value of the time function e(t) at the n-th sam-
pling instant.


64
Chap. 4
THE Z-TRANSFORM THEORY
(3) The Inversion Formula Method. The time function e*(t) may
be obtained from E(z) by the real inversion integral. The values of
e(t) at the sampling instants are determined by use of the following
formula:
1
e(nT)
=
2πj §. E(2) 2^~¹ dz
(4-29)
where I is a circle of radius z = ecr centered at the origin of the
z-plane, and c is of such a value that all the poles of E(z) are
enclosed by the circle.
The proofs and the illustrations of the above three methods are
given in the following:
1. The Partial Fraction Method
Recall that in the analysis of continuous signals, the partial frac-

E(x) =

z-transform of the exponential term Ae-at is Az/(z - e-ar). Therefore,
if each term of the partial fraction expansion has the form of
z/(z - e-ar), e*(t) can be expressed as the sum of exponential func-
tions. For this reason, it is more desirable to expand the function
E(z)/z and then to multiply each of the expanded terms by z.

must be investigated when a sampled signal is applied to the input

between G(z) and G(s).
Suppose that in the system shown in Fig. 4-10 a unit-step function
is applied at the input of the sampler S. at t = 0 and the sampler
is closed only at t=0 and then remains opened thereafter. Under
this condition the output of the ideal sampler consists only of an
impulse of unit area occurring at t=0. The output of the system,
c(t) in response to this unit-impulse input is simply the impulse
response g(t) of the system. Thus
c(t) = g(t) = impulse response of system G
(4-108)
If a fictitious sampler S, with the same sampling rate as that of
S (the two samplers are assumed to be synchronized) is placed at
the system output, the output of the switch S, is described by the
following equation:
-
c*(t) = g*(t) = Σ g(nT)8(t − nT)
n=0
(4-109)
where c(nT) = g(nT) is defined as the "weighting sequence" of the

illustrated in Fig. 4-11. The Laplace transform of g*(t) is now written
00

n=0
(4-110)

We can summarize our findings at this point as follows: When a
unit-impulse function is applied to a linear system with transfer func-
tion G(s), the output of the system is simply the impulse response
of the system. The output of the fictitious sampler S2 is a sequence
of impulses; the area of each impulse is equal to the value of the

impulse response g(t) at the corresponding sampling instant. This

The Laplace transform of the weighting sequence gives the pulse
transfer function G*(s).
Once the weighting sequence of a linear system is defined by

C*(s) = E*(s)G*(s)
Taking the z-transform on both sides of Eq. (4-116) yields
C(z) = E(z)G(z)
(4-116)
(4-117)
which is the z-transform relation of the sampled-data system. G(z)
is often referred to as the "z-transfer function" of the linear sampled-
data system.
Therefore, the z-transfer function G(z) relates the sampled input
E(z) to the z-transform of the output C(z) in the same way as the
ordinary transfer function G(s) does for E(s) and C(s). Take note,
however, that the actual output of the sampled-data system, c(t), is
still continuous with respect to time because of the smoothing prop-
erties of system components. However, the z-transform analysis
described by Eq. (4-117), which relies on the addition of the ficti-
tious sampler S2, gives information on c(t) only at the sampling
instants. This is certainly one of the disadvantages of the z-transform
method. In certain cases the loss of the information concerning
response between sampling instants may not be vital; e.g., if the
value of c(t) does not vary appreciably between sampling instants.
On the other hand, if c(t) contains large-amplitude oscillations between
sampling instants, the z-transform method can at best provide an
approximation to the actual response and sometimes may even provide
misleading results. Still another comment is that in view of Eq.
(4-110) the z-transfer function G(z) is derived from the impulse
response g(t) in exactly the same manner as E(2) is derived from
any signal e(t). Thus G(z) may be determined by any methods.
described in Sec. 4-2 or directly from the z-transform table.
The z-transfer function relation given by Eq. (4-117) can also be
derived from the relation between E*(s) and E(s) stated in Eq.
(2-68). The Laplace transform of the system output of the system
in Fig. 4-10 is

THE Z-TRANSFORM THEORY 77
C(s) = E*(s)G(s)
(4-118)

4.7 LIMITATIONS OF THE Z-TRANSFORM METHOD
In the preceding sections we have seen that the z-transformation
is a very convenient tool for the study of linear systems with
sampled-data. However, the z-transform method has limitations; and
in certain cases, care must be taken in the applications of the z-
transforms.
(1) In the first place we must realize that the derivation of the
z-transform method is based upon the assumption that the sampled
signal is approximated by a train of impulses whose areas are equal
to the input signal of the sampler at the sampling instants. This
assumption is considered valid only if the sampling duration of the
sampler is small when compared with the significant time constant
of the system.
(2) The z-transform of the output of a linear system, C(z),
specifies only the values of the time function c(t) at the sampling
instants; C(z) does not contain any information concerning the value
of c(t) between sampling instants. Therefore, given any C(z), the
inverse z-transform c(nT) describes c(t) only at the sampling instants
= nT.
t =

80
Chap. 4
THE Z-TRANSFORM THEORY
(3) In analyzing a sampled-data system by z-transform method,
the transfer function G(s) must have at least two more poles than
zeros (or equivalently, the impulse response of G(s) must not have
any jump behavior at t = 0). Otherwise, the system response obtained
by the z-transform method is unrealistic and sometimes even incorrect.

4.8 RESPONSE OF OPEN-LOOP SAMPLED-DATA SYSTEMS
BETWEEN SAMPLING INSTANTS
Although we have seen that the z-transform method is conven-
ient in the study of open-loop systems with sampled-data, (closed-
loop systems will be treated in the next chapter) z-transform
analysis yields no information on system response between sampling
instants. A complete description of any system response almost always
necessitates a knowledge of the waveform between sampling
instants. Several methods have been developed for the evaluation of

Equation (4-149) implies that the response between the sampling

instants, n = q + 1.

2. The Modified Z-Transform Method
Another interesting and powerful method which may be used to
evaluate the response between sampling instants of a sampled-data
system is the modified z-transform method. The method was first
introduced by Barkert of England and then later was developed by
Jury in this country. The method is essentially a modification of
the ordinary z-transform technique obtained by inserting fictitious trans-
portation lags (time delays) in the sampled-data system. By varying the
amount of transportation lag in a prescribed manner, we may recover
virtually all the information contained in the continuous signal be-
tween sampling instants.
The Delayed z-Transform In order to understand the development
R. H. Barker, "The Pulse Transfer Function and its Application to Sampling Servo
Systems," Proc. I. E. E., London, 99, Part 4, 1952, pp. 302-317.
E. I. Jury, "Synthesis and Critical Study of Sampled-Data Control Systems," Trans.
A. I. E. E., 75, Part 2, 1956, pp. 141-151; and E. I. Jury, “Additions to the Modified
z-transform Method," I. R. E. WESCON Convention Record, Part 4, August 21, 1957, pp.
136-156.

of the modified z-transform, we should first study the ordinary z-

that the z-transform of time functions delayed by integral multiples
of T may be obtained easily by means of the real translation theorem
stated in Sec. 4.5, Eq. (4-68). For instance, the z-transform of the
time function e(t - XT), where X is an integer, in simply equal to
2 times the z-transform of e(t). However, for a time function e(t)

Modified z-Transfer Function of Open-Loop Sampled-Data Systems
The delayed z-transform defined above may be applied to the evalua-
tion of responses of sampled-data systems between sampling instants.
Consider the open-loop system shown in Fig. 4-23a. The z-transform
of c(t), C(z), describes the output response at the sampling instants
only. Since the actual output of the system is continuous with respect
to time, a modified z-transfer function may be derived to provide a
relation between the sampled input and the continuous output. In

Sec. 4.8
THE Z-TRANSFORM THEORY 89
other words, in contrast to the ordinary z-transfer function G(z),
which gives the relation between the sampled input E(z) and the
sampled output C(z) as
C(x) = G(x)E(z)
(4-166)
we shall derive a modified z-transfer function G(z, m) for the system
such that
C(z, m) = G(z, m) E(z)
(4-167)
where m is a constant and C(z, m) is called the modified z-transform
of c(t). By assigning proper values for m, we may obtain virtually
all information of c(t) from C(z, m) by taking the inverse modified
z-transform. We shall show how this is done in the following.
If we introduce a fictitious delay AT in the forward path of the
system of Fig. 4-23a, we have the modified system shown in Fig.
4-23b. For reasons that will be clear later, the delay ▲ is chosen
as a fraction of the sampling period. Now consider that an impulse
of unit strength is applied as input e*(t) at t = 0 to the system of

The Inverse Modified z-Transform The major contribution of the
modified z-transform is that it gives information on the actual outputs
of linear sampled-data systems. The modified z-transform of c(t),
C(z, m), contains all the information of c(t) between sampling in-
stants. The process of the inverse modified z-transform is denoted
by 3', and may be effected by means of either the power series
expansion method or the inversion formula.
† Note that in the case of the z-transform formula given in Eq. (2-68), the time
function must not have any jump discontinuity at t=0. We have seen in Chapter
2 that if the time function has a jump at t = 0, the z-transform of the time function
c(t) evaluated by taking the contour integral around the right half of the -plane path
provides only one half of the value of c(0+). In the case of the modified z-transform,
Eq. (4-200) leads to the correct answer even if c(t) jumps at t O simply because the
time function is first shifted to the right by a period of AT, where ▲ is between 0
and 1; then the shifted function c(t - AT) is sampled by the fictitious sampler at the
regular sampling instants: 0, T, 2T, 3T, . . .. Therefore, unless ▲ = 0 or ▲ = 1, the
initial value of c(t), c(0+) is never sampled.
-

Chap. 4
THE Z-TRANSFORM THEORY

in z by long division. Just as in the z-transformation, if C(z, m)
is written as a ratio of two polynomials in z as

4.9 THEOREM of the modIFIED Z-TRANSFORMS
Several basic theorems similar to the z-transforms may simplify
and facilitate the application of the modified z-transform method to
the studies of sampled-data systems. The derivations and applications
of some of these theorems are as follows:
1. Real Translation If the modified z-transform of the time func-
tion e(t) is E(z, m), then
3m e(t±nT) = z¹¹E(z, m)
(4-220)
where n is an integer. This theorem is actually the same as the
delayed z-transform discussed in Sec. 4.8 where the delayed z-trans-
form is given by Eq. (4-161). Therefore, no additional proof is
necessary here.

100
Chap. 4
THE Z-TRANSFORM THEORY

4. Final Value Theorem
The final value theorem for the modified
z-transform states that if the time function e(t) has the modified
z-transform E(z, m) and if the function [(z - 1)/2]E(z, m) possesses
no poles on or outside of the unit circle in the z-plane, then
-
lim e(nT, m)
=

Proof: The proof of this theorem can be effected in much the

Chap. 4
THE Z-TRANSFORM THEORY
same way as that of the final value theorem for the ordinary z-
transforms. Therefore, it is left as an exercise at the end of this
chapter.
The final value theorems for the z-transforms and the modified
z-transforms normally will give identical results to the final value of
a time function c(t) if there are no ripples or hidden oscillations
between sampling instants in e(t). In general, if ripples between
sampling instants or discontinuities at the sampling instants exist,
the z-transform method is inadequate and usually will bring misleading
results. The final value theorem stated in Sec. 4.5 by Eq. (4-88)
could conceivably give the final value of e(t) only at the sampling
instants. However, if the final value theorem for the modified z-
transform is used where hidden oscillation exists, the final value of
e(t), represented by
lim e(t)
=
lim e(nT, m)
1400
n→00
(4-239)
will be a function of m; and as the value of m is varied from 0 to
1 (inclusive), the final value of e(t) during one sampling period is
determined.

BLOCK DIAGRAM, SIGNAL
FLOW GRAPH, AND MATRIX
REPRESENTATION OF
SAMPLED-DATA SYSTEMS
5.1 BLOCK DIAGRAM ANALYSIS AND TRANSFER
FUNCTIONS OF CLOSED-LOOP SAMPLED-DATA
SYSTEMS
In the preceding chapter the block diagrams of simple open-loop
systems with sampled-data have been shown, and they are analyzed
by means of the z-transform method. We have seen that when the
sampling operation is represented by a sampling switch (or an
impulse modulator), the block diagram concept, used so effectively
in the analysis of continuous-data systems, can also be adapted to
the studies and analysis of sampled-data systems.
In short, a block diagram is simply a graphic shorthand notation
representing the interaction between elements of a physical system.
Like the block diagrams that lead to the transfer functions or output
transforms of continuous signal systems, the transfer functions or
output transforms for sampled-data systems can also be evaluated
with the help of block diagram algebra. Furthermore, for sampled-
data systems the process of transfer function evaluation may be
effected in either the s- or the z-domain; the transfer function, for
instance, may be represented by either the pulse transfer function
G*(s) or the z-transfer function G(z).
104

Sec. 5.1 BLOCK DIAGRAM, SIGNAL FLOW GRAPH, AND MATRIX REPRESENTATION 105

from the case where the elements are connected directly together.

samplers) is (are) located. A single-loop sampled-data system with
error-sampling is shown in Fig. 5-2. The transfer function of the
system and the output response transform are derived as follows:

Output Transforms of More Complex System Configurations
In general, the labor involved in the derivation of the output
transforms of sampled-data systems increases with the increase in
complexity of the systems configurations. Unfortunately, the block
diagram reduction technique devised for continuous feedback control
systems cannot be efficiently applied to sampled-data systems simply
because of the mixing of sampled and continuous signals in the
latter. A simple example will illustrate this difficulty. In Fig. 5-4a
two parallel paths are shown that may be thought of as part of the
block diagram of a continuous-data system. We know that the two
paths can be combined and reduced to a single block as shown in
Fig. 5-4b. However, the two channels of the block diagram shown
in Fig. 5-5 cannot be combined into one, since one path has sampled
data and the other has a continuous signal.
As an illustration of the derivation of the output transforms of
sampled-data systems with complex configurations, let us consider
the block diagram shown in Fig. 5-6. With any experience in the

From the examples given above, we see that because the simul-
taneous equations of a sampled-data system contain both sampled
and unsampled forms of the same variable, an analytic solution of
the equations is quite difficult to obtain. In general, no definite rule
exists that will lead to the optimum procedure of taking the pulse
transform and making proper substitution of variables. Even an
experienced analyst might find himself enmeshed in a confusion of
tedious algebraic manipulation. In the following section the signal
flow graph technique of solving sampled-data systems problems is
introduced. As we shall see, when the signal flow graph of a sampled-
data system is constructed, the output transforms (or the transforms
of any system variable) as a function of the input signal can vir-
tually be written by mere inspection from the signal flow graph.

112
Chap. 5
BLOCK DIAGRAM, SIGNAL FLOW GRAPH, AND MATRIX REPRESENTATION
5.2 SIGNAL FLOW GRAPHS OF SAMPLED-DATA SYSTEMS
We have seen that system transfer functions of continuous-data
systems can be derived from either the signal flow graph or the
block diagram simply by use of Mason's gain formula. Now we shall
investigate how the signal flow graph technique can be extended to
the analysis and portrayal of systems with sampled-data. However,
any attempt to apply Mason's gain formula directly to the block
diagram or equivalent signal flow graph of a system with sampled-data
will encounter immediate difficulty. As is evident, the main difficulty
stems from the fact that sampling switches exist in sampled-data
systems; since we cannot replace the samplers by their equivalent
transfer functions, the conventional signal flow graph algebra simply
does not know how to handle a flow graph with sampling switches.
In a special case, if all the signals in a sampled-data system are
sampled, then all the transferred quantities of the systems are
starred and the general gain formula can still be applied directly.
For instance, let us consider the sampled-data system shown in Fig.
5-7. The signal flow graph of the system is constructed in Fig. 5-8.
Since only sampled signals exist throughout the system, the sampling
switches are omitted from the signal flow graph. Now applying the
gain formula to the signal flow graph with only sampled variables,
the closed-loop transfer function of the system is obtained as
R*
RA
Ε

(5-37)
We can conclude now that Mason's gain formula can be applied
only to signal flow graphs (or block diagrams) that contain nodes
either representing all continuous signals or all sampled signals but
not mixed signals.
However, the majority of the sampled-data systems contain both
sampled and continuous signals; therefore, the samplers cannot be
omitted simply. A signal flow graph method must be devised to
take care of systems with sampled as well as continuous data.
Next, we shall introduce a method of determining the output
transforms (or transfer functions) of sampled-data systems by use
of Mason's gain formula. The method involves the following steps:
(1) With the system block diagram as the starting point, construct
the "original signal flow graph" of the system. The original flow
graph is defined as merely the equivalent flow graph of the system
block diagram. For instance, Fig. 5-8 gives the original flow graph
of the block diagram shown in Fig. 5-7; the original flow graph of
the system in Fig. 5-2 is drawn in Fig. 5-9.

be applied directly. However, the following set of equations is

(5-50)
This step can generally be omitted in writing Eq. (5-49). Once
the samplers in the original flow graph are replaced by corresponding
input signals, Eq. (5-50) can be written directly from the flow
graph by applying Mason's gain formula and taking the pulse trans-
form of the variables at the same time. For instance, Eqs. (5-44)
through (5-46) can be written directly from Fig. 5-9 by applying
the gain formula and taking the pulse transform at nodes X1, X2,
and X, simultaneously.
(3) In the previous step we have demonstrated how the sampled
variables of a sampled-data system can be expressed as functions of
the inputs by use of the sampled signal flow graph and Mason's
gain formula. However, the sampled flow graph does not contain
the unsampled variables X1, X2, . . .. Since the actual output responses
of most sampled-data systems are of the continuous type, the expres-
sions of continuous responses are often desired. For this operation
we shall define the "composite signal flow graph." A composite
signal flow graph is a composition of the original flow graph and
the sampled flow graph of a sampled-data system; the sampling
switches in the original flow graph are deleted and are replaced by
their corresponding artificial input sources. If on the original flow
graph a sampler is located between node i and node j and the signal
is transmitted from i to j, the sampling operation is described by
drawing a branch of unity gain from node X* of the sampled flow
graph to node X, on the original flow graph. From the composite
signal flow graph, all the sampled and the unsampled output variables
can be determined as functions of the input signals by means of
Mason's gain formula. Actually, once the composite signal flow graph
is constructed, a separate sampled flow graph need not be constructed,

116 BLOCK DIAGRAM, SIGNAL FLOW GRAPH, AND MATRIX REPRESENTATION Chap. 5
since the former graph will contain all the information on the
unsampled variables.
Let us use the simple sampled-data system shown in Fig. 5-2 to
illustrate the construction of the composite flow graph introduced
above. The original signal flow graph of the system is shown in
Fig. 5-9, and the sampled signal flow graph is already obtained as
shown in Fig. 5-10. As is apparent, the variables in the two flow
graphs are related by Eq. (5-39); that is X₂ = X*. Therefore, the
composite flow graph is constructed by drawing a branch with unity
gain from node X of the sampled flow graph to node X2 on the
original flow graph. The composite flow graph is shown in Fig.
5-11. Now by use of Mason's formula the continuous output response
of the system is written

corresponding to the portrayal of Eq. (5-89); then, when Mason's
formula is applied to the modified flow graph, the modified pulse
transform (or modified z-transform) of X, is obtained.

5.4 TRANSMISSION MATRIX OF SAMPLED-DATA SYSTEMS+
The fact that a sampled-data system deals with sequences of
numbers rather than with continuous time functions makes the use
of matrix representation convenient for analysis and synthesis. We
have seen that the matrix method is particularly useful in the
analysis and design of sampled-data systems with time-varying
components as well as systems with nonlinear elements.
In this section the matrix representation of linear time-invariant
sampled-data systems will be discussed. In later chapters, the matrix
method will be extended to the studies of time varying and non-
linear sampled-data systems.

G(s)

Fig. 5-22 Open-loop sampled-data system.
Consider the open-loop sampled-data system shown in Fig. 5-22.
The system consists of a linear time-invariant plant preceded by a
sampler and followed by a fictitious sampler; the samplers are
assumed to have a sampling period of T sec and are synchronized.
It follows from the discussion on the weighting sequence given in
Sec. 4.6 that the continuous output of the system is obtained through
the use of the superposition principle; that is,

5.5 THE STATE-VARIABLE APPROACH
In general, system analysis and synthesis may be carried out by
two major approaches. One approach is the use of the conventional
transfer functions, block diagrams, or signal flow graphs. The other
method, which has gained popularity among modern engineers, is
the state-transition or state-variable approach. In the state-transition
approach the system is characterized by a set of first-order differential
or difference equations describing the state variables of the system.
Each component of the system is decomposed into the basic mathema-
tical elements describing it. The system is then analyzed or designed
by solving the set of first-order equations, rather than a single
higher-order equation or a set of higher-order equations.
The state-transition method has at least the following advantages
as compared to the block diagram or transfer function approach:
(1) By use of vector-matrix notation a set of first-order differential
equations may be represented by a single expression. This simplifies
the mathematical notation.
(2) Obtaining the solution of a set of first-order differential
equations is much easier than solving a high-order differential
equation.
(3) The initial conditions of the system are readily given by
↑ T. R. Bashkow, “The A Matrix, a New Network Description," I. R. E. Trans.
on Circuit Theory, Vol. CT-4, Sept. 1957, pp. 117-120; R. E. Kalman, J. E. Bertram,
"A Unified Approach to the Theory of Sampling Systems," Journal of Franklin
Institute, 267, May 1959, pp. 405-436. E. O. Gilbert, "A Method for the Symbolic
Representation and Analysis of Linear Periodic Feedback Systems," A. I. E. E. Trans.
Part 2, 79, pp. 512-523, 1960.

Sec. 5.5 BLOCK DIAGRAM, SIGNAL FLOW GRAPH, AND MATRIX REPRESENTATION
131
the state-transition equations. The calculation of the initial conditions
by conventional techniques can be tedious.
(4) The state-transition approach allows a unified study of
sampled-data systems. This includes uniform sampling, multirate
sampling, cyclic sampling, as well as random sampling and finite
pulse-width considerations.
(5) The state-transition method can be applied to certain types
of nonlinear systems and time-varying systems.

Systems
For an n-th order linear system with continuous-data, the system
dynamics may be represented by the following set of n equations:

corresponding output nodes of the integrators. The state-transition
equations of the system can then be determined from the transition
flow graph merely by applying Mason's formula and taking the inverse
Laplace transform.

Transition Equations of Sampled-Data Systems
In most sampled-data systems the signals found in the systems
are continuous as well as discrete. Thus, ordinarily a sampled-data
system containing discrete and continuous elements should be de-
scribed by sets of difference equations and differential equations. In
general, we may classify the dynamic elements in a sampled-data
system into the following three catagories:
(1) continuous dynamic elements - described by differential equa-
tions
(2) discrete dynamic elements - described by difference equations
(3) sample and hold elements-described by discrete state-transi-
tion equations.
The state variables and transition equations of the first two
categories have been discussed in the preceding sections. Namely,
we can write the set of differential equations in Eq. (5-126) for the
continuous elements and the set of difference equations of Eqs. (5-146)
and (5-147) for the discrete elements. Usually, sample-and-hold elements
are connected between discrete and continuous elements. We shall

Sec. 5.5 BLOCK DIAGRAM, SIGNAL FLOW GRAPH, AND MATRIX REPRESENTATION 137
assume that sampling and hold are one combined operation. Thus,
the output of a sampled-and-hold element between two consecutive
sampling instants is a step function. If the input and the output
of the i-th sampled-and-hold element are denoted by y(t) and h(t),
respectively, we can write.
h(kT+) = y (kt)
where kT really means kT + T for 0 <T<T.
(5-154)
In general, the input to a sampled-and-hold element is a linear
combination of the system's state variable and the system inputs.
Equation (5-154) can be written as
71
m
h.(kT*) = Σ Sı,x,(kT) + Z Viuru(kT)
j=1
where S, and Viu are constants.
u = 1
(5-155)

system can be obtained from the combined solutions of Eqs. (5-155),
(5-147), (5-146), and (5–126). In fact, the state-transition equations
can be written directly from the composition of the signal flow
graphs portraying Eqs. (5-128), (5-146), (5-147), and (5-155).

6
TIME RESPONSE OF
SAMPLED-DATA
CONTROL SYSTEMS
6.1 INTRODUCTION
Like the analysis of systems with continuous data, the study of
the transient response of sampled-data systems is extremely important.
In general, the output response of a sampled-data system can be
separated into two parts: the steady-state response and the transient
response. Since all physical systems contain inertia and friction, the
output response before the steady-state is reached does not follow
the reference input signal at all times. The deviation between the
actual output and the desired output is termed the error signal or,
generally, the actuating signal. Again, as in the study of continuous-
data systems, the transient behavior of a sampled-data system is
described by such terms as overshoot, rise time, delay time, settling
time, damping ratio, damping factor, natural undamped frequency,
etc. The definitions of some of these terms are given in Fig. 6-1.
The transient response also indicates the relative stability of the
system. The steady-state error of a system is defined as the error
between input and output signals when steady state is reached.
Although theoretically a steady-state-error-free system exists, all
physical systems do in practice have a certain amount of error in the
steady-state operation because of inherent properties of feedback and
friction.

TIME RESPONSE OF SAMPLED-DATA CONTROL SYSTEMS
