Fig. 6-1 Time response of sampled-data system to unit-step input
showing the time domain performance criteria.
A brief outline of the subjects covered in this chapter follows:
(1) Time Response of Systems with Typical Test Input Signals.
Unfortunately, the actual input excitations of feedback control
systems are not known ahead of time so that conventional analysis.
and design techniques require the assumption of some basic test
signals such as the step, ramp, parabolic, and sinusoidal functions as
system inputs. In studies of transient response the step response is
of importance because, as shown in Fig. 6-1, the performance criteria
in the time domain are all defined with respect to a step displace-
ment type of input. In the next section, we shall investigate the
transient behavior of a simple linear closed-loop system with and
without sampling. As was mentioned in Chapter 1, the main advan-
tage of sampling is to gain sensitivity and to achieve time sharing
of equipments. However, just as in any servo application, we should
expect to pay some kind of penalty for the desirable feature we
gain. Therefore, in the first part of this chapter, we shall compare
the performances of a continuous-data system and the same system
with sampling. The effect of variation of sampling frequency upon
transient response and system stability will also be investigated.
(2) Stability Analysis.
Various methods of testing system stability will be discussed. In
continuous-data systems the most useful methods have been the Routh-
Hurwitz criterion, the Nyquist criterion, and the root locus plot. We
shall see in this chapter, how these methods can be extended to the
studies of sampled-data control systems. The Nyquist criterion, how-
ever, will be covered in Chapter 7.

Sec. 6.2
TIME RESPONSE OF SAMPLED-DATA CONTROL SYSTEMS 145
(3) The z-Plane Technique.
We know that the poles and zeros of the transfer function in the
s-plane completely define the performance of a continuous-data system.
However, in the studies of sampled-data systems, concentration is
centered on the z-plane. By means of the root locus plot in the z-
plane, we shall see how the pole-zero configurations affect the time
response of sampled-data systems.
(4) Steady-State Errors.
By means of the final-value theorem for z-transforms, the steady-
state error of a sampled-data system can be evaluated for a specific
input signal. However, the z-transform method specifies the error
only at the sampling instants. For systems with no hidden oscillations
between sampling instants, z-transform analysis is quite adequate.

The output sequence c(nT) is drawn in Fig. 6-5 together with the
output response of the continuous-data system. The continuous out-
put response of the sampled-data system is estimated by connecting
the discrete output sequence by a smooth curve. Strictly speaking,
the modified z-transform should be used to determine the response
between sampling instants. However, in this simple system, the ap-
proximation by a smooth curve made here is quite good since the
sampling frequency is substantially higher than the natural frequency
of the system.
A comparison of the sampled-data system response with the re-
sponse of the continuous-data system shows that the sampling oper-
ation has caused a decrease in rise time, but the overshoot is in-
creased. The responses also show that the sampled system has faster
oscillation and a slightly shorter settling time. In general, the effect
of sampling is that of reducing the system stability. However,
sampling of the error signal does improve the stability of systems
with transportation lags.
In the above investigation we have arbitrarily chosen K to have
a value of unity. When the value of K is increased to 5, the effect
on the transient response of the sampled-data system is interesting.
If the sampling period is still one second, the output z-transform for

again shows that the effect of sampling generally is to reduce the
margin of stability. We recall a well known fact for the second-order
system with continuous-data as shown in Fig. 6-2: the system is
always stable for all positive values of K. However, with sampling
operation in the error channel, even a second-order system may be-
come unstable for a relatively low value of K.

and higher peak overshoot (over 40 percent) in the step response.
With the zero-order hold, it also takes a longer time for the transient
response to settle. The trend towards higher overshoot and oscilla-
tion in the system with hold device is expected, since the addition
of the hold circuit is equivalent to adding phase lag in the system.
and since it is well known that phase lag usually has an adverse
effect on the stability of feedback control systems.
6.3 STABILITY OF SAMPLED-DATA SYSTEMS
In the studies of linear feedback control systems with continuous
signals, we have always kept in mind that the most important per-
formance criterion of all is over-all system stability. If a system is
unstable, then to speak of its transient or steady-state response would
be insignificant, since the system is virtually useless unless it is first
stabilized by some means of compensation. Based on the information
we have obtained from the illustrative examples given in the last
section, we shall expect the problem of stability to be more acute in
control systems with sampled-data, since the sampling operation
usually undermines system stability.

150
Chap. 6
TIME RESPONSE OF SAMPLED-DATA CONTROL SYSTEMS
In words, the stability of a linear continuous-data system is de-
fined as: A system is stable if for any bounded input, the corresponding
output is also bounded. Logically, this same definition should also be
carried over to linear systems with sampled-data, especially since
most sampled-data systems do have output responses that are contin-
uous functions of time. However, the analysis and design of sampled-
data systems have relied to a great extent on the z-transform tech-
nique, which provides information on responses only at the sampling
instants in the form of c*(t) or c(nT). Therefore, the stability tests
of sampled-data systems are usually conducted with respect to the
output pulse sequence rather than with the actual output signal.
However, the stability analysis based on the z-transform method will
lead to erroneous results if the system response contains hidden
oscillations of the form shown in Fig. 6-6. In this case, the pulse
sequence occurring at the sampling instants converges, but the actual

time response is unbounded. In such a case, modified z-transform or
the submultiple-sampling method should be used.
Let us now consider the continuous and the sampled-data systems
shown in Fig. 6-7. The stability of the continuous system is com-
pletely determined by the location of the poles of G(s) in the complex
s-plane. For a stable system all the poles of G(s) must lie in the
left-half s-plane; if at least one of the poles of G(s) is found in the
right-half of the s-plane, the system is unstable. This can readily be
seen from the inverse Laplace transform of G(s); a pole of G(s)
with positive real part corresponds to a rising exponential function
in the time domain. If we assume that for the sampled-data system
in Fig. 6-7b there are no hidden oscillations in the system and thus
that z-transform method is applicable, the system is said to be stable
if the output sequence c(nT) is bounded for a bounded input. There-
fore, for a bounded c(nT), the corresponding actual response c(t)
(whatever form c(t) may take) must also be bounded, and its
Laplace transform C(s) must have poles only in the left-half s-plane.
As was shown in Sec. 4-5, the z-transformation maps the left-half
of the s-plane into the interior of the unit circle in the z-plane.
Therefore, the z-transform of the response c(t) must have all its
poles located inside the unit circle in the z-plane. Since C(z) is a
product of R(z) and G(z), we can now say that the stability of the
sampled-data system depends entirely on the pole location of the z-
transfer function G(z) because the input is assumed to be bounded.
For a stable system, the poles of G(z) must not be found in the
region outside the unit circle in the z-plane. The regions of stability
in the s-plane and the z-plane are depicted in Fig. 6-8. In the s-plane
the regions of stability and instability are separated by the imaginary
axis, whereas in the z-plane they are separated by the unit circle
z = 1. Poles of G(z) of the system in Fig. 6-7b that are on the
unit circle in the z-plane will give rise to sustained oscillation in
the output response.
Let us now consider a closed-loop sampled-data system of the form
shown in Fig. 6-9. Based on the discussions above, the closed-loop
system will be stable if the poles of the closed-loop z-transfer func-
tion C(z)/R(z) are all located inside the unit circle in the z-plane.
In other words, the roots of the characteristic equation.
1 + HG(z) = 0
(6-20)

Eq. (6-20), then the closed-loop sampled-data system is stable if and
only if all the zi's satisfy

At this time, we shall investigate the possible ways of testing the
stability of a sampled-data control system. We recall that for contin-
uous-data feedback control systems, system stability may be investi-
gated by use of one of the following five well-known methods:
(1) The Routh-Hurwitz Criterion: Determines whether any roots
of the characteristic equation lie in the right-half of the s-plane.
(2) The Nyquist Criterion: Investigates whether or not the open
loop transfer function plot encircles the (-1, j0) point in the G-
plane and hence determines the difference between the number of
poles and zeros of the closed-loop transfer function that are in the
right-half s-plane.
(3) The Bode plot: This may be used to determine whether or
not any real frequency exists at which the open-loop gain is greater
than 0 db when the phase shift is greater than 180 deg.
(4) Decibels of G as a Function of Phase Shift of G: This is a
plot of the magnitude of G in db versus the phase shift of G. From
this plot the absolute stability as well as the relative stability (meas-
ured by phase and gain margins) of the closed-loop system may be

Sec. 6.4
TIME RESPONSE OF SAMPLED-DATA CONTROL SYSTEMS 153
investigated. By superimposing this plot upon the Nichols chart, the
frequency response of the closed-loop system may be obtained.
(5) The Root Locus Plot: The root locus diagram indicates graph-
ically the location of the roots of the characteristic equation when
the open-loop gain K of the system is varied.
We shall see that the stability analysis of sampled-data systems
does not depend on the derivation of a wealth of new techniques at
all. As a matter of fact, with some modifications each of the tech-
niques mentioned above can be used to study the stability of sampled-
data control systems. The only new techniques that are introduced
solely for the purpose of studying stability of sampled-data systems
are the Schur-Cohn criterion, and Jury's criteriont which are counter-
parts of the Routh-Hurwitz criterion for continuous-data systems.
The Routh-Hurwitz test determines the root location of a polynomial
with constant coefficients (in s) with respect to the imaginary axis
in the s-plane, whereas the Schur-Cohn test and Jury's test provide
information on the whereabouts of the roots of a polynomial in z
with respect to the unit-circle in the z-plane.
In the following sections of this chapter, the Schur-Cohn criterion,
the modified Routh-Hurwitz criterion, Jury's criterion, and the root
locus plots will be discussed.
6.4 THE SCHUR-COHN STABILITY CRITERION
As has been shown in the preceding section, a sampled-data
system is stable if all the roots of the system characteristic equation
lie inside the unit circle in the z-plane. A test for the locations of
the roots of a polynomial in z with respect to the unit circle can.
be made by use of the Schur-Cohn criterion. In general, if the
characteristic equations of linear sampled-data systems are described
by
F(x) = anz" + An-12"-¹ + ... + a₂ z² + a₁ z + α。 0 (6-22)
where ao, a1, a2,

Schur-Cohn criterion may be stated as follows:
A sampled-data system is stable (all the characteristic equation
roots lie inside the unit circle) if and only if the sequence of "Schur-
Cohn determinants"-1, A1, A2, A3, . . Ak, . . . An-has n variations

From these two examples, we see that the application of the
Schur-Cohn stability criterion is not nearly as convenient as the
Routh-Hurwitz test ordinarily used for continuous-data systems. First
of all, for higher order systems the use of the Schur-Cohn test relies
on the evaluation of higher order determinants. There does not seem
to be any simple way of tabulating the equation coefficients so that
stability condition can be investigated without manipulating with the
determinants. Certainly, the attractiveness of the Routh-Hurwitz
criterion is due in part to the fact that the equation coefficients of
the continuous-data system can be tabulated into the form of the
Routh tabulation and then stability condition is obtained simply by
manipulation of the coefficients of the tabulation. Furthermore, the

156 TIME RESPONSE OF SAMPLED-DATA CONTROL SYSTEMS
Chap. 6
Routh's test will give the number of roots that are in the right-half
of the s-plane and also will tell whether any root exists on the
imaginary axis, whereas the Schur-Cohn test tells only whether all
the roots are inside the unit circle in the z-plane.

6.6 EXTENSION OF ROUTH-HURWITZ CRITERION
TO SAMPLED-DATA SYSTEMS
Any attempt to apply the conventional Routh-Hurwitz test to the
study of stability of sampled-data systems meets with immediate
difficulty. If we try to apply the test directly to the characteristic

158
Chap. 6
TIME RESPONSE OF SAMPLED-DATA CONTROL SYSTEMS
equation of a sampled-data system in the s-domain, we see that the
equation is of the form 1 + HG*(s) = 0 [or 1 + G*(s)H*(s), as the
case may be] which is normally a transcendental function of s;
therefore, the Routh's test breaks down completely. Trying to apply
the Routh's test to the characteristic equation in the z-domain also
fails, since the stability boundary in the z-plane is no longer the
imaginary axis but rather is the unit circle z 1. However, if we
map the interior of the unit circle in the z-plane onto the left-half
plane of another complex variable plane by some kind of transfor-

We shall see that Eq. (6-34) maps the interior of the unit circle in
the z-plane onto the left-half of the w-plane. For the purpose of
identification of this process, we shall call Eq. (6-34) the "w-trans-
formation." However, if in Eq. (6-33) a = b = 1 but c = 1 and d =

(6-35)
which is called the "r-transformation." The mappings of the primary
strip in the s-plane into the unit circle of the z-plane and then from
z to the r and w planes by the r and w transformations are shown
in Fig. 6-10. If all the periodic strips in the s-plane are considered,
the z-transformation maps the entire left-half of the s-plane into the
interior of the unit circle in the z-plane; the r or the w transfor-
mation then maps the inside of the z-plane unit circle onto the en-
tire left-half of the r or the w plane. This is illustrated in Fig. 6-11.
As far as the application of the Routh-Hurwitz criterion is con-
cerned, either the w- or the r-transformation may be used; we can
easily see that no real advantage is gained by choosing one trans-
formation over the other. Both transformations will convert the orig-
inal characteristic equation in z into a quotient of two polynomials
in either r or w of the same order. In order to be consistent, the
r-transformation is used here exclusively as the medium for the ap-
plication of the Routh-Hurwitz criterion to sampled-data systems.
As will be shown later in the discussion on frequency domain design
of sampled-data systems, for certain types of systems the use of the
r-transformation may lead to simpler solutions, whereas for other
types of systems the design may be more straightforward if the w
transformation is used.
The determination of stability of sampled-data systems by use of
the r-transformation is illustrated by the following examples:

As a closing remark to this section, we can say that the stability
study of sampled-data systems by use of the bilinear r-transformation
and the Routh-Hurwitz criterion is quite straightforward in prin-
ciple. In transforming from the z to the r domain, the degree of the
polynomial is not increased (it may actually be reduced if the orig-
inal polynomial has roots at z = 1). However, in the case of high-
order systems, the work involved in simplifying the polynomial in
r before applying the Routh's test is quite tedious.

6.7 EFFECT OF POLE-ZERO CONFIGURATION OF
C* (8) IN THE S-PLANE AND C(z) IN THE Z-PLANE
UPON SYSTEM TRANSIENT BEHAVIOR
We know that the Routh-Hurwitz criterion tests only the absolute
stability of a system. However, if a system is stable, the criterion
does not indicate how stable the system is. Additional information
about the time response of a stable sampled-data system at the
sampling instants must be obtained from the location of the poles
of C*(s) in the left-half of the s-plane or the poles of C(z) inside
the unit circle in the z-plane. A knowledge of the pole-zero locations
in the s-plane or the z-plane enables the designer to predict the
transient behavior of sampled-data systems.
From the studies of continuous-data systems, the correlation be-
tween the locations of the poles and zeros of C(s) in the s-plane and
the transient response of c(t) is well known. For instance, complex
conjugate poles of C(s) in the left-half s-plane give rise to sinusoidally
damped responses; poles on the negative real axis correspond to
monotonically decaying responses; and complex conjugate poles on
the imaginary axis will give rise to undamped constant-amplitude
sinusoidal oscillations. All poles in the right-half s-plane correspond
to unstable responses. Since the z-transformation is merely a map-
ping process, and the mapping of the s-plane into the z-plane has
been demonstrated in Sec. 4-3, the correlation between the pole-zero
location of C(z) in the z-plane and the transient behavior of the
sampled-data system at the sampling instants can be established
simply by investigation of the correspondence of the s-plane and the
z-plane pole-zero configurations. In regard to the pole locations of
C*(s) in the s-plane, we shall consider the following cases:
1. Poles on the Real Axis in the s-Plane
The periodic property of C*(s) has been established previously
in Chapter 2; that is, the poles of C*(s) that are located in the
primary strip of the s-plane are also duplicated in the complementary
strips. Therefore, in this case, poles of C*(s) on the real axis must
also be accompanied by complementary poles located on the hori-
zontal axes of s ojnws, where n = 1, 2, 3, . . . . The negative
real axis, together with its complementary axes of sσ ±jnws
(n = 1, 2, 3, . . .) in the s-plane, when mapped by the z-transforma-
tion correspond to the positive real axis inside the unit circle in the
z-plane. Similarly, the positive real axis and its complementary axes

z-plane. These can be shown by substituting so±jnw, into

conclude that a uniform variation in system transient response comes
from a positive real pole of C(z) in the z-plane. Furthermore, if the
pole is inside the unit circle, the transient dies out monotonically or
the output sequence C(nT) decays uniformly; and if the pole is out-
side the unit circle, the transient increases in magnitude monotonically
and the output sequence is divergent.

the sampling frequency w, is at a minimum specified by Shannon's
sampling theorem. Apparently, if the sampling is decreased from the
present value so that w₁>w./2, the sampled-data will not be able to
describe the original signal completely, and the z-transform method
becomes unreliable.

6.8 EFFECT OF THE POLE-ZERO CONFIGURATIONS
IN THE Z-PLANE UPON THE MAXIMUM
OVERSHOOT AND PEAK TIME OF TRANSIENT
RESPONSE OF SAMPLED-DATA SYSTEMS
In the preceding section we have obtained the relationship between
the characteristic equation roots in the z-plane and the transient
response of a second-order sampled-data system. When the character-
istic equation has complex roots inside the unit circle in the z-plane,
the corresponding output response of the system will be oscillatory
with positive damping. In general, the complex roots that are inside
and are closest to the unit circle give rise to more oscillatory re-
sponses (less damped). It remains for us to determine the relation-
ships between the pole-zero configuration of the closed-loop transfer
function and the maximum overshoot and peak time (time at which
maximum overshoot occurs) of a sampled-data system.
For a simple second-order system with continuous-data, if the
closed-loop transfer function is given by

Since, as illustrated in Fig. 6-18, c(nT) describes the values of the
continuous response c(t) only at the sampling instants, the problem
of evaluating Cmax(t) from the information contained in c(nT) is a
difficult one. The likelihood that c max(t) = Max c(nT) is very small,
implying that the maximum overshoot happens to occur exactly at
a sampling instant. The determination of the maximum overshoot
and the peak time by use of c(nT) has been discussed in the litera-
ture. However, in these earlier methods, we must assume that only
two dominant poles of C/R(z) contribute significantly to the over-
shoot and peak time; all other closed-loop poles and zeros are con-
sidered to be very near to the origin of the z-plane. The method
used here is similar to the work of D. P. Lindorff; the maximum
overshoot and the peak time are computed by considering not only
the dominant quadratic poles but also the influence of a closed-loop
zero which may be located at any point on the real axis in the z-
plane.

6.9 ROOT LOCUS METHOD FOR SAMPLED-DATA SYSTEMS
The root locus technique has been quite useful and popular in
the analysis and design of linear control systems with continuous-
data. The root locus diagram of a continuous-data system is essen-
tially a plot of the roots of the characteristic equation as a function
of the loop gain K (K varies from 0 to ∞). In the preceding sec-
tion, we have shown that the stability and transient behavior of
sampled-data systems depend entirely upon the poles (and zeros, for
transient response) of a closed-loop pulse-transfer function C*(s)/R*(s)
or the closed-loop z-transfer function C(z)/R(z). Therefore, if the
conventional root locus technique can be conveniently adapted to
sampled-data system, the transient behavior and other important
properties of these systems may conceivably be obtained with ease
from a plot of the poles of C*(s)/R*(s) or the poles of C(z)/R(z)
with the system loop gain K as a variable parameter.
Let us first consider the possibility of constructing the root locus
plot of a sampled-data system in the s-plane. For the simple closed-
loop system shown in Fig. 6-23, the closed-loop pulse-transfer func-
tion is given by
C*(s)

the root loci of the characteristic equation take the form of Fig.
6-24b when the forward gain K is varied from 0 to ∞. Clearly, for
more complex pole-zero configurations for HG*(s), the construction
of the root loci in the s-plane will be more difficult because of the
periodic property of HG*(s). However, the closed-loop z-transfer
function C(z)/R(z) is a rational function of z, and the characteristic
equation of the system in Fig. 6-23 in the z-domain is an algebraic
equation in z,
1 + HG(z) = 0
(6-79)

straightforward since rules of construction of the conventional root
locus diagrams are directly applicable to the z-plane loci. In essence,
the construction of the z-plane root loci is to find the points that
satisfy the following conditions:

and the loci are depicted in Fig. 6-26. The critical value of K for system

misleading feature of the z-plane root loci, since one would think that normally
the stability of systems is measured by the closeness of the root loci with the
unit circle. The fact is that the loci in Fig. 6-25 and Fig. 6-26 seem to be
of the same form, but the values of K at various points on the two plots are
not in correspondence.

From these two examples, we see that the construction of the
root locus diagram for a sampled-data system in the z-plane is similar
to that of the continuous case, except that the interpretation on
system stability is different since the stable region is now inside the
unit circle.

Sec. 6.10
TIME RESPONSE OF SAMPLED-DATA CONTROL SYSTEMS 189
When investigating the transient behavior from the z-plane root
loci, the constant damping, the constant damping ratio, and the con-
stant frequency loci in the z-plane must be referred to. The mapping
of these loci from the s-plane to the z-plane has been given in Sec.
4.3; the application of these loci with the root locus plots will be
discussed in the next section.
By use of the method illustrated in Examples 6-8 and 6-9, the
root loci of any sampled-data system with rational HG(z) may be
constructed. Just as in continuous-data systems, the Spirulet can also
be used as an aid in the cut-and-try method of plotting the root
loci in the z-plane. The root loci of some simple z-transfer functions.
are given in Table 6-34.
6.10 TRANSIENT RESPONSE AND RELATIVE STABILITY
FROM ROOT LOCUS PLOTS
Once the root locus diagram of a given sampled-data system is
constructed in the z-plane, the absolute stability as well as the rela-
tive stability of the system can be observed from the root loci. Ab-
solute stability requires that all roots of the characteristic equation
lie inside the unit circle in the z-plane. Relative stability, on the
other hand, is also studied in terms of the locations of the roots of
the characteristic equation. The questions that concern us are: If the
system is stable, how stable and how good is it? Does its transient
behavior satisfy the designer's specifications? Specifically, we want
to obtain information of some performance criteria such as the set-
tling time, maximum overshoot, and damping ratio of the system.
In general, if the realization of certain minimum settling time and
specific damping ratio is of interest, the behavior of the root loci
with respect to the constant damping factor and the constant damp-
ing ratio loci in the z-plane should be investigated. These loci and
the constant frequency loci were given in Sec. 4-3. The constant
damping loci are a family of concentric circles centered at the origin
of the z-plane; the radius of the circle corresponding to a given
damping factor σ ise, where T is the sampling period in seconds.
The constant frequency Toci are straight lines emanating from the
origin of the z-plane at angles of = T radians measured from the
positive real axis. These loci and their s-plane counterparts are shown.
† Available from the Spirule Company, 9728 El Venado, Whittier, Calif.
M. Mori, "Root-Locus Method of Pulse Transfer Function for Sampled-Data
Control Systems," I. R. E. Trans. on Automatic Control, PGAC-3, November 1957, pp.
13-20.

190 TIME RESPONSE OF SAMPLED-DATA CONTROL SYSTEMS
Chap. 6
in Fig. 4-4 and 4-5. If the design of a certain sampled-data system
requires a certain minimum settling time, then all the characteristic
equation roots of the system must lie to the left of the constant
damping locus s = σ in the \s-plane, and correspondingly in the z-
plane all roots must lie inside the circle with radius (Fig. 6-
29). For a constant damping ratio, the constant loci in the z-

to the left of the constant damping ratio line in the s-plane (if this
damping ratio is 50 percent, the roots must not be found in the

The relative stability of a sampled-data system can now be in-
vestigated by superimposing the constant, σ, and a loci on the
system root loci plot. Let us consider the system shown in Fig. 6-4.
The root locus plot of the system is again constructed in Fig. 6-33
for T = 1 sec. Inspection of the root locus plot shows that the root
locus intersects the = 0.5 locus at the point that corresponds to
K = 0.49. Therefore, if the forward gain of the system is 0.49, the
damping ratio of the second-order system is equal to 50 percent.
This answer is significant, since in this particular system the forward
transfer function between the sampler and the output provides suffi-
cient attenuation at frequencies higher than w/2 = π, so that only
the constant locus in the primary strip is necessary. By use of the
constant damping and the constant frequency loci, the damping factor
and of the system when K = 0.49 are found to be 0.332 sec¹ and
0.558 rad/sec, respectively.
6.11 SYSTEM RESPONSE BETWEEN SAMPLING INSTANTS
Thus far, the z-transform method has been used to evaluate the
output responses of sampled-data systems at sampling instants only.
In Sec. 6.2, the output sequence of a simple second-order system with
sampled-data is obtained by the z-transform method, and the output
responses are sketched in Fig. 6-5 for the system operating with and
without a zero-order hold. However, the z-transform analysis does
not yield any information on the response between sampling instants.
Therefore, in this particular system the continuous output response
is estimated by connecting the output samples by a smooth curve.
In general, in a well-behaved system if the sampling frequency is
high as compared with the natural frequency of the system, this
estimation of the continuous response from the sampled data is rea-
sonably accurate. But for a certain class of systems, oscillation may
exist in the response between sampling instants; then a complete
description of the actual system response would certainly require the
knowledge of the response waveform between sampling instants as
well. Also, in evaluating the maximum overshoot of a time response,
the maximum value given by the sampled sequence is usually less
than the actual value, unless the maximum overshoot happens to fall
right on a sampling instant. In order to calculate the maximum

Sec. 6.11
TIME RESPONSE OF SAMPLED-DATA CONTROL SYSTEMS 195
overshoot more accurately, we again have to recover the signal be-
tween sampling instants.
In Chapter 4 the submultiple sampling method and the modified
z-transform method are used to evaluate the response between samp-
ling instants for open-loop sampled-data systems. These methods will
now be extended to the studies of closed-loop systems with sampled-
data.
1. The Submultiple Sampling Method
The application of the submultiple sampling method to the eval-
uation of response between sampling instants of a closed-loop system
with sampled-data is illustrated in Fig. 6-34. In this single-loop system
fictitious samplers Sno and Sn with sampling period T/n where n =
1, 2, 3, are inserted between S. (the regular sampler with
sampling period T) and G(s) and at the output terminal, respec-
tively. The fictitious samplers are not physical components; they are
used simply to facilitate the calculation of system response during
the sampling periods. Since the input to the fictitious sampler Sno is
a train of impulses of period T, and Sno is sampling at a rate n
times as fast as So, the insertion of the switch Sno does not in any.
way affect the original system behavior. Therefore, the system shown
in Fig. 6-34 behaves exactly as if the two fictitious samplers were
absent.

Thus far in this section we have demonstrated how the response
between sampling instants of a closed-loop sampled-data system can
be evaluated by means of the submultiple sampling and the modified
z-transform methods. The particular system chosen in the illustrative
examples, however, is of such a nature that the z-transform method
is quite valid in the calculation of the system response. But in general
if the system response between sampling instants is not well behaved,
it may be necessary to apply the two methods discussed above so
that the behavior of the system response between sampling instants
may be computed. In order to demonstrate the real usefulness of the
modified z-transform and some oscillatory properties of system re-
sponse between sampling instants, the following example is given.

The values of c(nT, m) are calculated and tabulated in Table 6-4 for n =
1, 2, 3, 4; and various values of m. The complete output response of the
system is depicted in Fig. 6-40. Clearly the actual response between sampling
instants is altogether different from the smooth curve predicted from the z-
transform approximation. This difference verifies our original prediction that
the z-transform method will give misleading results in this particular system.
The oscillation between sampling instants, as observed in this system, not
indicated by the z-transform solution is often referred to as the "hidden
oscillation." In practice, the hidden oscillation between sampling instants may
be positively damped (as in the example just given), undamped, or negatively
damped (unstable, or hidden instability). A significant point in the system of
this example is that the poles of C(z) and the poles of C(z, m) are identical.
This indicates that the stability of the system response at the sampling instants
and in between the sampling instants is the same. However, in one rare
class of systems the modified z-transform of the system output may have more
poles than C(z). In this case, if all the poles of C(z) be located inside the
unit circle of the z-plane (indicating that the response at the sampling instants
is a stable one) but one or more poles of C(z, m) be located outside the unit
circle, the response between the sampling instants would actually be unstable.
This type of instability is sometimes referred to as the "hidden instability,"
since, like the hidden oscillation phenomenon, it is not indicated by the or-
dinary z-transform method.

6.12 STEADY-STATE RESPONSE STEADY-STATE ERROR
ANALYSIS
In the preceding sections, only the transient response of sampled-
data systems is discussed. However, just as in systems with contin-
uous signals, the steady-state response is also of great importance
in the analysis and design of sampled-data systems.
The steady-state error of a linear feedback control system with
unity-feedback (with or without sampled-data) is defined as the dif-
ference between the reference input r(t) and the actual output c(t)
when steady state is reached; that is

steady-state error at the sampling instants is described by

In the studies of linear feedback control systems with continuous-

depends on the type of reference input applied to the system and
also, to a great extent, on the nature and characteristics of the system
itself. We shall show that these properties are carried over to feed-
back control systems with sampled-data. Therefore, the concept of
the error constants and the error coefficients can be conveniently
extended from the continuous-data systems to the sampled-data systems.
The error constants are a direct measure of the steady-state error
of a system when typical test signals such as a step, ramp, or acceler-
ation function is applied. The definitions and the derivations of the
error constants are given as follows:

when a system has a zero steady-state error at the sampling instants,

description of the steady-state error may be made by use of the
modified z-transform. In Fig. (6-161) we see that the actual steady-
state error of a sampled-data system may be written as

•
FREQUENCY RESPONSE OF
SAMPLED-DATA SYSTEMS
7.1 INTRODUCTION
In the preceding chapter time response of sampled-data systems
has been studied through the use of the z-transform or the modified
z-transform technique. The stability of closed-loop sampled-data
systems is investigated by application of the Schur-Cohn criterion or
the modified version of the Routh-Hurwitz criterion (the r-transforma-
tion) or Jury's criterion to the z-transform characteristic equations.
However, among the many techniques available for the analysis
and design of feedback control systems, the frequency response method
has proved quite popular. The basic features of the frequency response
method are that the description of the system is given in terms of
its response to sinusoidally varying input signals and that the sinus-
oidal steady-state behavior of the system is obtained by replacing
the Laplace operator s by jo in the system transfer function. As is
well known, the output response for linear systems with continuous-
data contains only the same frequency component as the sinusoidal
input signal, and only in systems with nonlinear elements may
harmonics or subharmonics of the input signal appear in the system
output. However, for linear systems with sampled-data, the sampler
acts as a harmonic generator so that the system output in general
may contain higher harmonics of the input. The main difficulty in
studying sampled-data systems by the frequency response method,
215

216 FREQUENCY RESPONSE
Chap. 7
therefore, stems from the fact that these high frequency components
in the system usually make the construction of the frequency loci plots
more difficult.
The study of sampled-data systems in the frequency domain
essentially relies on the extension of all the existing techniques
devised for the analysis of continuous-data systems. Some of the
well-known methods are:
1. The Nyquist Diagram
From the Nyquist diagram of the open-loop system transfer
function, the stability of the closed-loop system can be investigated.
The behavior of the Nyquist plot with respect to the (-1, 0) point
in the open-loop transfer function plane gives information on the
absolute stability as well as the relative stability of the system.
Frequency response characteristics of the system can also be obtained
directly from the Nyquist plot.
2. The Bode Diagram (Gain and Phase versus Log of Frequency)
The Bode diagram is a plot of the amplitude and phase angle
of the open-loop transfer function as a function of frequency (or
log of frequency) and may be used to investigate the absolute and
relative stabilities of the closed-loop system.
3. The Gain-Phase Plot
The gain-phase plot of the open-loop transfer function is used
to determine system stability by investigating the amplitude in db
versus the phase angle of the open-loop transfer function.
In the following sections, we shall see how the above mentioned
conventional methods may all be extended to aid the analysis and
design of sampled-data control systems.
7.2 THE NYQUIST PLOT
The Nyquist criterion is essentially a graphical study of the
stability of a closed-loop system. We have established in the preceding
chapter that the stability of linear control systems with continuous-
or sampled-data is determined by the location of the roots of the
characteristic equations. For a continuous-data system, all the roots
of the characteristic equation must lie in the left-half of the s-plane,

Sec. 7.2
217
FREQUENCY RESPONSE
whereas for a sampled-data system, the roots of the characteristic
equation must be found inside the unit circle /z/= 1 in the z-plane
for system stability. Let us consider the single-loop feedback systems
shown in Fig. 7-1 (a) and (b). For the continuous-data system,
the closed-loop transfer function is

the continuous-data system may be studied by constructing the

Logically, the same procedure may be extended to sampled-data
systems by constructing the Nyquist plot for HG*(jw) or HG(z).
Because of the difference in the criterion on pole-zero locations.
in the s-plane and the z-plane, the Nyquist criterion must be restated.
for sampled-data systems.*
The Nyquist path originally defined in the s-plane for continuous-
data systems is shown in Fig. 7-2a. The path is described by four
separate sections and virtually encloses the entire right-half of the
S-plane with the exception of the origin and points on the imaginary
axis where the roots of the characteristic equation may locate. The
corresponding Nyquist path in the z-plane mapped by the z-trans-
formation is shown in Fig. 7-2b. The small semicircles at z = + 1

and other points on the path correspond to the small indentations
on the s-plane Nyquist path. The large circle of infinite radius in
the z-plane corresponds to the large semicircle (Sec. IV) in the
s-plane. The Nyquist path in the z-plane is not shown as a continuous
closed path because, corresponding to point (5) on the path in the
s-plane, the z-plane path jumps abruptly from the unit circle to the
infinite-radius circle and at point (7) the z-plane path jumps abruptly
back from the infinite circle to the unit circle. The exact place
where the jump occurs is indeterminate. However, the area that is
enclosed by the z-plane Nyquist path is the region between the two
circles. Later we shall see that though this Nyquist path appears
to be discontinuous, no difficulty results in the interpretation of the
stability to the Nyquist diagram.
The procedure and statement of the Nyquist criterion for sampled-
data systems are given as follows:
(1) The Nyquist path in the z-plane is defined as shown in Fig.
7-2b.
(2) The Nyquist plot of HG*(s) or HG(z) is constructed. This
is a plot of HG*(s) or HG(z) when z takes on the values along
the Nyquist path in the z-plane (or s takes on all values along
the path in the s-plane).
(3) The Nyquist criterion states that for a stable closed-loop
sampled-data system, the Nyquist plot for HG (2) must encircle the

Sec. 7.2

of poles of HG(z) that are outside the Nyquist path in the z-plane,
and the encirclement (if there is any), must be made in the clock-
wise direction.

As the above discussion indicates, the application of the Nyquist
criterion to sampled-data systems is essentially an investigation of
the behavior of the Nyquist plot of HG(z) with respect to the
(-1, 0) point. Therefore, the major work in the application of the
Nyquist criterion is to construct the Nyquist diagram for HG(z);
once this is done, the stability condition of the closed-loop system
is determined by mere inspection of the diagram. However, the
plotting of the Nyquist diagram for sampled-data systems is compli-
cated by the fact that the pulse-transfer function HG*(s) is an
infinite summation of functions, due to the sampling operation. The
transfer locus can no longer be sketched for a sampled-data system by
"Enclosed" means encircled in the counterclockwise direction. The area that is
enclosed by a closed-path is always to the left of the path when one travels along the
path in the prescribed direction.
In this case, if HG(z) encircles the (-1,0) point at all, the direction must be
counterclockwise; and since N = Z = positive number, the point will be enclosed.
§ Transfer locus usually is considered only as a portion of the complete Nyquist
diagram. In the s-plane, the transfer locus of G(s)H(s) corresponds only to sections
I and III on the Nyquist path in the s-plane.

mere inspection of the transfer function, at least not as simply as
in the continuous-data case, where the transfer locus may usually be
sketched by inspection of the form of the transfer function G(s)H(s)
of the system.

1. Two-Term Approximation Method

The plot of HG*(jw) using the two-term approximation method

locus for G(jw)H(jw) that corresponds to frequency range from
- ∞ to +∞ is single-valued, whereas the locus for HG*(jw) repeats
itself for every sampling frequency; that is, the plot of HG*(jw)
for 0 <ww, is identical to that for frequencies between w, and
2 ws, 2, and 3 ws, . . ., etc. This contrast is expected, since we have
established the fact that HG*(jw) is a periodic function with period
equal to jos.

Normally, if the open-loop system is stable, only the unit circle
portion of the Nyquist path in the z-plane need be considered because
under this condition the open-loop transfer function HG(z) does
not have any pole outside the unit circle and P = 0. Usually the
zeros of HG(z) are determined by inspection of the transfer function;
thus the behavior of HG(z) with respect to the origin of the HG(z)
plane need not be investigated. Since the poles of HG(z) are
identical to the poles of 1 + HG(z), N must equal zero for the
closed-loop system to be stable. Therefore, if P = 0, the Nyquist
criterion may be stated as:
If a sampled-data system is open-loop stable, the closed-loop system
is also stable if the Nyquist plot of HG(z) that corresponds to
Sec. I, II, and III (the unit circle) on the Nyquist path in the z-
plane does not enclose the (−1,j0) point.

Apparently, the corner frequencies of the plot are at w, = 1, 1, and
1.525. The asymptotes of the magnitude curves for (jo, - 1) and
(jw,+1) are identical, each with a slope of +20 db/decade of
frequency. Thus, the magnitude curve of (jw, + 1)(jw, − 1) at high
frequencies has an asymptote of slope equal to +40 db/decade of
frequency. The intersection of this asymptote with the w, axis is
at w, 1. The only difference between the Bode plots of these two
simple terms is in the phase shift curves. The phase shift of (jw, + 1)
varies from 0 to +90 degrees, whereas the phase shift of (jw, − 1)
varies from + 180 to 90 degrees as w, is varied from 0 to infinity.
Therefore, the phase shift curve of (jw, + 1)(jo, -1) is a horizontal
line at 180 degrees for all values of wr. The complete Bode plot
for HG*(jor) of Eq. (7-43) is now constructed in Fig. 7-9. For
this asymptotic plot, the gain margin of the system is measured to
be 5.77 db, and the phase margin of the system is approximately 40
degrees. The actual value of the phase margin should be some-
what less than 40 degrees since the actual point where the magnitude
curve crosses the 0 db axis would be at a lower frequency than the
crossover point made by the asymptotes used in the figure. As a
matter of fact, the exact phase margin found from the Nyquist plot
in Fig. 7-3 or Fig. 7-7 is approximately 35 degrees.
In a similar manner, the Bode diagram of the open-loop transfer
function of the system with zero-order hold is constructed in Fig.
7-10. The gain margin is about 2.5 db and the phase margin is 15
degrees.
From the discussions given above, we see that with the use of

7.4 THE GAIN-PHASE PLOT
The gain-phase diagram for an open-loop transfer function G(z)
is a plot of the magnitude of G(z) as a function of the phase shift

Sec. 7.4
FREQUENCY RESPONSE 233
of G(z), with frequency as a varying parameter on the locus. The
gain-phase plot of G(z) may be used together with the Nichols
chart to determine the frequency response of the closed-loop system.
For a basic sampled-data system with unity-feedback, the magni-
fication of the closed-loop system is given by
M =

(7-44)
where M is a function of complex variables. The magnitude and
phase shift of M, when plotted against frequency, represent the
frequency response of the closed-loop system. From the theory of
continuous-data systems, we know that the peak value of M(M,)
usually gives a qualitative measure of the relative stability of the
system. The optimum value of M, for most control systems should
lie within 1.1 and 1.5.

of all systems, or of systems with hidden oscillations, must employ

unit step function input possesses hidden oscillations between
sampling instants. The z-transform analysis in this case predicted a
monotonically increasing time response, whereas the actual response
oscillates violently in between sampling instants. Clearly, if the
z-transform method is carried over to the frequency domain analysis
of the system, similar misleading information will result. However,
we shall show that by using the modified z-transform method, salient
characteristics of the system frequency response and relative stability
may be accurately anticipated.

8
DESIGN AND COMPENSATION
OF SAMPLED-DATA SYSTEMS
BY CONTINUOUS NETWORKS
8.1 INTRODUCTION
Generally all performance specifications given on the steady-state
and transient responses of a feedback control system cannot be satis-
fied simultaneously given only the basic fixed elements over which
the engineer has little or no control. Although the adjustment of
the loop gain would seem to be a simple way of changing the system
performance, this adjustment is very seldom satisfactory as a means
of improving the system, except perhaps in very trivial situations.
For instance, if the system loop gain is increased to reduce the
steady-state error, the transient response usually will become more
oscillatory, or the system may even become unstable. Therefore, in
control system engineering, compensating devices must frequently be
introduced whose function is not only to provide absolute stability
but more important to meet specifications called for on the steady-
state as well as the transient performances.
Basically, the principle of compensation of sampled-data systems is
similar to that of systems with continuous-data. Regardless of whether
the sampling operation is inherent or purposely introduced into a
system, the output step response of a sampled-data system is usually
continuous with respect to time and is characterized by the usual
terms of specifications, such as percent overshoot, response time, rise
245

246
Chap. 8
DESIGN AND COMPENSATION
time, delay time, steady-state error, etc. Therefore, the system should
be compensated so that all these quantities lie within the specified
margins. However, due to the sampling operation, the design of the
compensation networks for sampled-data systems does present more
problems and difficulties than for systems without sampling. The
possibility of using computers as compensating elements has also
made the design of sampled-data systems more versatile and interesting.
Whenever the preliminary studies made on a certain sampled-data
system reveal that the over-all system performance does not satisfy
the given specification, compensation should be employed. Theoreti-
cally, compensation of sampled-data control systems may be incorpo-
rated in any suitable manner, but the following methods are the most
common.
1. Cascade Compensation
Compensating elements are inserted in the forward path of a
sampled-data system. The following two schemes may be used:

DESIGN AND COMPENSATION 247
(a) Compensation by Continuous Networks. In this method contin-
uous-data networks or devices such as those used in continuous-data
control systems are connected in series with the elements of the
controlled system (Fig. 8-1a).
(b) Compensation by Digital Networks. In this method, digital
controllers or networks are employed as compensating elements. The
input and output signals of a digital controller are all in pulsed form.
A digital network may be a network with synchronized samplers
located at the input and output terminals of the network, or in
general, it can be a special-purpose digital computer or a portion of
a general-purpose digital computer. A sampled-data control system
with cascaded digital controller is illustrated in Fig. 8-1b.†
2. Feedback Compensation
This includes inserting compensating elements in the feedback
path, or forming additional minor feedback loops and other more
complex measures such as the conditional feedback. A sampled-data
system with conditional feedback is illustrated in Fig. 8-2.

3. Multirate Sampling Compensation
The performance of certain sampled-data systems may be improved
by use of a multirate controller which has samplers at its input and
output; the samplers do not operate with the same sampling rate.
In general, the multirate controller may be located in either the
forward or the feedback path. A typical control system with multi-
rate controller is illustrated in Fig. 8-3.
Multirate Controller

H(s)
Fig. 8-3 Sampled-data with multirate controller.
As far as the design techniques are concerned, we recall that the
design of systems with continuous-data is usually carried out either
in the frequency domain or the time domain (s-plane). This is also
true for the compensation and design of sampled-data systems, in
view of the fact that we have extended most of the conventional
methods such as the Bode plot, Nyquist plot, root locus diagram,
etc., to the sampled-data case. In terms of the Bode plot and the
Nyquist plot, the compensation of sampled-data systems essentially
involves the reshaping of the original system locus until the desired
values for M,, phase margin, gain margin, and bandwidth are obtained.
In terms of the root locus diagram, we are working with virtually the
pole-zero configuration of the closed-loop transfer function that gives
information on the frequency response as well as the time response.
By adding poles and zeros at proper places in the z-plane, the root
loci of a sampled-data system may be reshaped so that the corre
sponding system has improved behavior.
In the present chapter, only the compensation with continuous
networks will be discussed. Digital compensation and other techniques
will be covered in Chapter 9.

Sec. 8.2
DESIGN AND COMPENSATION 249
8.2 CASCADE COMPENSATION BY CONTINUOUS-DATA
NETWORKS
A sampled-data control system with a cascade continuous-data
compensating network G.(s) is shown in Fig. 8-4. Since the com-
pensating network is connected directly to the zero-order hold and
the controlled system, the z-transform of the open-loop transfer
function is
C(z)/E(z) = Z[Gro(s) Ge(s) G1(s)] = GnoGeG1(z)
(8-1)
The main objective of continuous-data compensation is to select a
realizable network with transfer function Ge(s), which will cause the
sampled-data system to perform satisfactorily when inserted in it
as shown in Fig. 8-4. However, as Eq. (8-1) shows, the compen-
Zero-order Compensating Controlled
hold
network
system
R(s)
E(s)
Ts
1-e
H(s)
Gc (s)
G₁(s)
-C(s)
S
Fig. 8-4 Sampled-data system with continuous-data compensation.
sating network function G.(z) cannot be derived and separated from
the over-all expression Gho GeG₁(z): hence the influence of various
forms of Ge(s) on the over-all system behavior is not apparent.
Therefore, the effort of selecting a suitable Ge(s) usually involves
the construction of the transfer locus of Go GeG₁(z) (if the frequency
domain method is used) each time a new compensating network is
suggested. Perhaps only after the construction of quite a few
GoGe G1(2) plots can an intelligent choice of Ge(s) be made. For
this reason, although cascade compensation by means of continuous-
data networks may seem to be the simplest method to carry out, the
design technique is far from being as straightforward as its counter-
part in continuous-data control systems.
Several approximation methods in regard to the design of contin-
uous-data compensation as shown in Fig. 8-4 have been devised in
the literature, but none can be regarded as really satisfactory.
+ W. K. Linvill, “Sampled-Data Control Systems Studied Through Comparison of
Sampling with Amplitude Modulation," Trans. A. I. E. E., 70, Part 2, 1951, pp. 1779-

Either the approximation is too rough, or the amount of work in-
volved in the design is still overwhelming. For the purpose of
illustration, the continuous-system approximation method: is described
in the following.
In the continuous-system approximation method, the sampler and
the zero-order hold are approximated by a pure time delay equal to
one-half the sampling period of the system. The approximating system
is then a continuous system with time delay, and the conventional
design techniques may be used.
Let us consider the system shown in Fig. 8-4. The transfer func-
tion of the zero-order hold has been given in Eq. (3-10) as

in the vicinity of the new gain crossover.) A phase-lag compensation would
seem to be more appropriate for the compensation of this system.

8.3 CASCADE COMPENSATION BY BILINEAR
TRANSFORMATION METHOD†
The reasons for the rather wide spread use of frequency domain
design techniques for continuous-data systems are quite obvious. First,
the method guarantees at the outset of the design that the com-
pensating network is of a realizable form (preferrably of the RC
type); and second, successive application of the cut-and-try process
† M. P. Pastel and G. J. Thaler, "Sampled-Data Design By Log Gain Diagrams,"
I. R. E. Transactions on Automatic Control, Vol. AC-4, No. 2, Nov. 1959, pp. 192–197.

254
Chap. 8
DESIGN AND COMPENSATION
can be quickly made in the Bode diagram and the Nichols chart. We
have demonstrated in the last section that for the design of sampled-
data systems the above mentioned advantages are preserved only if
certain approximation schemes are adopted. Without approximation
the Bode diagram method cannot be employed, and furthermore,
directly connecting a continuous compensating network into the
sampled-data system requires complete recalculation of the system
z-transfer function.
In the preceding chapters the bilinear transformation z = (r + 1)/
(1) has been used to convert z-transform function G(z) of a
sampled-data system into a new function G(r), which is a rational
function in variable r. This bilinear r-transformation maps the unit
circle in the z-plane onto the imaginary axis in the r-plane. There-
fore, the Bode diagram of G(r) can be drawn in log of magnitude
and phase against frequency. Then, by use of the Nichols chart and
correlation information available from continuous-data system, the
dynamic behavior of the closed-loop sampled-data system can be
predicted from the G(r) plot and the Nichols chart.
In this section we shall present a straightforward method of
designing sampled-data control systems with continuous-data network
utilizing the r-transformation. The method does not rely on any
approximation of the sampling operation and has all the advantages
of the frequency design of continuous-data systems.
The block diagram of the system under consideration is shown
in Fig. 8-8, in which G₁(s) and G.(s) are the transfer functions of
R(s)
E(s)
Gho(s)

hold
Gc(s)

network
G₁(s)

C(s)
Fig. 8-8 Sampled-data control system with continuous-data
compensation.
the controlled system (fixed elements) and the compensating network,
respectively. The proposed steps of design are as follows:
(1) Determine the r-transform function GoGi(r) of the system.
without compensation by substituting z = (r + 1)/(r− 1) into
Gho G1(z).
(2) Construct the Bode diagram for GhoG₁(r) and transfer the
log magnitude and phase versus w, loci to the Nichols chart. Predict
the dynamic behavior of the uncompensated system by evaluating the
phase margin, gain margin, peak resonance, resonant frequency, and
bandwidth from the Nichols chart.

Sec. 8.3
DESIGN AND COMPENSATION 255
(3) Should the system need improvement, a compensating function
G(r) of the form of (1 + arr)/(1 + Tг) or (1 + aтır)(1 + bτ₂r)/
(1 + t₁r)(1 + Tar) or any other more complex type, if necessary, is
multiplied to GhoGi(r) to reshape the transfer locus of the uncom-
pensated system. These compensating functions in the r-domain, in
general, can be classified as phase-lead, phase-lag, or lead-lag con-
figurations. But they may have no direct significant correlation of
phase-lead or phase-lag in the s-plane. The purpose of identifying
G(r) in the r-domain is purely for the use of the knowledge of
continuous-data systems compensation in regard to the effects of
phase-lead and phase-lag networks on the system transfer locus. The
transfer function of the actual compensating network in s, Ge(s) is
determined from taking the inverse transform of Ge(r) GOG₁(r), or
Ge(s) Go(s) G₁(s) = inverse transform of [G(r)GoGi(r)] (8-15)
Since Go(s)G(s) is known, Ge(s) is readily obtained from the last
equation.
However, the physical realizability of G.(s) must always be kept
in mind. Furthermore, if possible, Ge(s) should be realized by RC
elements. In order for G.(s) to be RC realizable, the following re-
quirements are necessary:
(a) The poles of Ge(s) must lie in the left-half of the s-plane
and must be simple and real.
(b) The number of poles of G.(s) must be greater than or at
least equal to the number of zeros of Ge(s).
The zeros of Ge(s) may lie anywhere in the s-plane. Therefore,
the poles of Ge(s) must be restricted to the negative real axis of the
s-plane. Since the poles of G.(s) are generated by the poles of G‘(r),
it follows that G(r) must have only simple poles to the left of the
-1 point on the real axis in the r-plane (see Fig. 8-9).
(4) Once G(r)GoG(r) is determined, take the inverse transform.
to obtain Ge(s) Go(s) G1(s). It should be noted that since

Thus, the r-transform z = (r + 1)/(r − 1) and the w-transform
z = (1 + w)/(1 - w) may lead to the same (or similar) results for
Ge(s) in a given problem. Since the solution of Ge(s) for a given
design problem is not unique, independent use of the r and the w

transform methods is unlikely to lead to identical results for Ge(s)
as illustrated above. But for a fixed set of design specifications, the
results obtained by the two methods should be equally valid.
As a summary to this section, some pertinent facts about the
continuous-data compensation of sampled-data systems using r or w
transformation are summarized as follows:
(1) The r-transform method.
(a) For RC realizability of G.(s), Ge(r) must have only simple
poles to the left of the -1 point on the real axis in the
r-plane. The zeros of G(r) can lie anywhere in the
r-plane.
(b) Usually, a phase-lead model for G(r) is more desirable.
(c) In order to determine G. (s), a specific function (1, 1/(r+1)
or 1/(r+1)(r-1)) must be multiplied to Ge(r) G10G(r)
corresponding to the number of poles of G₁(s)/s at s = 0
before the partial fraction expansion is taken.
(2) The w-transform method.
-
(a) For RC realizability of Ge(s), G'(w) must have only simple
poles that lie between 0 and 1 on the real axis in the
w-plane. The zeros of G(w) can lie anywhere in the
w-plane.
(b) Usually, a phase-lag model for G(w) is more desirable.
(c) In order to determine G.(s), a specific function (1,1/(1—w),
1/(1 − w)(1 + w)) must be multiplied to G'(w) GroG₁(w),
corresponding to the number of poles of G₁(s)/s _at_s = 0
before taking the partial fraction expansion.
8.4 FEEDBACK COMPENSATION WITH CONTINUOUS-
DATA NETWORKS
Compensating networks for sampled-data control systems can also
be introduced into feedback paths. In general, the compensators may
be of the continuous-data or the digital-data types. However, only
feedback compensation with continuous-data networks will be con-
sidered in this chapter.
Two simple forms of feedback-compensated systems with sampled-
data are depicted in Fig. 8-14. In more complex situations of course
compensations may be introduced in the forward path as well as the
feedback paths.
As can be shown, the two feedback-compensated systems with
continuous-data networks in Fig. 8-14 may be represented by equi-
valent sampled-data systems with digital compensators located in the

266 DESIGN AND COMPENSATION
Chap. 8
forward paths only. Therefore, designing the feedback compensations
with continuous-data networks is essentially equivalent to finding the
cascade-digital-compensators in the equivalent systems.

However, there is still the physical realizability of H(s) to be
considered. In other words, what are the restrictions on Ge(z) so
that H(s) is always a physically realizable transfer function?
Evidently, the realizability of H(s) (and of H(s)/s) relies entirely
upon the physical realizability of the z-transform of H(s)/s, and vice
versa. For a transfer function to be realizable physically, the system
represented by the transfer function must not produce an output
prior to the application of an input. This statement is true for
continuous-data as well as sampled-data systems. For the output to
occur at or after the application of an input, the series expansion
of 3[H(s)/s] should have no posititive powers in z. Since 3[H(s)/s]
can usually be expressed in terms of the ratio of a power series in
z¹, the requirement for physical realizability is met with the follow-
ing form:

If H(s) is to be a realizable RC transfer function, then all the
poles of H(s) must be simple and must lie on the negative real axis of

lie at any place in the s-plane. Therefore, H(s)/s can be expanded

In order to expand 3[H(s)/s] into the form of Eq. (8–55), Ge(z)

the RC realizability of H(s) requires in addition that G.(z) be of
the form of Eq. (8-53); G.(2) must have an equal number of zeros and
poles; and the zeros of G.(z) must be simple and lie inside the unit
circle in the z-plane. There are no restrictions on the location of the
poles of G.(z), and therefore, an RC transfer function for H(s) can
be derived from even an unstable G.(z) that satisfies all the above
mentioned requirements but has a pole or poles outside the unit
circle.

This section has outlined methods for using feedback compensa-
tion of sampled-data systems with continuous-data networks. In
designing the feedback compensating network, an equivalent cascade-
digital compensation model may be used. Since the transfer function
of the digital compensator G.(z) is isolated from the transfer func-
tion of the controlled process Gi(z) (see Eq. (8-48)), various con-
ventional design techniques such as the root locus and the Bode
diagram methods can be used immediately, without approximation
in designing G.(z). However, detailed discussions and illustrations of
the design of digital compensations will be given in the next chapter.
Note that the feedback structure depicted in Fig. 8-16 may also
serve as a realization of a digital compensator utilizing RC networks.

9
DESIGN AND SYNTHESIS BY DIGITAL
CONTROLLERS
9.1 INTRODUCTION
In the preceding chapter, compensation of sampled-data control
systems is accomplished by the insertion of continuous-data networks
in the forward or the feedback paths of the systems. Since the
continuous-data network is connected directly to the controlled
process, the z-transform of the product of the two transfer functions
is a single function. Therefore, analytically it is difficult to evaluate
the effects of various continuous networks on the over-all performance
of the system. Usually, in order to reduce the design effort, approxi-
mation methods such as the time-delay approximation described in
the last chapter have to be used. A design method using the bilinear
r-transformation or the w-transformation was introduced. The method
has the advantage of giving exact solutions without using approxi-
mations.
Alternately, compensation of a sampled-data system can also be
accomplished by introducing pulsed-data networks, or digital control-
lers into the system. The process, therefore, is referred to as the
digital compensation of sampled-data systems.
Although continuous-data networks are simple to implement and
economical to build, the digital controller is in general more versatile,
and thus better system performance can be achieved by means of
digital compensation. Furthermore, digital compensation may be
272

Sec. 9.1
273

applied to continuous-data systems as well as sampled-data systems.
Several possible configurations of the digital compensation of
sampled-data and continuous-data control systems are depicted in
Fig. 9-1.
Figure 9-1a shows that a digital controller is introduced in the
forward path of a feedback control system. The system could be
considered as a sampled-data system or a continuous-data system
with digital compensation. If the uncompensated system is a sampled-
data system, D(s) and the sampler following D(s) represent the
digital compensator unit. If the uncompensated system is considered
a continuous-data system, D(s), the two synchronized switches and
the zero-order hold Go(s) represent the digital compensator unit.

274 DESIGN AND SYNTHESIS BY DIGITAL CONTROLLERS
Chap. 9
The system shown in Fig. 9-1b can be considered as a continuous-
data system with bypass digital compensation. The actuating signal
E(s) to the controlled process is equal to the sum of the continuous
error E(s) and the sampled error E2(s). Figure 9-1c illustrates the
insertion of a digital controller in the feedback path of a continuous-
data system.
The conventional design techniques utilizing such tools as the
Nyquist plot, the Bode diagram, and the root locus plot, can be
applied readily to the design of digital compensation. Let us investigate
the sampled-data system shown in Fig. 9-1a. The closed-loop
z-transfer function of the system is written
C(z) D(z) GhoG₁(2)
=
10
R(z) 1+D(2)GoG1 (2)
(9-1)
In Eq. (9-1) since D(z)GoG₁(z) is the product of the pulse transfer
function D(z) of the digital controller and the z-transform of
GhoGI(s), the variation of D(z) can be controlled independently.
Therefore, in terms of the frequency loci, the reshaping of the locus
of GhoGi(z) by use of various forms of D(z) can be studied in a
straightforward manner, and the selection of the desired D(z) is
easily accomplished.
Likewise, selection of the desired D(z) can be effected by observ-
ing the effect of the adjustment and variation of the poles and
zeros of D(z) upon the root locus of the characteristic equation.
Under certain circumstances, zeros may be introduced to D(z) to
cancel the unwanted poles of GOG(2), and new poles of D(z) are
added at appropriate places to improve the system performance.
The first part of this chapter is devoted to the use of con-
ventional techniques for the design of digital compensation of feed-
back control systems with an emphasis on the Bode plot and the
root locus methods.
9.2 THE DIGITAL CONTROLLER
Before entering into the design of digital compensation, we shall
discuss the construction and the realizability of the digital controller,
the fundamental ingredient of a control system with digital compen-
sation. The block diagram of a digital controller is shown in Fig.
9-2.
With reference to Fig. 9-2, the transfer function of the digital
controller is defined as
E2(z)
D(z) =
E,(z)

Digital controller
Fig. 9-2 The block diagram of a digital controller.
The input e(t) to the digital controller is in the form of discrete
samples of e(t). The digital controller performs certain linear
operations on the input samples and delivers a modified pulse sequence
e (t) to the output.
There are many ways of implementing a digital controller. A
digital controller may contain a passive network preceded and
followed by samplers. The digital controller may also be a special-
purpose digital computer or a general-purpose computer. This ap-
proach enables one to realize certain functional operations that are
not obtainable with passive elements.
The a priori requirement in the design of the digital controller
D(z) is that it be physically realizable. The effort would be worth-
less if the design results in a transfer function for D(z) which
cannot be realized by physical elements, passive or active.
The condition of physical realizability of any system implies that
no output signal of the system will appear before an input signal
is applied. If the transfer function D(z) of the digital controller is
expressed as a ratio of two polynomials in z
D(z) =
Anz" + An-12″-1 +
bmz+bm-12m-1 +
+ ao
+ bo
(9-3)
Expanding D(z) in a power series in z', the coefficients of the
series represent the values of the weighting sequence of the digital
controller. The coefficient of the z* term corresponds to the value
of the weighting sequence at t = kT, where k is an integer. Clearly,
for the digital controller to be physically realizable, the power
series expansion of D(z) must not contain any positive power in
z. Any positive power in z in the series expansion of D(z) simply
indicates "prediction" or simply that the output signal precedes
the input signal. Therefore, for D(z) to be physically realizable,
the highest power of the denominator must be equal to or greater
than that of the numerator in Eq. (9-3), or simply mn.
Quite often D(2) has equal number of poles and zeros and is
written as
D(z)

where n and m are any positive integers. In this case, the denomi-
nator must not contain any factor of z'. Therefore, the condition
for D(z) to be realizable is that bo 0 in Eq. (9-4).
The realization of the transfer function D(z) of a digital con-
troller by physical networks or devices is quite flexible and may take
many forms. The most common measures are the realization by
pulsed-data RC networks and digital computers.
1. Realization of D(z) by Pulsed-data RC Networks
The use of a pulsed-data RC network to realize the transfer
function D(z) of a digital controller follows directly from the
discussion given in Sec. 8-4. As was shown in Sec. 8-4, the design
of a feedback compensation with continuous-data networks can be
carried out in an equivalent system with series digital compensation.
In essence, the two systems shown in Fig. 8-16 are equivalent to
each other if the transfer functions are related by

The problem in this section of course involves the determination of
H(s), the transfer function of the feedback pulsed-data network,
once D(z) is given.
A digital controller can also be realized with a series pulsed-data
network consisting of a zero-order hold and an RC network preceded
and followed by a sampler. In more complicated situations a com-
bination of the series and the feedback pulsed-data configurations
may lead to the realization of more complex digital controllers.
These three possibilities of realizing a digital controller are identified
and discussed below as the series pulsed-data networks, the feedback
pulsed-data networks, and the series-feedback pulsed-data networks.
Series Pulsed-data Networks. The series pulsed-data network which
is used for the realization of the pulse transfer function of a digital
controller is depicted in Fig. 9-3. The transfer functions of the two
systems in Fig. 9-3 are related by

In order to realize H(s) by an RC network, the pulse transfer
function D(z) must have the following properties:
(i) D(z) must have the same number of poles and zeros.
(ii) The poles of D(z) are arbitrary.
(iii) The zeros of D(z) must be simple, real, positive, and lie
inside the unit circle of the z-plane.
The justifications to these restrictions have been discussed in
detail in Sec. 8.4 and therefore are not repeated here.
Note that in the feedback structure, restrictions are placed on
the location of the zeros rather than on the poles of D(z). In fact,
H(s) is RC realizable even if D(z) has poles outside the unit circle.
This characteristic of the feedback structure is not unexpected since
it is well known that any feedback system, even one with only
passive elements, may be stable or unstable under various operating
conditions.
An example illustrating the realization of a pulse-tranfer function
by the feedback pulse-data network is given in Example 8-3.
(c) Series-feedback Pulse-data Networks. Both the series network
and the feedback pulse-data network discussed above contain re-
strictions in the realization of pulse transfer functions. In the series
structure the zeros of D(z) are arbitrary, whereas in the feedback

exception of course that D(z) must be physically realizable. Many
such combinations are conceivable, but let us consider the simple
feedback-series pulse-data network shown in Fig. 9-6. Sklansky has

Dr(z) and D,(z) are identical to the transfer functions of the
feedback pulse-data network and the series pulse-data network in
Eq. (9-15) and (9-6), respectively. Therefore, D(z) must have all
the properties of a pulse transfer function that can be realized by
the feedback pulse-data network with RC elements. This means that
Dr(z) should contain all the poles of D(z) that are complex and
all the unstable poles. The zeros of D(z) must be simple, real, and
positive, and lie inside the unit circle. The number of poles and
zeros of D(z) must be equal. Similarly, D,(z) must satisfy all the
requirements in order to be realized by a series pulse-data network
with RC elements. Thus D.(z) should contain all the zeros of D(z)
that are complex or on or outside the unit circle. Since it is assumed
that D(z) is physically realizable and that D,(z) has an equal
number of poles and zeros, D、(z) certainly does not have more zeros
than poles.

2. Realization of D(z) by Digital Computers
The implementation of a pulse transfer function D(z) can be
accomplished with a general-purpose or a special-purpose digital
computer; the latter is sometimes also referred to as a pulsed-data
processing unit. Because of the advantages in computing speed,
storage capacity, and flexibility, the use of digital computers in
control systems has become increasingly important. Furthermore,
due to the discrete property of the signals in a digital controller,
the realization of a pulse transfer function by a digital computer
seems to be quite natural.
In general, a pulse transfer function D(z) can be realized through
a program of a general-purpose digital computer. The following
three methods of programming have been discussed in the literature,‡
direct programming, cascade programming, and parallel programming.
In the pulse-data processing unit method, a special-purpose digital
computer may be built which is capable of performing specific
arithmetic manipulations of multiplication, storage, addition, and
subtraction, etc. Digital and analog devices may also be combined
in a pulse-data processing unit. For instance, the storage operation
may be implemented with either analog or digital devices. If digital
elements are used, the stored samples could be retained in coded
forms on registers, magnetic tapes, or drums. If analog elements
are used, data could be stored in the form of some analog quantities
such as current, voltage, or mechanical displacement.
The above mentioned methods of implementing a pulse transfer
function are discussed in the following paragraphs.
↑ A. R. Bergen, J. R. Ragazzini, "Sampled-data Processing Techniques For Feedback
Control Systems," Trans. A. I. E. E., Part 2, Nov. 1954, pp. 236-244.
‡ J. M. Salzer, "Frequency Analysis of Digital Computers Operating in Real Time,”
Proc. I. R. E., Vol. 42, No. 2, 1954, pp. 457-466.

the past information of the input and also on the past information

program, two basic mathematical operations are required. The first
operation is the data storage. Past samples on the output and the
input signal must be stored in order that they may be used in the
computation of the output sample e(t). The second operation
involves arithmetic manipulations including multiplication of the
stored output and the input samples by constants b b。_and_ax bɔ,
