288 DESIGN AND SYNTHESIS BY DIGITAL CONTROLLERS
Chap. 9
(d) Pulsed-data Processing Units or Special-purpose Computers. In
the method of realizing a pulse transfer function by digital pro-
gramming, a program of a general-purpose digital computer is written
so that when the machine reads this program, it will carry out the
mathematical operations described by the specific pulse transfer
function. When different pulse transfer functions are being realized,
the same digital computer may be used with variations in the
programming. On the other hand, a special-purpose computer, some-
times also referred to as a pulsed-data processing unit, may be built
specially for the realization of a specific pulse transfer function D(z).
However, a pulsed-data processing unit is good only for the reali-
zation of a certain pulse transfer function. Separate computers must
be built to realize different pulse tranfer functions.
The principle of realization of D(z) by a pulsed-data processing
unit is similar to that of digital programming described in the
preceding sections. In essence the data processing unit should be
capable of performing specific arithmetic manipulations such as multi-
plication, time delays, and addition and subtraction. In a data
processing unit these arithmetic manipulations may be implemented
by transmission lines for time delays, potentiometers and summing
amplifiers for multiplication, weighting, and subtraction and addition.
These operations need not be carried out by digital elements only.
Sometimes digital as well as analog devices may be used in the
implementation of a pulsed-data processing unit.
To illustrate the realization of a pulse transfer function by a
pulsed-data processing unit, the following simple example is con-
sidered.
Example 9-2

9.3 DIGITAL COMPENSATION DESIGN THROUGH
BILINEAR TRANSFORMATION
=
In Sec. 8.3 the bilinear transformations z (r + 1)/(r− 1) and
z = (1 + w)/(1 - w) were used for the Bode diagram design of
continuous-data network compensation of sampled-data systems. In
a similar fashion the extension of the Bode diagram method to the
design of digital compensation may be easily carried out if any one
of the bilinear transformations is utilized. In fact, the Bode diagram
design of digital compensation in the r or the w domain is more
straightforward than that of the continuous network compensation,
since the transfer function of the digital controller can be adjusted
independently without affecting the transfer functions of the other
parts of the system. Therefore, the effects of the adjustment and
variation of the poles and zeros of D(w) (or D(r)) upon the over-
all system behavior are easily observed.
With reference to the system shown in Fig. 9-1a, the design
procedure of the digital compensation is outlined as follows:
(1) Assuming that the w-transformation is used, determine the
w-transform function GoG₁(w) of the uncompensated system by
substituting z = (1 + w)/(1-w) into G10G1(2).
Go
(2) Construct the Bode diagram for GoG₁(w) in magnitude
versus ww and phase shift versus ww. Transfer the plots in the Bode
diagram to the Nichols chart if necessary. Predict the dynamic
behavior of the uncompensated system by finding the phase margin,

290
Chap. 9
DESIGN AND SYNTHESIS BY DIGITAL CONTROLLERS
gain margin, resonant frequency, and bandwidth from the Bode plot
and the Nichols chart.
(3) Should the system need compensation, the open-loop trans-
fer function of the system with digital compensation becomes
D(w)GoG₁(w). A proper digital controller D(w) is to be selected
so that the desired system performance specifications are satisfied.
The selection of D(w) simply follows the conventional technique
of compensating continuous-data systems, which involves a trial-and-
error procedure and is to some extent based on the experience and
the imagination of the designer.
The physical realizability of D(z) requires that it have at least
as many poles as zeros or that m>n in Eq. (9-3). To determine
the restrictions on D(w) so that the corresponding D(2) is always
realizable, let us consider that D(w) is described by the form

where a and b are related to c and d, respectively. Equation (9-48)

physical realizability condition of D(z) does not affect the condition.
of the number of zeros and poles of D(w). Thus in Eq. (9–47) p
and q can be chosen arbitrarily and the corresponding D(z) will
always have the same number of poles and zeros. However, if it is
desired that the digital controller is stable, then all the poles of
D(w) must lie in the left-half of the w-plane (corresponding to the
inside of the unit circle in the z-plane), and in addition D(w) must
not have more zeros than poles. From Eq. (9-48) note that if p > q,
D(z) will have (p − q) poles at 2 = 1 that correspond to sustained
oscillatory responses. However, an unstable pulse transfer function
is considered realizable. In certain cases the use of unstable digital
controllers is even desirable.

the realization of D(z) by one of the methods discussed in Sec. 9.2.

follows.

9.4 DESIGN IN THE Z-PLANE USING THE ROOT LOCUS
In Sec. 6.7 the root locus technique has been extended to the
analysis of sampled-data feedback control systems. Note that prefer-
rably the root locus plots for sampled-data systems should be plotted
in the z-plane and that their properties are quite similar to those
of the root locus for continuous-data systems in the s-plane. There-
fore, the application of the root locus method to the analysis of
sampled-data control systems is quite straightforward. Once the root
loci of the characteristic equation are plotted in the z-plane, much
knowledge concerning the transient response of the system can be
gathered by observing the location of the roots for the particular
loop gain K.
On the other hand, the design of sampled-data control systems
can also be carried out in the z-plane using the root locus. In essence,
the design of a sampled-data system in the z-plane involves the
search for a set of characteristic equation roots that give rise to a
satisfactory over-all system performance. In terms of the root locus
design, these desirable characteristic equation roots are found by
reshaping the root loci of the original system through adjustment
of the loop gain and the use of compensation networks. The most

296 DESIGN AND SYNTHESIS BY DIGITAL CONTROLLERS
Chap. 9
elementary problem in root locus design is the determination of the
loop gain to yield suitable relative stability. The loop gain of the
system can be adjusted to give appropriate dynamic performance as
measured by the position of the complex poles with respect to the
constant damping ratio curves inside the unit circle. However, no
simple rules are available for the determination of appropriate com-
pensation networks from the root locus diagram. In most design prob-
lems the effects on the characteristic equation roots when the loop
gain K, as well as other parameters, is varied must be studied.
Therefore, the design in the z-plane with root locus usually involves
a certain amount of trial-and-error. Usually, some knowledge on
the effect of adding or shifting of open-loop poles and zeros on the
shape of a root locus is helpful on the part of the designer. The
use of the root contour method is also illustrated in this chapter
when a sampled-data system has variable parameters other than K.
In the design of continuous-data control systems, usually, the
design may fall into one of the following categories: (1) phase-lead
compensation, (2) phase-lag compensation, and (3) cancellation com-
pensation. Other more complex schemes of course do exist, but
knowing the effects of these three basic types of compensation
usually gives a designer much insight into the design problem. With
reference to the design of sampled-data systems by the Bode diagram,
the basic forms of the compensating network D(w) or D(r) have
also been classified as phase-lead and phase-lag; and certainly can-
cellation compensation can always be used whenever and wherever
feasible. Therefore, in the following paragraphs, the design of com-
pensation for sampled-data systems is also described as phase-lead,
phase-lag, or cancellation.
Phase-Lead Compensation
A simple phase-lead network model in the w-domain is described.
by the transfer function
1 + ATW
D(w)
=
1 + τω

Eq. (9-63) is referred to as a phase-lead transfer function, since in
terms of the Bode diagram it produces positive phase shift that may
be added to the system phase shift in the vicinity of the gain cross-
over frequency (ww) to increase the phase margin. However, the
B. C. Kuo, Automatic Control Systems, (Englewood Cliffs, N. J.: Prentice-Hall,
Inc., 1962), Chapter 8.

Sec. 9.4
DESIGN AND SYNTHESIS BY DIGITAL CONTROLLERS 297
use of phase-lead compensation is not recommended under all cir-
cumstances. In fact, it has been shown that the use of phase-lead
compensation is unlikely to bring satisfactory results when the
uncompensated system has the following properties:
(1) when the transfer function of the controlled process G₁(w)
has two poles that are close together and the corner frequencies
are located near the gain crossover frequency (ww) in the Bode plot
(2) when the transfer function of the controlled process G₁(w)
has a pair of complex conjugate poles located near the gain cross-
over
Both the above cases will cause the phase shift curve of G₁(w)
to vary rapidly near 180 degrees.

-
(3) when the uncompensated system is initially unstable or lightly
damped.
For these same reasons phase-lead compensations were not recom-
mended in the illustrative examples on the design of sampled-data
systems (Example 9-3, for instance).

has a tendency to push the loci away, no simple ways exist for telling which
combinations are the most effective for stabilizing the system because of the
unlimited number of possible combinations of the pole and zero of D(z).

result in stable systems. But then the damping ratios of the systems are less

Note that the system is improved, though only slightly, when the value
of is relatively small. However, if the value of 7 is too small, the pole of

at this pole and ends at -∞, the compensated system will be very lightly
damped or unstable. On the other hand, the root loci in Fig. 9-18 show that
for large values of the phase-lead compensation also results in unstable
systems.
The general ineffectiveness of the phase-lead compensation is anticipated
in this problem, since the original system is on the verge of instability and
this situation is one for which phase-lead compensation is not recommended.
In the next section we shall see that a phase-lag compensation is more satis-
factory for improving the stability of this system.
We can also show that if the loop gain K is reduced from the value of
2.43, the effectiveness of the phase-lead compensation is improved; however,
this improvement is usually at the expense of higher bandwidth and resonant
frequency.

The Root Contour Method
The root contour diagram of a closed-loop system is the plot of
the roots of the characteristic equation when any system parameter
other than the open-loop gain K is varying. Apparently, the root
contour diagram is quite useful in the design of feedback control
systems, since it can be used to indicate the effects of variations in
the poles and zeros of the open-loop transfer function. By use of
the root contour plot, the effects of phase-lead and phase-lag com-
pensations may be clearly indicated, and appropriate values of the
parameters of the controllers can be easily determined.
As an illustrative example to the root contour method, let us
consider the sampled-data system given in Example 9-3 and Example
9-4. Since the phase-lead and the phase-lag controllers have basically
the same transfer function, but with different ranges for a, the open-
loop transfer function of the sampled-data system with either com-
pensation is given by

On the other hand, when a <1, the compensation is phase-lag.

for small values of T. But the system can be stabilized at will if a
large value of is used.
Cancellation Compensation
On a theoretical basis at least, it is always possible to use a
digital compensator with poles and zeros that can cancel the un-
desirable poles and zeros of the controlled process transfer function,
and new open-loop poles and zeros can be inserted at more advan-
tageous locations in the z-plane to satisfy the design specifications.
The cancellation compensation suggested above is often used in
systems that have complex poles in the controlled process, since in
such cases simple phase-lead compensation is known to be ineffective.
However, the cancellation compensation does not always furnish a
completely satisfactory solution to a design problem, Quite often,
the resulting compensator may involve excessive complexity; and if
the undesirable complex poles of the controlled process are near the
unit circle in the z-plane, inexact cancellation can often result in a

conditionally stable system. As a simple example to the effect of
inexact cancellation, let us refer to the root locus diagrams given
in Fig. 9-24. In Fig. 9-24a the pole-zero configuration of the open-
loop transfer function of a hypothetical sampled-data system is shown.

(9-81)
is devised to cancel the complex poles at p₁ and p, and to add new
poles at z = a and z = b. Figure 9-24b shows a particular case of
inexact cancellation, which occurs when the complex zeros of the
digital controller are not exactly equal to the poles p and p₂ of
the controlled process. In this case, the inexact cancellation does
not have any adverse effect on the performance of the compensated
system, since the closed-loop dipole near p₁ and p as a result of
the inexact cancellation, does not contribute significantly to either
the transient or the frequency response.
On the other hand, if the poles and zeros of the inexact cancel-
lation are arranged as shown in Fig. 9-24c, parts of the root loci
between the uncancelled pole and zero pairs lie outside the unit
circle. Then the system may be conditionally stable or even unstable.
9.5 SYNTHESIS IN THE Z-PLANE
Thus far, the frequency domain and the root locus method of
design of the digital compensation of sampled-data control systems
have been discussed. The principal disadvantage of these methods
of design lies in the extent of the cut-and-try effort imposed on the
designer. In proceeding from specifications to the final system, the
designer must base his decisions on his past experiences and on the
nature of the problem and select an appropriate compensator to
modify the open-loop system in such a way that the closed-loop
system is satisfactory. If as the result of the designer's first rough
guess, the specifications are not met by the system, he must keep
on modifying the open-loop system until the closed-loop system has
a satisfactory performance.
A logical synthesis procedure should be devised to enable the
designer to proceed from specifications to the final system in a
straightforward manner. A well-known method for the synthesis of
linear continuous-data control systems using pole-zero configurations
in the s-plane was first set forth by Truxal. The philosophy of
↑ J. G. Truxal, Automatic Feedback Control System Synthesis, (New York: McGraw-
Hill Book Company, 1955), Chapter 5.

310 DESIGN AND SYNTHESIS BY DIGITAL CONTROLLERS
Chap. 9
Truxal's synthesis will be extended to the synthesis of sampled-data
control systems in this section.
The synthesis procedure essentially involves the following three
steps:
(1) The closed-loop z-transfer function of the final system is
determined from the specifications and the fixed parts of the system.
(2) The corresponding z-transfer functions of the open-loop system
and the digital controller are found.
(3) The digital controller is synthesized.
In contrast to the conventional design methods, the synthesis
procedure outlined above enables the designer to work with the
over-all system function, so that in a sense the system is forced to
meet the specifications.
Determination of the Closed-Loop Transfer Function From the
Specifications and the Fixed Parts of the System
The first step in the synthesis involves the determination of the
closed-loop transfer function of the sampled-data system from the
specifications and the fixed elements of the system. Although, in
general, specifications in control systems may take a wide variety
of forms, any but the most commonly used ones are difficult to
consider. Therefore, for the proposed synthesis procedure only the
following time domain specifications are imposed :
damping ratio of the control poles
overshoot
peak time
velocity error constant
The first three specify the transient response of the system, whereas
the last one defines the steady-state performance of the system to a
ramp input.
The basic model of the over-all system is shown by the block
diagram in Fig. 9-25. The system is characterized by two basic
components: the digital controller and the controlled process that
includes the fixed elements of the system. The closed-loop transfer
function of the system in Fig. 9-25 is given by

In general, the selection of the desired closed-loop function M(z)
is governed by both the specifications and the nature of G,(x).
Evidently, the M(z) selected by the designer not only must satisfy
all the specifications but also should lead to a transfer function D(z)
for the digital controller which is physically realizable. It has been
shown in Sec. 9.2 that for D(z) to be physically realizable the
degree of the numerator in z must not exceed that of the denomi-
nator.
Solving for D(z) in Eq. (9-82) in terms of M(z) and G,(z)

(9-84)
As the last equation demonstrates, to ensure that D(z) is a physically
realizable pulse transfer function (numerator order equals or is less
than the order of the denominator), the excess of poles over zeros
for M(z) must at least equal the excess of poles over zeros for
G,(z). For instance, if G,(z) has two finite poles and one finite
zero, an excess of poles over zero of one, the selected configuration
for M(z) must contain at least one more pole than zeros.
If a stable digital controller is desired, then the poles of D(z)
must lie inside the unit circle with the exception of a simple pole
at ≈ = 1. An unstable D(z) of course may still be realizable, but
if the synthesis can be accomplished with a stable digital controller,
certainly an unstable D(z) should be avoided because it will cor-
respond to an unstable open-loop system.
-
With reference to Eq. (9-84), we notice that the stability of
D(z) requires that the polynomial q(z) – p(z) have no zero on or
outside the unit circle with the exception of a possible simple zero
at z 1. Furthermore, if the fixed elements G,(z) has zeros outside
the unit circle, M(z) must contain the same zeros, or these zeros
will become the unstable poles of D(z).
=

performance specifications. These specifications have been listed.
previously as the damping ratio, maximum overshoot, peak time,

and velocity error constant. The relationships between the pole-zero

and peak time have been established in Sec. 6.7. Figures 6-20 and
6-21 give the percent overshoot and the peak time, respectively, in

These relationships and the plots that were obtained previously are
basic and of great importance in regard to the synthesis procedure
proposed here.

width is particularly objectionable in sampled-data control systems,
since the passage of high frequency harmonics generated by the
sampler may cause hidden oscillations in the output response. Also,
because of the inherently low relative stability or instability of a
sampled-data system, a phase-lead compensation seldom gives satis-
factory compensation results. The addition of a simple zero to
C(z)/R(z) (accompanied by a pole near the origin) at any place
on the real axis other than the origin or near the origin has the
effect of causing larger overshoot in the step response and wider
bandwidth. The synthesis is complicated by the fact that the transient
response is no longer controlled just by the zero z, and the basic
control poles p, and p2. Therefore, with the addition of the zero the
design curves of Figs. 6-20 and 6-21, which are prepared on the
basis of the simple P1, P2, z, configuration, are no longer valid for
the prediction of the transient response and have to be modified.
In view of the reasons given above, the method of adding a zero
for increasing K, is not generally preferred in the synthesis of a
sampled-data control system. The dipole method is considered to be
more effective and straightforward.
Example of the Synthesis Method
The method of synthesis is now illustrated by the following
examples. The examples are chosen for the purpose of facilitating
the understanding of the basic elements of the design procedure.

The system designed in this example has a velocity constant four times
greater than that of the system designed in the previous example. When
the step responses of the two systems are compared, the overshoot of the
dipole compensated system has only about 3 percent higher overshoot. How-
ever, the transient response of the dipole compensated system contains a long
tail of small amplitude. This long-duration tail is anticipated in all systems
with integral compensation, since the added transient term due to the dipole
has a time constant of 1/pt, which is long compared to the original time
constants of the system.
In this section, an attempt has been made to present an extension
of Truxal's synthesis method of the continuous-data systems to the
synthesis of sampled-data systems. The synthesis procedure is aimed
toward reducing the complexity of the problem to the point where
the dynamic characteristics of the system can be described by two
control poles and one zero in the z-plane. The synthesis procedure
essentially involves a selection of these control poles and zero to
meet the performance specifications and a physically realizable digital
compensator. The compensator need not be stable, although stability
is preferable. Additional poles may be added to C(z)/R(2) near the
origin of the z-plane if the fixed part of the system has an excess
of poles over zeros of more than one. However, the realizability of
D(z) in sampled-data systems is not nearly as critical as in the
continuous-data case, since a third order continuous-data plant with
hold, say
Gp(s)
=
(1 e-Ts)
s(s+1)(s+ 2)
(9-137)

Sec. 9.6
DESIGN AND SYNTHESIS BY DIGITAL CONTROLLERS 325
corresponds to a z-transfer function G,(z) with three poles and
two zeros, producing an excess of poles over zeros of only one. A
dipole may be added near z = +1 in the z-plane to increase the
velocity error constant without appreciably affecting the bandwidth
and the transient overshoot of the designed system.
9.6 DESIGN OF SAMPLED DATA SYSTEMS WITH
"MINIMAL" OR "DEADBEAT" RESPONSES+
In the preceding sections the design and synthesis of sampled-
data control systems have relied to a great extent on the extension
of the techniques of synthesis and design of continuous-data control
systems. The criteria for the frequency-domain design of sampled-
data control systems are generally expressed in the form of the
phase and gain margins, resonant peak, and bandwidth. The design
may be viewed as the shaping of the frequency locus of the system
to obtain satisfactory relative stability. In the synthesis of sampled-
data systems, the criteria of performance are expressed in the form
of time domain specifications, such as the overshoot, peak time, and
damping ratio.
Because of the flexibility in the application and realizability of
a digital controller, a sampled-data system can be designed with
minimum settling time and zero steady-state error at the sampling
instants when a specific test signal such as a step, ramp, or parabolic
is applied. With reference to the system configuration of Fig. 9-35,
R(z)
E(z)
D(s)
Gro(s)
C(s)
G₁(s)
D(z)
Gp (z)
Fig. 9-35 Single-loop sampled-data feedback control system.
a physically realizable digital controller D(z) can be designed so
that for a particular test input the outputs at the sampling instants
are as specified. In essence the design criteria are as follows:
(1) The system must have zero steady-state error at the sampling
instants for the specified input test signal.
(2) The rise time should equal a minimum number of sampling
periods.
(3) The settling time, measured at the sampling instants, should
be finite.
† J. E. Bertram, “Factors in the Design of Digital Controllers for Sampled-data
Feedback Systems," Trans. A. I. E. E., vol. 75, Part 2, July 1956, pp. 151–159.

326 DESIGN AND SYNTHESIS BY DIGITAL CONTROLLERS
Chap. 9
(4) D(z), G,(z), and C(z)/R(z) must all be physically realizable.
A response that satisfies all these properties is referred to as a
"minimal" or "deadbeat" response.
The design of the "minimal" response system in a sense has the
same philosophy as the synthesis method described in the preceding
section. The design procedure starts with the selection of an over-
all system function M(z) meeting the specifications of minimum
rise time, finite settling time, and zero steady-state error and simul-
taneously resulting in a physically realizable controller. To obtain
such complete control over the transient behavior of the system
requires specification of both the zeros and poles of the over-all
system function M(2). However, in view of the four design criteria
listed above and of the selection of the over-all minimal response
function, M(z) for a given input signal is likely to have restrictions.
These restrictions are found by considering the criteria and realiza-
bility conditions in the following:
Steady-State Error and Finite Settling Time
As has been shown previously, the transfer function of the digital
controller in the system of Fig. 9-35 is given by

on the function 1 M(z) which appears in the denominator of

(9-169)
Referring to the computations above, we may conclude that the
designed system has a minimal response at the sampling instants
only when the input is a step function. When the system is subjected
to an input other than a step function, the response is not only
not minimal but is not acceptable by any standards. This char-
acteristic, however, is typical of the minimal response design. The
designed system may be said to be "tuned" for a particular test signal,
and it does not respond satisfactorily to any other type of input.
Figure 9-37 illustrates the responses of the minimal response
systems designed on the basis of the step, ramp, and acceleration
inputs. In each case the effect of having only minimal response for
a particular input is clearly shown. For instance, Fig. 9-37b shows
that the step response of a system which is designed to have a
minimal response to a ramp input has an overshoot of 100 percent.
Comments on the Minimal Response Design
Both the minimal response design and the z-plane synthesis
method rely heavily on the cancellation of the undesirable poles
and zeros of G,(z). In this respect the philosophies of the two
methods are quite similar. In the design for minimal response D(z)
is selected with poles and zeros cancelling the unwanted poles and
zeros of G,(z) so that all the poles of the over-all system function
lie at the origin in the z-plane. In the synthesis method described
in Sec. 9.5, D(2) is designed to cancel the undesirable poles and
zeros of G,(z), and at the same time, poles and zeros of D(z) are
inserted at more appropriate places so that the over-all system
satisfies the specified performance criteria.
Since the minimal design can result in a system whose output
follows the input exactly after a minimum number of sampling
periods with no overshoot and since the design procedure is straight-
forward, the minimal design would, at first glance, seem to be a far
superior method than the z-plane synthesis and any of the other
methods described previously. However, the minimal response design
has at least the following disadvantages:
(1) The design refers to the response at only the sampling

Sec. 9.6
DESIGN AND SYNTHESIS BY DIGITAL CONTROLLERS 333
instants. A minimal response at the sampling instants does not
necessarily assure a minimal response at all times.
(2) The design is optimum only for the specific input for which
it is intended. The designed system does not provide satisfactory
performance to other inputs.
(3) From the point of view of sensitivity, the multiple order pole
of the closed-loop transfer function at the origin (or at any other
point in the z-plane) is undesirable. It will be shown that the
sensitivity of the multiple order pole due to a change in any system
parameter k is infinite.t
The sensitivity of the roots of the characteristic equation of a
sampled-data system with respect to the incremental change of a
system parameter k is defined as
Si

Thus at a multiple order root of the characteristic equation, either
dk/dz = 0 or S is infinite. In other words, the minimal response
design which places all poles of C(z) R/(z) at the origin of the
z-plane has the system operating at a point of infinite sensitivity
of pole position to parameter variations. Furthermore, Eq. (9-179)
shows that the larger the number of poles placed at a single point,
the more sensitive will be the pole locations to parameter changes.
The derivations given above simply show that a minimal response
is extremely sensitive to parameter changes in practical systems.
Therefore, in the design of any system, if consideration is placed
on the sensitivity of response to parameter changes, the use of the
minimal response design must be precluded.
Design of Systems with Plant Poles and Zeros Outside the Unit Circle
We know that the minimal response design of sampled-data control
systems is achieved through cancellation of the undesired poles and
zeros of the transfer function of the controlled process G,(z).
Unfortunately, because of parameter variations and inaccuracy in
the description of a physical system by a transfer function, perfect
cancellation of poles and zeros are impossible in any practical system.
It has been shown previously that an imperfect cancellation of
either a zero or a pole of G,(z) outside the unit circle by the pole
and zero of D(z) may lead to a system which is unstable. For this
reason the design procedures discussed previously have been limited
† R. H. Barker, "The Theory of Pulse-Monitored Servo and Their use for Predic-
tion," Report No. 1046, Signals Research and Development Establishment, Christchurch,
England, Nov. 1950.

Sec. 9.6
DESIGN AND SYNTHESIS BY DIGITAL CONTROLLERS 335
to only those systems with G,(z) which have poles and zeros inside
the unit circle. For a system with a G,(z) that has poles and zeros
outside the unit circle, a separate treatment is necessary.
Let us first illustrate the effect of imperfect cancellation of poles
and zeros of G,(z) that lie outside the unit circle.

The zeros of the last equation are the roots of the characteristic

imperfect cancellation of z, and p, may result in a system which is
conditionally stable. On the other hand, if z, and p, are located
just inside the unit circle, imperfect cancellation of the pole and
zero may still lead to an unstable system.
The conclusion drawn from this discussion is that if a controlled
system of a sampled-data control system has a pulse transfer function
which contains zeros and poles which lie on or outside the unit
circle in the z-plane, no attempt is to be made to cancel these poles
and zeros with the poles and zeros of a digital controller, since
imperfect cancellations may cause the system to be unstable. This
simply imposes additional constraints on the selection of the over-
all system transfer function for the minimal response design. Rewrit-

9.7 DESIGN USING THE STALENESS WEIGHTING FACTOR
In the preceding section we have seen that sampled-data systems
which are designed for finite settling time and zero steady-state error
for a particular test input usually do not provide satisfactory per-
formances when other types of inputs are applied. For instance, the
system in Example 9-9 is designed to respond with finite settling
time and zero steady-state error when subjected to a ramp input.
However, the step response of the designed system exhibits a highly
undesirable peak overshoot of 128 percent. A system that is extremely
sensitive to the form of its input signal is seldom acceptable in
practice, since it is very unlikely that the system will be subject to
only the specific kind of input signal for which it is designed.
Therefore, a design procedure should be developed to lead to a
system that will respond satisfactorily not just to one signal but to
a number of different test signals. A compromise may be reached
among the responses of a system to several different test signals by
introducing a "staleness-weighting factor" into the over-all system
transfer function. If M(z) denotes the over-all system function that
provides a minimal response (zero steady-state error and minimum
finite settling time) for a specific test signal, the staleness-weighting
factor is introduced into the system in the following manner:
Insertion of the factor (1 cz¹) into the denominator of
1 - M(z) yields
-
1 - M,(z)
=
-
1 - M(z)
(1 − cz¹)
(9-210)
where c is the staleness-weighting factor. For the over-all system to
be stable, c should satisfy the relation -1 < c < +1.
When the staleness-weighting factor is introduced into the denomi-
nator of 1 M(z), the characteristic equation of the compensated
† J. E. Bertram, "Factors in the Design of Digital Controllers for Sampled-data
Feedback Systems," Trans. A. I. E. E., 75, Part 2 July 1956, pp. 151–159.

Sec. 9.7
DESIGN AND SYNTHESIS BY DIGITAL CONTROLLERS 341
system is no longer of the form 2" = 0 and the error of the system
does not settle to zero in a finite period of time. However, the
addition of the staleness-weighting factor does not improve the
infinite sensitivity of the system response to parameter variations,
since the characteristic equation still contains a multiple order pole
at z = 0. With a proper choice of the value of c, the peak overshoot
of the step response can be reduced when the system is designed
for a ramp input, and the transient response can be reduced to a
tolerable amount within reasonable time interval.
The use of the staleness-weighting factor in the design of sampled-
data systems is illustrated by the following numerical example.

when it is written as a series or polynomial in z¨¹:

Unfortunately, the minimum values of the sums of the squared
errors of a ramp response and a step response often occur at oppo-
site ends of the permissible range of the staleness-weighting factor
c. In any given system, a minimum of the squared error can usually
not be found that will result in optimum performance for both the
step and the ramp inputs.
9.8 COMBINATION OF THE Z-PLANE SYNTHESIS
AND THE MINIMAL RESPONSE DESIGN
Sampled-data control systems designed by the minimal response
method described in the preceding sections, whether or not the
staleness-weighting factor is used, all contain the following disad-
vantages:
(1) The design is conducted on the basis of the response at the
sampling instants with the result that intersampling ripples may
exist in the output response.
(2) The characteristic equation of the system has multiple order
roots at z = 0, a condition which corresponds to that of the system
operating at a point with infinite sensitivity to parameter variations.
(3) Even with the staleness-weighting factor, a system which will
perform satisfactorily in response to various types of inputs is dif-
ficult to design.
In this section the z-plane synthesis method described in Sec.
9.5 will be combined with the design procedure for finite settling
time and zero steady-state error. In essence, a pair of complex control
poles are inserted in the over-all system transfer function for the
control over the transient response; and simultaneously, the system
must also have zero steady-state error for a ramp input. The domi-
† J. E. Bertram, op. cit.

344 DESIGN AND SYNTHESIS BY DIGITAL CONTROLLERS
Chap. 9
nant poles of M(z) are selected to lie in the first and the fourth
quadrants of the z-plane so that hidden oscillations do not exist in
the output response.
The basic concept and procedure of the proposed design technique
can best be explained by the following example.
Example 9-11
The sampled-data control system under consideration has the block diagram
shown in Fig. 9-35. The fixed part of the system is described by the transfer

compensator which will enable the system to meet the following set of design
criteria:
(1) The steady-state error of the system in response to a ramp function
input should be zero (K, = ∞).
(2) The overshoot of the step response should be as small as possible
and the rise time and the settling time as short as possible.t
(3) The damping ratio of the dominant control poles of C(z)/R(z) should
equal 0.707.
Since G,(z) has one more pole than zero, to insure that D(z) is physically
realizable, the over-all system function M(z) must have at least one more
pole than zero. To satisfy the zero steady-state error requirement on the
ramp response, 1- M(z) should contain the factor (1-1). This also
satisfies the requirement that 1 – M(z) must include the poles of G,(z)
which lie on or outside the unit circle in the z-plane. The poles and zeros
of G,(z) inside the unit circle are cancelled by the poles and zeros of D(z).

9.9 SYNTHESIS BY THE STATE VARIABLE AND
STATE TRANSITION APPROACH
The state variable and state transition concepts introduced in
Chapter 5 as a general approach to the analysis of linear discrete-
data control systems can be used for the purpose of synthesis. We
shall see that with the help of the variable gain concept, the state
transition method provides a simple and systematic synthesis procedure
for linear (as well as nonlinear) discrete-data control systems. The
development of the synthesis procedure makes use of the transition
flow graph and equations. Digital compensation is designed for the
linear system to have deadbeat performance. In Chapter 12 the same
technique is extended to synthesize nonlinear control for the com-
pensation of linear and nonlinear systems. The synthesis method
using the state variables concept is essentially a numerical method.
Once the synthesis procedure and equations are set up, the com-
putation can be carried out simply by a digital computer. The
method has one important advantage over the minimal response
design covered in Sec. 9.6 in that the state transition approach.
insures that the deadbeat response does not have intersampling
ripples.
Consider the sampled-data control system shown in Fig. 9-41.
The variables x1, x2, Xp, are the state variables of the controlled
process; h(t) is the output of the zero-order hold, and e(t) is the

(9-233)

the time interval kT <t≤ (k + 1)T.

designed is optimum only when a step input is applied to the system. The
system may not perform satisfactorily when any other basic test signal such
as a ramp or a parabolic function is applied.
In this section we have described the synthesis of control systems
with digital controllers by means of the state transition technique.
The synthesis procedure involves four major steps and is very simple
and straightforward. The synthesis method makes use of the variable
gain amplifier representation of the unknown controller. The content
of the controller is entirely general. Once the optimum K is deter-
mined for k = 0, 1, 2, 3, . . ., n, the controller could actually contain
linear elements, nonlinear elements, and time-invariant as well as
time-variant elements. The nature of the state transition design
assures that the output response of the synthesized system is free

Sec. 9.9
DESIGN AND SYNTHESIS BY DIGITAL CONTROLLERS 351
from intersampling ripples (provided that the sampling frequency is
high enough). However, the design is optimum only for the specific
input for which it is intended. The designed system does not provide
satisfactory performance for a wide class of inputs.

10
MULTIRATE AND SKIP SAMPLING
The sampled-data systems considered in the preceding chapters
have one feature in common; they contain a number of samplers
which operate in synchronism with the same sampling rate. Such
systems are usually described as single-rate sampled-data systems. In
general, there are systems which contain two or more samplers
operating with different sampling rates; the sampling may or may not
be synchronized. Systems having a sampler which operates with a
nonuniform aperiodic or cyclic-variable rate are also common; and
are called cyclic-rate sampled-data systems. The multirate or cyclic-
rate sampled-data systems may also be regarded as skip-sampled
systems, since the sampling schemes can be thought of as the result
of periodically omitting (or skipping) samples from a uniform sampling
operation.
In order to distinguish it from single-rate sampled systems a
multirate sampled-data system is defined here as a system containing
synchronized switches that do not operate with the same sampling
rate.
In many instances, the practical application of multirate sampled
systems arises from actual physical existence of such systems. This
may occur, for instance, in the case of flight-control systems when
a data link computer operates at a rate different from the speed of
the radar antenna. In addition to the above mentioned case, situa-
G. M. Kranc, "Input-Output Analysis of Multirate Feedback Systems," I. R. E.
Transactions on Automatic Control, November 1957, pp. 21-28.
352

Sec. 10.1
353
MULTIRATE AND SKIP SAMPLING
tions may arise where multirate sampling must be incorporated
intentionally to improve the performance of a given system. A digital
controller operating at a higher rate than the basic sampling rate of
the system is an example of this sort of situation. In a later section
we shall show that systems with properly designed multirate digital
compensators have better performance than those with single-rate
sampling. The skip or cyclic-rate sampling technique may also be
employed to obtain a novel type of controller with which deadbeat
performance can be achieved.
10.1 MULTIRATE SAMPLING
Open-Loop Systems
The block diagrams of typical open-loop systems with multirate
sampling are shown in Fig. 10.1. In the systems of Figs. 10-1a and
10-1b, the samplers are assumed to be synchronized, but one sampler
is operating N times faster than the other. In Fig. 10-1c the two
synchronized samplers operate with rates of 1/T₁ and 1/T2, respec-
tively. Usually, in order to simplify the mathematics of analysis, it
is necessary to assume that N is an integer and that T₁ and T₂ are
commensurable.

The previous section is devoted to the study of the multirate
sampling system of Fig. 10-1a through the use of the weighting
sequence and the z-transform methods. The purpose of going through
these seemingly complicated derivations of C(z) is to obtain a good
understanding of the characteristics of a multirate sampling system.
Actually, a simplified analysis of the system can be made by refer-
ring to the equivalent block diagram with skip-sampled samplers and
applying the signal flow graph technique. As has been shown pre-
viously, the signal flow graph method can be used effectively in the
study of multiloop sampled-data systems. The concept of the sampled-
flow graphs shall now be extended to sampled-data systems with
multirate samplers.
Let us consider the open-loop multirate system shown in Fig.
10-1b. Since the input signal is sampled with a rate of N/T and
since the output of the system is sampled with a rate of 1/T, the
analysis of the system is necessarily different from that described for
the system of Fig. 10-1a. The equivalent skip-sampled block diagram
of the system is depicted in Fig. 10-8a, and the equivalent signal
flow graph with skip-sampled samplers is constructed in Fig. 10-8b.

T = T/N₂, where T is the least common multiple of T, and T, and
N₁ and N, are integers. In general, if a sampled-data system contains
switches that operate with periods of T1, T2, . . ., Tn, a number T
and a set of integers N₁, N2, . . ., Nn can always be found such that
T₁ = T/N₁, T₂ = T/N2, T3 = T/N3, . . ., Tn T/Nn, provided that
the ratios of T₁ to T₂ to T3 to .. Tn are rational numbers. As
would follow, the two samplers of the system under study can be
replaced by equivalent skip-sampled samplers. The equivalent block
diagram of the system is shown in Fig. 10-9a, and the signal flow
graph of this equivalent block diagram is shown in Fig. 10-9b. The
z-transform of the system output is determined in the following

When a zero-order hold or a transportation lag is located in an
open-loop system with multirate sampling, the transfer function G(s)
necessarily contains terms of the form, ears G₁(s), where a is a posi-
tive number and G₁(s) is a rational function of s. Under such con-
ditions Eqs. (10-10), (10-27), and (10-34) are still valid for the
respective open-loop sampling schemes shown in Fig. 10-1, as long
as the exponential terms in G(s) are combined with the exponential
terms of the equivalent samplers.

Closed-Loop Systems With Multirate Sampling
The block diagram of a closed-loop system with multirate sampling
is shown in Fig. 10-11a. The two samplers are assumed to operate
with periods Ti =T/N₁ and T₂ = T/N2, respectively, where N₁ and
N₂ can be any finite nonzero integers. Since the presence of additional
multirate (or single rate) samplers would only increase the amount
of algebra necessary without affecting the method of analysis, the
system of Fig. 10-11a can be used to point out all the basic points
encountered in the general problem of analysis. The first step in the
procedure is to apply the skip-sampled technique, thus obtaining the
signal flow graph model shown in Fig. 10-11b. On this flow graph

numerical example is given below.

10.2 VARIABLE-RATE SAMPLING
In one class of sampled-data systems the samplers do not operate
with a uniform rate; that is, the durations between consecutive
sampling pulses are not constant. Such systems are called variable-
rate sampled-data systems. Although the sampling scheme of the
variable-rate samplers may have various forms, the following discus-
sion is limited to systems containing samplers that operate with
periodically variable rates; this type of system is described as the
cyclic-variable rate sampled-data system. As will be shown later,
cyclic-variable rate sampling offers many useful features such as the
improvement of the performance of a sampled-data system and the
time-sharing of equipment in discrete-data systems.
The block diagram of a typical cyclic-variable rate sampled-data
system is shown in Fig. 10-16a. The sampler S operates with a con-
stant sampling rate of 1/T samples per second, whereas the sampler
Se operates with a cyclic-variable rate at instants t = 0, T/N₁, T/N:,
T/N3, . . ., T/Nn; T, (1 + 1/N₁)T, (1 + 1/N½)T, (1 + 1/N3)T,
(1 + 1/N)T; 2T, (2 + 1/N₁)T, (2 + 1/N₂)T, . . ., (2 + 1/Nn)T; 3T,
where N₁> N₂ > N3> . . . >Nn>1. The sampling functions
of S and S. are described in Fig. 10-16b. An examination of Fig.
10-16b reveals that the cyclic-rate sampler Se can be replaced by a
parallel combination of the equivalent skip-sampled samplers used in
preceding sections. However, unlike the case of multirate sampling, the
the delays of the skip-sampled components here are not uniform but
are determined by the values of N1, N2, N3, . . ., Nn. For instance,
the kth skip-sampled sampler consists of a basic sampler with sampling

In order to illustrate the technique of analysis and the character-
istics of sampled-data systems with cyclic-variable rate samplers, two
numerical examples are given below.
Example 10-4
Consider the simple cyclic-variable rate sampled-data system shown in
Fig. 10-19a. Assume that the sampler operates at instants of t = 0, T/N, T,
(1 + 1/N)T, 2T, (2 + 1/N)T, 3T, . . ., (k + 1/N)T, (k + 1)T, . . ., where
N is a positive number greater than unity and T is equal to one second.
The sampling function of the sampler is depicted in Fig. 10-19b.
The purposes of this example are to determine the optimum value of N
for which the system is most stable and to show the effects of the variations
of N on the system performance.
The equivalent signal flow graph of the system is now depicted in Fig.
10-19c. The cyclic-rate sampler is decomposed into two basic samplers opera-
ting in synchronism with a sampling period of one second. The equivalent
sampler in the first branch is not accompanied by any advance and delay
units; the sampler in the second branch is preceded by an advance unit with
transfer function eTsN and followed by a delay unit with transfer function
e-TSN. Since the sampler in the second branch operates at the instants t = T/N,
(1 + 1/N)T, (2 + 1/N)T, . . ., (k + 1/N)T, . . ., the transfer functions of
the zero-order holds in the two branches are different and are given by

The characteristic equation has a fixed root at +0.368 and a variable root
whose location depends on the values of N and K. For the closed-loop cyclic-
rate sampled-data system to be stable, the roots of the characteristic equation
must lie inside the unit circle of the z-plane. Therefore, for stability the values
of K and N must satisfy the following condition:
|(1.368K² + 0.736K + 0.368) — (0.368e¹N + e¯¹/N)(K + 1)K| <1 (10-97)

(10-99)
The stability boundary curvest for the system are plotted from Eqs. (10-
98) and (10-99) in Fig. 10-20. Observe that when the sampler operates at
a uniform rate of one sample per second (T = 1 sec), the maximum gain
for stability is equal to 2.16; when the rate of the uniform sampling is in-
creased to two samples per second (T = 0.5 sec or N = 2), the marginal
value of K for stability is 4.1. The effect of the cyclic sampling is clearly
demonstrated by the stability boundary curves. For both large and small
values of N, the closed-loop system with cyclic sampling is stable even for
very high gains. For instance, when 1/N equals 0.1 or 0.9, the system is
stable for values of K between 31.75 and 35.1 (of course the sensitivity of the
system stability with respect to the variation in system gain K is poor).

10.3 STATE VARIABLES AND TRANSITION EQUATIONS
OF MULTIRATE AND SKIP-SAMPLED SYSTEMS
The state variable and state transition methods introduced in
Sec. 5.6 can readily be used to study sampled-data systems with
multirate- or variable-rate sampling. We shall demonstrate that the
state transition equations of a system with multirate- or variable-
rate sampling can be written out directly from the transition flow
graph in much the same way as those for a system with uniform-
rate sampling.
1. Cyclic Sampling

2. Nonsynchronous Sampling
A sampled-data system with nonsynchronized samplers is shown
in Fig. 10-27. As depicted in Fig. 10-27, the two samplers do not
operate at the same instants. The state transition technique is applied
to this system in a manner similar to that in which it was applied
to the cyclic sampling case. First, the transition equations at t = to
= kT immediately after the operation of S, are written to describe
the initial conditions on the state variables x1, x2, x3, and x₁. The
state transition flow graph for the time interval kT <t< kT + T₁
is drawn using these transition equations and connecting the flow
graphs of the continuous dynamic elements. The transition equations

11
STATISTICAL ANALYSIS AND
DESIGN OF SAMPLED-DATA
CONTROL SYSTEMS
11.1 INTRODUCTION
In many practical applications of control systems, the actual
signals and disturbances applied to a given control system are random
in nature, unlike the deterministic step, ramp, or sinusoidal functions
considered in the preceding chapters. These signals and disturbances
which are random functions of time can often be described only by
their statistical properties. Therefore, the treatment of control systems
must be considered with continuous as well as sampled-data for
actual random signals and disturbances.
The chief aim of the statistical design of control systems with
random inputs is the creation of an optimum system that will
render a minimum error between the actual output and the desired
output. The design criteria are based mainly on the minimization
of the mean square error, a concept which was first used by
Wiener. The mean-square-error criterion may not be ideal for all
control systems. However, the popularity of the criterion stems from
the fact that if the minimization of the mean square error is used
as the design criterion, the random signals or disturbances are suf-
↑ N. Wiener, The Extrapolation, Interpolation, and Smoothing of Stationary Time
Series, (New York: John Wiley & Sons, Inc., 1948).

ficiently described by their correlation functions and power spectral
density functions. Shown in Fig. 11-1a is a block diagram which
illustrates the formulation of a linear optimum system with continu-
ous-data, based on the minimum mean square error criterion. The
design objective is to select a system with the transfer function M(s)
where M(s) may represent an open-loop system or a closed-loop
system with constraints on open-loop elements in a manner that
would minimize the mean square value of the error e(t). The input
signal is assumed to be the sum of a random signal r(t) and a random
noise n(t). The error e(t) is defined as the difference between the
actual system output c(t) and the desired system output ca(t).
The concept of the minimum mean-square-error design of linear
continuous-data systems can be extended to the optimum design of
systems with sampled-data. Fig. 11-1b illustrates the block diagram
of optimum design of a sampled-data system. The design objective is
the determination of the z-transfer function M(z) that will minimize
the mean square sampled error e²(nT). In reality, the block diagram
of Fig. 11-1a may represent a sampled-data system if the actual
system with transfer function M(s) contains samplers or digital
elements.

STATISTICAL ANALYSIS AND DESIGN
11.2 STATISTICAL CHARACTERISTICS OF CONTINUOUS
RANDOM SIGNALS
In this section we shall briefly review some of the important prop-
erties of continuous random signals so that the concepts of statistical
description of random processes may be extended to discrete random
signals. Some previous knowledge of basic probability theory and
statistical theory is assumed. Therefore, the material presented here
consists of merely the definitions and descriptions of some important
terms and concepts often used in the analysis of random signals.
Sample Space and Sample Points. The aggregation of all possible
outcomes of a random experiment is called a sample space. With each
basic outcome of the experiment, a sample point may be assigned.
A sample space may consist of a finite number of sample points, and
the sample space is said to be discrete. For instance, as a die is
thrown, the sample space consists of six discrete sample points; as
a coin is thrown, the sample space consists of two discrete points,
namely, heads or tails. On the other hand, a discrete sample space
may consist of an infinite number of points and is called a continu-
ous sample space. A well-known example of a continuous sample
space is a thermal noise voltage caused by thermally excited electrons
in a conductor.
Random Variable. The sample points in a sample space are desig-
nated a variable that takes on the values of the points. A random
variable may be discrete or continuous in correspondence with the
discrete sample space or the continuous sample space. If the random
variable is denoted by X, then in the coin throwing experiment
X is discrete, X = heads or X = tails, whereas in a thermal noise
voltage, X is a continuous random variable and may take on all
possible values of the noise voltage.
Probability Density Functions. The probability density function,
p(x), describes the statistical characteristics of a random process.
The probability that a random variable X will lie in an infinitesimal
range between x and x + dx is
P(x < X < x + dx) = p(x) dx
(11-1)
The probability that the variable X will lie in the range x to x is
P(x₁ <X < x2) = = √ p(x) dx
(11-2)
When the random variable represents points of a random signal
or a random process that is a function of time, probability density

394 STATISTICAL ANALYSIS AND DESIGN
Chap. 11
functions of various orders may conveniently be defined. The first-
order probability density function p(x) can also be written as
p(x, t), denoting the probability that the variable X lies between
and x + dx at the time t. The second-order probability density func-
tion, p(x₁, t₁; x2, t₂), denotes the probability that the X lies between
x₁ and x₁ + dx₁ at t₁ and also lies between x2 and x2 + dx₂ at time
tą. Higher order probability density functions may be defined similarly.
Probability Distribution Function. The probability distribution
function P(x, t) is defined as the probability that the random vari-
able will be less than some value x at the time t. Therefore,
P(x,t) = P[X(t) < x] = ſ_p(x,t) dx
If P(x, t) possesses a first derivative, we have
(11-3)
p(x,t)
=
dP(x, t)
dx
(11-4)
Therefore, Eqs. (11-3) and (11-4) relate the probability density
function and the probability distribution function.
Time Approach and Ensemble Approach. In general, the statistical
properties of a random signal may be measured in two ways: the
time approach and the ensemble approach. In the time approach
measurements of the random signal are made beginning at some
time t₁. An ensemble approach may also be used in making statisti-
cal measurements. Assuming that we can collect a large number of
identical signal generators, the ensemble approach suggests the
measurements of the large number random signals simultaneously at
the time t₁.
Stationary and Ergodic Processes. If in a random process, the
statistical properties are constant and are independent of the time
origin at which they are measured, the process is said to be stationary
with respect to time. In the time approach, if the measurements of
a random signal made beginning at time t₁ produce identical results
to the measurements made beginning at time t₂ or at any other
time, the process is stationary. In the ensemble approach, a random
process is defined as stationary in time if the statistical properties
measured on a large number of random signals from identical sources
are the same, regardless of the time at which the measurements are
made.
A random process is said to be ergodic if its statistical properties
are identical under the time and the ensemble measurements. The
definitions imply that an ergodic process is always stationary, since
the stationary process is a presupposition of ergodicity. However, not

Sec. 11.2
STATISTICAL ANALYSIS AND DESIGN 395
all stationary processes are ergodic. A random process may be shown
to be stationary under the time ensemble measurements, but the two
results may not be identical.
Moments and Averages. The nth moment of a random variable
x(t) is defined to be the statistical average of the nth power of x(t):
_x"

where p(x, t) is the probability density function of x.
The first moment of x(t) is just the average value of x(t);
that is,
av[x(t)] = x(t) = (x(t)) = √__xp(x,t) dx
(11-6)
Equation (11-6) gives the average from the ensemble approach. We
could, however, perform a time averaging process. The time average
of x(t) is simply

In the equation above p(x, t₁; y, t) is the probability that x has a

probability density function of x and y.
Correlation Functions. The correlation functions are measurements
of the statistical dependence of one random signal upon another or
upon itself. If x(t) and y(t) are two random processes or signals,

the cross-correlation between x and y is represented by the cross-

the correlation functions are looked upon as time functions (of 7),

11.3 STATISTICAL CHARACTERISTICS OF SAMPLED
RANDOM SIGNALS
(11-19)
In the preceding sections we have defined correlation functions
and power spectral density functions which are used for the charac-
terization of continuous random signals. These concepts can also be
extended to the statistical descriptions of sampled or discrete random
signals. As has been pointed out, there are two ways of determining
the correlation functions of random processes: the time average and
the ensemble average. However, in sampled-data systems, in addition
to the two ways of averaging, there are at least two possible ways
of considering a sampled signal. First of all, we can consider the
values of a signal at the sampling instants only. In this case, the
signal is treated as a sampled sequence or a sequence of numbers.
Another way of considering a signal is to treat it as a sampled signal
consisting of a train of amplitude modulated pulses. Referring to
Fig. 11-2a, r(t) is a member of a stationary random process which

First, let us consider that the signal r(t) is characterized by the
values at the sampling instants only. The sampled sequence is denoted
by r(nT), where n = 0, ±1, ±2, ±3,..., ±∞. We shall now show
that if r(t) is assumed to be a member of a stationary ergodic process,
r(nT) is also a stationary ergodic random sequence, and the time
averaging and ensemble averaging are equivalent for this discrete
case. Furthermore, the sampled sequence averages are equal to the
time average of the continuous time function r(t).
Since r(t) and r(nT) are all ergodic, the second probability density

In addition to being a function of the shift variable 7, the auto-
correlation function determined from the ensemble approach is also
a periodic function of time. This fact checks with the physical
interpretation of ensemble averaging. Since any number of the en-
semble of r*(t) has values only at the sampling instants t = kT, the
average of the ensemble has to be evaluated at the sampling instants.
and has zero values at all other times. The difference between Eqs.
(11-36) and (11-38) is illustrated by the typical correlation function
of *(t) shown in Fig. 11-4. The time-averaged autocorrelation
function of r*(t) is time-invariant and is shown to be a sequence of
impulse sheets. The ensemble-averaged autocorrelation functions of
r*(t) appear as impulse trains in a two-dimensional t versus 7 plane.
In the following sections only the time-invariant correlation functions
will be considered. Design of optimum sampled-data systems using
the time-invariant correlation functions has been investigated by
DeRusso.t

11.5 MEAN-SQUARE ERROR
As is well known, when the minimization of the mean-square
error is chosen as the performance criterion, the design of optimum
linear continuous-data control systems may be carried out using the
descriptions of the stationary signals in the system by correlation.
functions and spectral densities. Evidently, the mean-square error
criterion can also be extended to the design of optimum linear
sampled-data control systems. In this section, we shall relate the
mean-square errors of some typical sampled-data systems to the
correlation functions and the spectral densities of the input and the
output signals. The systems are assumed to be free from noise and
disturbances. Although open-loop configurations are illustrated, the
derivations can be extended easily to closed-loop systems.
Let us consider the sampled-data system shown in Fig. 11-9. The
system is characterized by having sampled inputs and outputs. The

As is evident now, the mean-square error may be expressed as a
function of the power spectral densities of r(t) and r*(t) and the
transfer functions M(s) and Ma(s). In an analysis problem, e²(t)
may be obtained from Eq. (11-107) if Mɑ(s), M(s) and r(t) are
specified. In the design problem, r(t) and Ma(t) are specified, and
the design objective is to determine M(s) so that e²(t) is a minimum.
The transfer function M(s) used here is entirely general and could
represent a feedback system with or without sampling.
However, the evaluation of the integral of Eq. (11-107) usually
may present some difficulties, since M(s) in sampled-data systems
usually contains the term e-T as well as s. If e(t) can be accurately
described by the error sequence e(nT), the mean-square sampled
error can be used as a close approximate of the true error. In this
case, the z-transform method may be applied. The sampled error is
not a good measure of e(t) if the system has inter-sampling ripples
or hidden oscillations. A compromise may be made by using the
modified z-transform technique. The "modified" error sequence may
be written as
e(nT, m) = ca(nT, m)

The mean-square error of the system is plotted in Fig. 11-13 as a function
of wo and T. When T is equal to zero, e²(t) is also zero; when T approaches
infinity, e²(t) approaches the value 2. When T is held constant, increasing
wo will increase the mean-square error as expected.
Since many sampled-data systems utilize zero-order hold for data-recon-
struction, further investigation must be conducted on the statistical behavior
of the hold device. Referring to Fig. 11-12, we see that the actual system
consists of an ideal sampler and a zero-order hold. As Fig. 11-4a and Eq.

11.6 OPTIMUM SYNTHESIS USING MEAN-SQUARE
ERROR CRITERION
The principle objectives of the preceding section are the introduc-
tion of the mean-square-error criterion and the establishment of the
relationship between the statistical properties of the input and out-

418 STATISTICAL ANALYSIS AND DESIGN
Chap. 11
put signals of sampled-data systems. In this section, we turn our
attention from analysis to synthesis. Furthermore, in all the discus-
sions of the previous section, we have neglected noise and interference
in control systems. However, noise and interference are present in
most practical control systems, and they tend to obscure the signal
used for the optimalizing control and are the basic restrictions in
the design of optimum control systems.
The statistical synthesis of sampled-data systems in the sense of
minimum mean-square error can be readily visualized by referring
to the block diagram of Fig. 11-15a. M(z) is the closed-loop transfer

The synthesis procedure developed below is essentially an exten-
sion of the theory of optimum design of linear filters developed by
N. Wiener and A. Kolmogorofft and is also parallel to the work of
J. T. Tou. The application of the Wiener-Kolmogoroff criterion to
the design of linear continuous-data control systems is well known
and is given in the literature.§ The theory of the optimum system
design by Wiener and Kolmogoroff is based on an application of the
calculus of variations to the integral of Eq. (11-135). If M(z) is an
optimum transfer function, e²(nT) must be at a minimum. Replacing
the optimum M(2) by a function M(z) + eŋ(z) where e is a param-
eter and (2) is an arbitrary but physically realizable function of
z would increase the mean-square-sampled error to e²(nT) + ▲e²(nT)
where Ae²(nT) is the change in e²(nT). Then, a necessary condition
that e²(nT) is a minimum is

NONLINEAR SAMPLED-DATA
CONTROL SYSTEMS
12.1 INTRODUCTION
Problems occurring in practice originally stimulated interest in
the study of sampled-data systems containing nonlinear elements.
As in systems with continuous signals, account must often be taken
of physical imperfections such as saturation, backlash, dead zone,
and hysteresis of components in sampled-data systems. Most physical
systems can be considered linear only within a limited range of
their operations. If a sampled-data system is designed according to
the linear theories and methods discussed in the preceding chapters,
the components used in the system must operate in linear fashions
under various operating conditions. If under certain circumstances
the components are driven into regions of nonlinear characteristics,
linear design theory describes the system performance only approxi-
mately or quite often gives a completely erroneous prediction of it.
Broadly speaking, a nonlinear system is one for which the
principle of superposition is no longer valid. In linear systems
mathematical analysis is characterized by the fact that if the separate
application of inputs ri(t) and r2(t) produces responses c₁(t) and
c2(t) respectively, the simultaneous application of signals ri(t) and
r2(t) will produce an output c₁(t) + c₂(t). This is why in linear
systems, system response need be analyzed only to the deterministic
type of signals such as the step function, ramp function, acceleration
430

Sec. 12.1
NONLINEAR SAMPLED-DATA CONTROL SYSTEMS 431
function, and the sine function. These signals can then be combined
to approximate signals of almost any kind. Unfortunately, this
unique property of superposition does not carry over to nonlinear
systems. This fact is also a basic reason why nonlinear differential
equations (nonlinear difference equations for sampled-data systems)
are more difficult to solve and thus require special attention. At
present, no definite set of mathematical tools exists to solve a general
class of nonlinear systems with either sampled or continuous data.
Furthermore, when sampling and nonlinearity are present in the
same system, new phenomena arise which can hardly be described by
the theories and techniques of linear sampled-data systems or by
that of nonlinear system without sampling. The interaction between
sampling and nonlinearity brings about a whole class of complicated
and unexpected problems.
Although nonlinearities in feedback control systems generally are
due to shortcomings of physical components, in one class of systems
nonlinear elements are intentionally introduced in order to improve
system performance. In systems with continuous-data, the on-off or
relay-type servot such as the automobile voltage regulators and on-off
furnace controls are common examples of this class of nonlinear
system. The concept of relay control is also extended to sampled-
data systems. Actually, with the addition of the sampling switch,
nonlinear control of sampled-data systems becomes more versatile;
several investigators have recently suggested the pulse-width controlt
which is a modified version of the ordinary relay control in which
continuous control is exercized over the duration of the full positive
or negative correction applied by the relay to the controlled system
during each sampling period.
Two well-known methods, which have proved both useful and
popular in the study of continuous-data nonlinear feedback control
systems, are the describing function and the phase-plane methods.
† H. L. Hazen, "Theory of Servomechanism," J. Franklin Inst., September 1934;
H. R. Weiss, "Analysis of Relay Servomechanisms," J. Aeronaut. Sci., July 1946;
D. A. Kahn, “An Analysis of Relay Servomechanisms," Trans. A.I.E.E., 1949.
R. F. Nease, "Analysis and Design of Nonlinear Sampled-Data Control Systems,"
WADC Tech. Note 57-162, Servomechanisms Lab., Massachusetts Inst. of Tech., June
1957.
W. L. Nelson, “Pulse Width Control of Sampled-Data Systems,” Tech. Report T-35/B,
Columbia University, July 1959.
R. J. Kochenburger, “A Frequency Response Method for Analyzing and Synthesizing
Contactor Servomechanisms," Trans. A.I.E.E., 69, 1950, Part 1 pp. 270-34.
A. A. Andronow, and C. E. Chaikin, Theory of Oscillations trans. Lefschetz, S.,
(Princeton, N. J.: Princeton University Press, 1949).

432 NONLINEAR SAMPLED-DATA CONTROL SYSTEMS
Chap. 12
Also, the first published studies of nonlinear control systems with
sampled-data are the extensions of these two above mentioned
methods. For systems which have low-pass frequency response chara-
cteristics, the possible unstable behavior of the systems may be
assumed to consist of periodic, quasi-sinusoidal oscillations approxi-
mated by their first harmonic component. The method is essentially
a steady-state analysis; it does not give any information on the
transient behavior of the system. On the other hand, the phase
method may be regarded as a direct study of the system difference
equations. The phase plane diagram gives indication of both the
transient and the steady-state behavior of a system in the time
domain. However, as is well known, the major limitations of the
phase plane technique are that only second-order systems (or higher-
order systems which can be approximated by second-order systems)
can be effectively studied by the method, since for high-order systems
the phase space becomes difficult to visualize, and it cannot be
extended to systems with sinusoidal inputs.

figuration to describe the development of the analysis procedure of
the state transition technique.

has two consecutive corrections in the positive direction and two in the

It is both interesting and instructive to find the plots of the system output
c(t) due to different initial conditions. When the initial conditions of x₁(0)
= −0.1 and x,(0) = 0.6 are assumed, the nonlinear system has an autonomous
periodic response of period 6T. This is equivalent to saying that the dif-
ference equations that describe the nonlinear system have a periodic solution
of period 6T. Similarly, by assigning various other combinations of x,(0)
and x(0) as initial conditions of the system, other possible modes of sustained
oscillations are discovered. When x,(0) = 0.1, and x,(0) = 0.4, the period of
oscillation of c(t) is 4T, and the relay output signal has one correction in
the positive direction and one in the negative direction during each cycle.

period of 5T. We shall see in a later section that with T = 1 second and
relay dead zone D = 0.1, the modes mentioned above are the only possible

case, the required digital controller and the nonlinear element are re-
presented by a variable gain amplifier. However, because of the non-
linear aspect of the system the state transition technique cannot be
relied upon to provide a design solution for all nonlinear systems.
12.3 THE PHASE PLANE METHOD
Phase plane method is a graphical technique of solving linear
and nonlinear differential equations. However, phase plane method
has been applied to control systems predominantly in the studies of
nonlinear systems simply because the Laplace transform method of
solving linear differential equations is so convenient and straight-

forward that there seems to be no advantage in applying the graph-
ical method to linear systems.
If a second-order system is characterized by the dependent varia-
ble c(t) and its first derivative with respect to time dc(t)/dt, the
phase plane diagram of the system is a plot of de/dt as a function
of c. At any time t₁, the coordinate [c(t₁), dc(t)/dt\-] in the phase
plane determines the "state" of the system at tt. As time is
varied from 0 to ∞, the representing point (c, c') will trace out
a continuous path which indicates the complete history of the sys
tem in regard to c and c' at all times; the path itself is called the
phase plane trajectory. For instance, in a servo system the phase
tc' represents the first derivative of c(t) with respect to time.

plane diagram is usually a plot of the output velocity c' as a func-
tion of the output displacement c. In Fig. 12-7 the typical phase plane
diagram and the corresponding output response of a second-order
servo system with unit step function input are shown. The arrows
on the phase plane trajectory indicate the increase in time. For an
electrical network a plot of current i as a function of electric charge
q also forms a phase plane diagram.
The phase plane concept may also be extended to higher order
systems or differential equations. However, the representation of the
state of the system for higher order systems requires higher-dimen-

440

sional plots. This is usually the main difficulty in applying phase
plane technique to systems with order higher than two, since a mul-
tidimensional "phase space" diagram is very difficult to visualize
and construct. In addition to the above-mentioned limitation, the
phase plane technique is normally used to study only transient and
steady-state performances of systems subject to only step function
and linear time function inputs and/or with initial conditions. For
continuous-data systems, we know that the phase plane technique
does not solve problems involving systems with sinusoidally excited
systems. However, we shall show in this chapter that it is possible
to apply the phase plane method to sampled-data systems with sine.
wave inputs.

Singular Points
The points (c = 1, v = 0) in the phase plane diagrams of Fig.
12-18 are called the singular points of the phase plane trajectories.
A singular point may generally be classified as a focus point, a sad-
dle point, or a vortex. The classification depends entirely on the
variations of the coefficients of the differential equation or the roots
of the characteristic equation. For instance, the singular point in
Fig. 12-18b is a vortex; and the singular point in Fig. 12-18c is an
unstable focus point.
If in Eq. (12-14) r(t) = 0,

When m(t) = 0, the phase plane trajectory is reduced to a simple
straight line described by
C - Co =
-(v — Vo)
(12-32)
Equation (12-31) is actually the equation of the phase plane tra-
jectory for a second-order relay-type system with continuous-data as
well as with sampled-data, since up to this point no mention is made
of the fact that the derivation of Eq. (12-31) is based on sampled-
data only. As is apparent, Eq. (12-31) is valid as long as m(to) is
constant or zero. The difference between the phase plane trajectory
of a sampled-data system and that of a continuous-data system ap-
pears to be in the switching time of the relay. For a continuous-

448
Chap. 12
NONLINEAR SAMPLED-DATA CONTROL SYSTEMS
data system, the switching of the relay depends strictly on the
magnitude of the actuating signal e(t). But for a sampled-data sys-
tem, since e*(t) is a discrete function of time, the switching not only
depends on the magnitude of e*(t) or e(t) at the sampling instants
but can occur only at the sampling instants. The construction of
the entire phase-plane trajectory of a relay-type system with sampled
data may appear to be difficult, since time does not appear ex-
plicitly on the phase plane trajectory. Fortunately, a set of switch-
ing lines can be derived in the phase plane; the intersections of
the phase plane trajectory and the switching lines indicate the pos-
sibility of relay switchings. The details are described as follows:
-
=
For a sampled-data system, we let t to T, and the state
transition takes place from Cn to Cn+1 and vn to Un+1, respectively,
where cn denotes the value of c(t) at t = nT and vn denotes the
value of v(t) at t = nT. Cn+1 and vn+1 are the values of c(t) and
v(t) at t = (n + 1)T, respectively. Substituting these quantities into
Eq. (12-31) yields.
Cn+1 − Cn = − (Vn+! — Vn) — M₂ ln (Mn — Un+1

we must determine exactly where the point Cn+1, Un+1 lies on the
trajectory. The time for the representing point on the trajectory to
travel from (cn, Un) to (Cn+1, Un+1) is equal to one sampling period
T. The relationship between Cn, Un and Cn+1, Un+1 can be obtained
from Eq. (12-28); that is
Cntton = cntont mặt

Equation (12-34) gives the sum of the coordinates of the switching
points as a function of the initial conditions (Cn, vn), the relay
switching function, and the sampling period T. In general, m, and
Tare known quantities, so that for given cn and vn, the correspond-

point (C+1, V+1) in the phase plane is determined from the intersec-

jectory passing through (cn, vn).
