=
The first step in the construction of the phase plane diagram involves the
drawing of the set of switching lines as shown in Fig. 12-13. The reference
switching line, which is described by the equation co+v 0, has a slope of
-1, and passes through the origin in the phase plane. All other switching
lines have the same slope and are spaced at a horizontal distance of integral
multiples of T from the co + % = 0 line. At t = 0, the error signal is +1;
hence m。 = +1. The operating point will move from the origin along one of
the +1.0 trajectories shown in Fig. 12-11 until the trajectory intersects the
switching line cn + Vn = T and the intersection determines the (c1, v₁) point.
Since c₁ is less than one, e, is positive, and m₁ = +1. The operating point
will continue to move along the same trajectory until it intersects the next
switching line cn + vn = 2T and the intersection point gives (c₂, v₂). Now
C₂ is greater than the magnitude of the reference input; hence e, is negative,
and m₂
1. The operating point must now be switched to and must move
along the -1 trajectory that passes through the (c₂, v₂) point until the trajec-
tory intersects the line c + Vn T and the point of intersection corresponds
to (C3, v3). As is apparent, c3 is still greater than unity; therefore, m3 = −1
and the operating point will continue to move along the -1 trajectory until
it intersects the switching line în + vn 0, and at the intersection m₁ = +1
the operating point is switched to a +1 trajectory. From Fig. 12-13 we can
see that as the above-mentioned switching process continues, the system will
eventually become unstable and exhibit a limit cycle. The period of the limit
cycle shown in Fig. 12-13 is 4 seconds, and the magnitude of the limit cycle
is approximately 0.85. The output response and the relay switching function
m(t) of the system are shown in Fig. 12-14.

As shown in Fig. 12-16, the set of switching lines are drawn in the phase

Figure 12-16 shows that the phase plane trajectory finally exhibits a limit
cycle with a period of 5 sec. (T = 5T). The amplitude of oscillation is
approximately 1.12, peak-to-peak.
Note that the system response indicated by the phase plane diagram of
Fig. 12-16 cheks quite closely with the response of Fig. 12-5b, which is
obtained by use of the state transition method. The phase plane method gives
the exact continuous response at all times, whereas the difference equation

454 NONLINEAR SAMPLED-DATA CONTROL SYSTEMS
Chap. 12
method, being an analytical method, produces the response only at the
sampling instances, and the actual response in between sampling instants
must be estimated from the discrete data. This fact explains why the estimated
response of Fig. 12-5b shows an amplitude of oscillation of only 0.52 peak-
to-peak, whereas the actual amplitude is 0.56.
The reader may find it interesting and instructive to check the responses
shown in Figs. 12-4 and 12-5a and c using the phase plane method in the
manner described above.
A stabilizing effect may be achieved if the relay is allowed to have a
dead zone. This fact has been shown previously in Sec. 12.2 by the state
transition approach, and two stable responses of the relay-type sampled-data
system are shown in Fig. 12-6 when the relay dead zone is 0.6. To illustrate
the stabilizing effect of the dead zone, the phase plane diagrams of the two
responses of Fig. 12-6 are plotted in Fig. 12-17.

Several interesting points are observed from the phase plane diagram of

stable steady-state value of c(t) for T = 1 sec. is zero because operating point
of the system at the sampling instants must lie on one of the switching lines,
and in this case the only one of these lines which intersects the horizontal
(v0) axis inside the dead zone is the line cn + vn A, and the point of
intersection is at the origin in the phase plane. Also, for the initial conditions
of Co
1.5 and v。 = 0.5 with r(t) = 0, the system is stable only if the point
a on the phase plane trajectory lies inside the dead zone. Therefore, the
width of the dead zone required for the system to be stable under the specified
initial and input conditions can be determined from Fig. 12-17. In a similar
fashion, the width of the dead zone required for stability when the system
is subjected to a step input of any magnitude can be determined.

Phase Plane Diagrams of Systems With Sinusoidal Inputs
The phase plane method can also be applied to a second-order
relay-type system which has a sine wave input signal. In fact, the
method described in the following paragraphs can be used to approx-
imate the response of a continuous-data system with relay and a
sine wave input. In this case the sampler and the hold circuit of
the system in Fig. 12-1 become fictitious elements.
When analyzing relay-type sampled-data systems with sinusoidal
inputs, the relationship between the signal frequency and the samp-
ling frequency is of great importance. In general, the ratio of the
signal frequency to the sampling frequency can be one of the fol-
lowing: (1) an integer, (2) a rational number, and (3) an irrational
number. The phase plane analysis is simplest when the frequency
ratio is an integer. But the phase plane method can also be applied
if the frequency ratio is a rational number or an irrational number.
The method is best illustrated by a numerical example.
Example 12-5
Consider the relay-type sampled-data system shown in Fig. 12-1. The
sampling period is 1.0 sec., G(s) = 1/s(s + 1), and the relay characteristic is
described by Eq. (12-5). The initial conditions are c₁ = 0, and
Vo 0. A

centered at the origin, and the operation point is switched to a +1 trajectory.
As this process is carried on, Fig. 12-18a shows that the phase plane diagram
exhibits a limit cycle after the initial transient. The period of the oscillation
is equal to the period of the input signal (4 sec). The input signal, input
sequence, and the output signal of the system are plotted in Fig. 12-18b.
Fig. 12-19 gives the phase plane diagram and the output response of the
same system under the same input and initial conditions, but with the dead
zone increased to 0.4. The output response is again a sustained oscillation
with period equal to twice the period of the input sinusoid.
The preceding example has demonstrated that the phase plane
method can be applied to a relay-type sampled-data system with a
sinusoidal input in a straightforward manner. The main essence in
the method is the identification of the magnitudes of the input
sequence r which must be taken into account when computing
switching at the sampling instants. Clearly the method can also be
applied to cases where the signal frequency to sampling frequency
ratio is a rational number or an irrational number. However, in the
case of the irrational ratio of frequenies, the input sequence does
not vary periodically, and therefore, the output response is not
periodic. Normally, an infinite amount of time would be needed to
carry out the entire output response.
As is clear, the method described above is not limited to only
sampled-data systems with sinusoidal inputs. The method can be ap-
plied to a relay-type sampled-data system with almost any input as
long as the input sequency r is defined at the outset of the analysis.
For a ramp input, the phase plane analysis should be conducted in
the e versus e' plane, since the output response c would increase
with time.
12.4 THE INCREMENTAL PHASE PLANE METHOD+
Difference Equations
In the preceding section, the idea of the conventional phase plane
has been applied to the analysis of nonlinear sampled-data system.
The conventional phase plane method consists essentially of writing
↑ J. A. Aseltine, "Nonlinear Sampled-Data System Analysis by the Incremental Phase
Plane Method," Proc. of the International Federation of Automatic Control, Moscow,

the second-order differential equation of a system as a pair of first
order equations involving two dependent variables (usually one of
the variables is the derivative of the other) and then plotting the
paths generated in the phase plane by the two variables with time.
as a parameter.
We have found that difference equations bear the same relation
to sample-data systems as ordinary differential equations do to con-
tinuous-data equations. This implies that an 'incremental phase
plane" may be defined as to consist of the plot of the first difference
of a variable versus the variable itself.

The Incremental Phase Plane (The A-plane)
The incremental phase plane, or the A-plane, is defined as a plane
which has the first difference of a variable as ordinate and the varia-
ble itself as abscissa. If a second-order sampled-data system is char-
acterized by the dependent variable c(t), the incremental phase
plane diagram of the system is a plot of the first difference Ac₂ of
the sequence c(nT) as a function of cn. Each solution point in the
A-plane represents a value of cn and the first difference Ac₁ for n =
0, 1, 2, 3, . . .. Hence the trajectory in the A-plane is a curve that
passes through all the solution points (cn, Acn) for n = 0, 1, 2, 3, . .
. . ..
However, we should point out that the difference equation approach

462 NONLINEAR SAMPLED-DATA CONTROL SYSTEMS
Chap. 12
gives solutions only at the sampling instants. Therefore, although
the trajectory of the A-plane is a continuous curve, only discrete
points on the trajectory that correspond to sampling instants are
significant.
Let us use the relay-type sampled-data system of Example 12-1
to illustrate the construction of the A-plane trajectory. If assump-
tion is made that the relay contacts are able to open and close in-
stantaneously with zero duration, the positions of the relay and the
hold device of the system can be interchanged without affecting the
analytical results of the system. The equivalent system, with the
relay and the hold device transposed, is shown in Fig. 12–21.

operating point at t = 2T is still found on the K = 0.632 trajectory,

>1,
must be drawn passing through the (c₁, Ac₁) point; and the next

property of the trajectory (K = −0.104), both point a and point
b satisfy the relationship
C₂ = Ac₁ + C₁
(12-79)
This ambiguity is removed by recognizing the fact that time pro-
gresses in the direction of the arrows along the trajectory; thus a
change of the operating point in either direction from the point
(c₁, Ac₁) along the trajectory must cause a change in the correspond-
ing direction in the point (C2, Ac₂). As shown in Fig. 12-23a, if the
operating point is shifted in the forward direction along the trajec-
tory to (ci, Ací), the point at a moves in the same direction to a',
whereas the point at b moves in the opposite direction to b'.
Therefore, a is the correct location for (c2, Ac₂). It can be shown
that for the K = +0.104 and the K = -0.104 trajectories, whenever
an ambiguity arises, such as between points a and b in Fig. 12-23a,
the nearest point reached along the trajectory is the correct one.
In Fig. 12-23b, the K = +0.632 trajectory is shown plotted through
an initial condition point (co, Aco). With the same reasoning used
above, we see that the point b rather than point a is the correct
point for the next switching point (c1, Ac₁). In general, we can state
that for the K = +0.632 or the K = -0.632 trajectories, the far-
thest point reached along the trajectory is the correct switching
point.

Based on the foregoing analysis of the relay-type sampled-data
system, the incremental phase plane method would seem to have
hardly any advantage over the conventional phase plane method.
The construction of the conventional phase plane diagrams of relay-
type sampled-data systems simply relies on the use of simple fixed
templates of the +1 and -1 trajectories. The switching lines are
derived and are drawn in the phase plane, and the intersections of
the switching lines and the phase plane trajectories give the response
quantities at the sampling instants. Moreover, the conventional phase
plane method gives the response not only at the sampling instants,
but at all times. However, how the conventional phase plane method
can be extended to analyze sampled-data systems with nonlinearities
other than the relay type is not at all clear. On the other hand,
although the incremental phase plane method is more difficult to
use in analyzing relay-type systems, it can be used to analyze a varie-
ty of other nonlinearities. We shall illustrate this feature by con-
sidering a sampled-data system with saturation.
The block diagram of a sampled-data system with saturation is
shown in Fig. 12-25.

12.5 THE DESCRIBING FUNCTION METHOD
The describing function method introduced by Goldfarb and
Kochenburgert has been a powerful and popular tool in the analysis
and synthesis of nonlinear systems with continuous-data. The des-
cribing function enables one to study the steady-state sinusoidal re-
sponse of a nonlinear system of any finite order. The application of
the describing function method relies on the following assumptions
and considerations:
+ L. C. Goldfarb, "On Some Non-linear Phenomena in Regulatory Systems," Avto-
matika i Telemekhanika, 8, No. 5, 1947, pp. 349-83.
R. J. Kochenburger, "A Frequency Response Method for Analyzing and Syn-
thesizing Contactor Servomechanisms," Trans. A. I. E. E., 69, Part 1, 1950, pp. 270-284.

472 NONLINEAR SAMPLED-DATA CONTROL SYSTEMS
Chap. 12
r(t)
e(t)
n
G(s)
c(t)
Nonlinear
Controlled
element
process
(linear)
Fig. 12-29 Nonlinear feedback control system with continuous-
data.
(1) The continuous-data system contains only one nonlinear ele-
ment. A typical form of the nonlinear system for describing func-
tion studies is shown in Fig. 12-29.
(2) The input to the nonlinear element n is assumed to be
sinusoidal. The output of n is generally not a sine wave, but never-
theless, it is a periodic function and can be represented by a Fourier
series. Only the fundamental component of the output of the non-
linear element is assumed to be significant. Actually, if the input to
n is considered to be sinusoidal, the output of n will contain com-
ponents at the fundamental frequency and in general at all higher
harmonic frequencies. Since the harmonics in the outputs of most
of the common types of nonlinearities encountered in servo systems
are often of smaller amplitudes than the amplitudes of the funda
mental components, most servo systems act as low-pass filters so that
the higher harmonics in the output of the nonlinear elements are
attenuated when compared to the fundamental component.
When the basic assumptions listed above are satisfied, the non-
linear element n can be represented by an equivalent transfer func-
tion called the "describing function," which is defined as the ratio
of the fundamental component of the output to the amplitude of
the input. Therefore,
Describing function N
=
Fundamental component of output from Fourier analysis
Amplitude of the sinusoidal input signal
(12-90)
Once the describing function of the nonlinear element of a given
system is determined, it can be used to study the stability of the
nonlinear system. If the nonlinear element is properly represented
by its describing function N, where N is generally a complex num-
ber and a function of the input signal amplitude and frequency, it
follows that the closed-loop transfer function of the system in Fig.
12-29 is
=
C(s) NG(s)
R(s) 1+ NG(s)
(12-91)

Sec. 12.5
NONLINEAR SAMPLED-DATA CONTROL SYSTEMS 473
The stability of the closed-loop nonlinear system depends upon the
zeros of the equation
1+ NG(s) = 0
(12-92)
Just as in the case of the linear system, if all the zeros of Eq.
(12-92) are located in the left-half of the s-plane, the system is
stable; if one or more zeros are found in the right-half of the s-
plane, the system is unstable. The roots of Eq. (12-92) may also be
interpreted as the condition when
G(s) = -1/N
(12-93)
Normally, the stability analysis can be carried out in either of the
following domains: (1) the Nyquist diagram, or (2) the gain-phase
plot. In the case of the Nyquist analysis, for instance, the system sta-
bility is investigated by plotting the G(s) and the -1/N loci in the
polar coordinates. The G(s) plot is the ordinary frequency locus of the
linear part of the system, but the -1/N locus can be considered to
be the locus of the critical point, which in linear theory is the (-1,
jo) point in the complex G-plane. When N is a function of only
the input signal magnitude E to the nonlinear element, the critical
points of -1/N form a locus which is a function of E. If N is a
function of both E and w, a family of loci must be constructed;
on each locus @ is constant, but E is a variable parameter.
The relative position of the G(s) locus and the -1/N plot in-
dicates the stability condition of the nonlinear system. When the
critical points of -1/N lie to the left of the G(s) locus or are not
enclosed by it (assuming that G(s) is itself a stable system func-
tion), the closed-loop system is said to be stable, since any distur-
bances which appear in the system will tend to die out. Converse-
ly, when any part of the -1/N locus lies to the right of the G(s)
locus and thus is enclosed by it, the implication is that any distur
bances which are characterized by the values of E corresponding to
the enclosed critical points will provide unstable operations. The
intersection of the G(s) and -1/N loci corresponds to the possibili-
ty of a periodic oscillation characterized by the value of E on the
-1/N locus and the value of on the G(s) locus. For the situation
in which the -1/N locus is a function of both E and w, periodic
oscillation is possible only if the values of w at the intersection are
identical on the two loci. The periodic solution described above may
be stable or unstable despite slight disturbances in amplitude E or
frequency from the equilibrium condition. If the equilibrium state
is a stable one, the amplitude of oscillation tends to return to its
original value following a disturbance. If the equilibrium is unstable,
the oscillation either increases or decreases in amplitude depending

The system shown in Fig. 12-30 demostrates the extension of
the describing function method to the study of nonlinear sampled-
data control systems. Early contributions on the study of relay-type
sampled-data systems by means of the describing function technique
have been made by Chowt and Russell. In these early investigations
the nonlinear element n is considered to include the sampling switch,
the zero-order hold, and the on-off relay. Thus the system configu-
ration of the sampled-data system is identical to that of the con-
tinuous-data system shown in Fig. 12-29. For a sinusoidal input
e(t) whose period is an integral multiple of the sampling period T,
the output of the nonlinear element n is a periodic rectangular
wave. Therefore, the conventional describing function technique or-
dinarily used for continuous-data systems can be applied directly.
The nonlinear sampled-data system is essentially treated as a non-
linear continuous-data system, since the sampler is absorbed by the
nonlinear element.
In this section a new describing function called the "discrete
describing function" is derived utilizing the z-transformation. In
this case the nonlinear element n in the sampled-data system is
considered to include only the on-off relay. The discrete describing
function method has the following advantages and features:
(1) The discrete describing function is natural for sampled-data
systems; it is analogous to the use of the conventional describing
function for continuous-data systems.
(2) The discrete describing function method can be applied to
systems with or without zero-order hold devices
(3) Systems with more than one synchronized sampler can be
analyzed.
† C. K. Chow, "Contactor Servomechanisms Employing Sampled-Data," Trans.
A.I.E.E., 73, 1954, pp. 51-64.
E. A. Russell, “Design Criterion for Stability of Sampled-Data On-Off Servo-
mechanisms," Ph. D. thesis, Columbia University, New York, 1953.
§ B. C. Kuo, "The z-transform-Describing Function for Nonlinear Sampled-Data
Control Systems," Proc. of the I. R. E., 48, No. 5, May 1940, pp. 941-942.

(4) The actual output of the nonlinear element, rather than the
fundamental component of the Fourier series, is considered.
Stability of the System
The nonlinear sampled-data system under investigation is shown
in Fig. 12-30. The sampler is assumed to be ideal, meaning that the
sampler output e*(t) is an impulse train magnitude modulated by
the input e(t). The relay characteristic has a dead zone D and was
given previously by Eq. (12-1). The stability of the nonlinear sampled-
data system is defined as follows: If the system has a self-sustained
oscillation in response, the system is said to be unstable; other-
wise, it is stable. In other words the present analysis deals mainly
with the condition of sustained oscillations when the reference input
r(t) becomes zero after some time t.
..."
In the error signal e(t) is sinusoidal with period TenT, where
n = 2, 3, 4, .. and T is the sampling period in seconds, the sampler
output e*(t) must also be a periodic function, although it may not
have the same frequency as e(t). Since e*(t) can have values only
at the sampling instants, the period of e*(t) must also equal nT.
Thus when the characteristics of the zero-order hold are considered,
the relay, G(s), the functions h(t), m(t), and c(t) see Fig. 12-30
must also have the same period as e*(t). This is a very important
feature of this system.
Under the condition of self-sustained oscillation, r(t) = 0; hence
e(t) = r(t) − c(t) = −c(t)
(12-94)
which means that e(t) must also have the same period as c(t).

The derivation of the discrete describing function of a nonlinear
element is based on the assumption that the input signal to the
nonlinear element is a sinusoidally modulated impulse train. The dis-
crete describing function is defined as the ratio of the z-transform
of the output v*(t) to the z-transform of the assumed sinusoidally
modulated input e*(t). Thus
N(z) = V(z)/E(z)

The critical region of Te = 4T and A = 2 is plotted in Fig. 12-37.
Examination of the expression derived for -1/N(z) and the con-
ditions on e(t) that give each value of ▲ leads to the following
conclusions:
(1) The discrete describing function N(z) of the relay under
consideration is a function of the relay dead zone D, and of the
frequency a, the amplitude E, and the phase of the input sinusoid
e(t).
(2) The regions bounded by the loci of -1/N(z)|x_and
-1/N(2) nin in the gain-phase plot for TenT (n = 2, 3, 4, . . .)
are symmetrical about the -180 deg axis. The maximum widths.
for the critical regions are (2π/Tc)T for Te = 4T, 6T, 8T, . . ., nT,
n = even integers, and (27/T.)(T/2) for T. = nT, n = odd integers.
When n is very large, the critical regions are very narrow and final-
ly approach a straight line which lies on the 180 deg axis when
n is infinite.
-
(3) For certain values of E and ø, −1/N(z) is infinite, and the
critical regions extend to infinity (open region). The asymptote of
the -1/N(z) Inax boundaries is always the -180 deg. axis, whereas
the asymptotes of the -1/N(z)in boundaries are (27/T.)(T/
2 r. nr for n = even integer> 2, and ±(2π/Tc)(T/4)|r=nr for
n = odd integers greater than 1.

Stability Analysis and Limit Cycles From Gain-Phase
Plot and Describing Function
-
The condition of self-sustained oscillation in the system of Fig.
12-30 is defined as existing when the denominator of Eq. (12-98) is
equal to zero; that is,
1 + N(z) G₁(z) = 0
Equation (12-120) can also be written as
G₁(2) = -1/N(z)
(12-120)
(12-121)
Since the left-hand side of Eq. (12-121) is the z-transfer function of
the linear plant and the right-hand side consists of the negative in-
verse of the z-transform describing function of the nonlinear ele-
ment, the stability analysis of the nonlinear system with sampled-
data follows the well-known procedure of the describing function.

488 NONLINEAR SAMPLED-DATA CONTROL SYSTEMS
Chap. 12
and gain-phase plane studies of a nonlinear system with continuous-
data. In terms of the gain-phase plot of G₁(z), the graphical stabili-
ty study of the sampled-data system consists of the following steps:
(1) Plot G₁(z) on gain-phase coordinates for Tc = 2T, 3T, 4T, . . .
using T as a parameter on the loci.
(2) Superpose on the gain-phase coordinates the family of critical
regions of 1/N(z) for T. = 2T, 3T, 4T,
The following conclusion can be reached from the inspection of
the relative position of the G₁(z) loci and the critical regions:
(1) If a portion of the G₁(z) locus for some Te falls within the
critical region of this T. for a certain relay dead zone D (when D
varies, the critical regions of −1/N(2) simply shift up or down along
the 180 degree axis), then a set of E, 6, A and Te, exists such
that
=
-
G₁(2)=-1/N(2)
Consequently, for any sampling period T the portion of the G₁(z)
locus that lies in the corresponding critical region will produce a
self-sustained oscillation characterized by ▲ at Te, p, and E, and the
system will be unstable.
(2) If the G₁(z) loci for more than one value of Te fall within
their respective critical regions, for the same value of D the system
can have more than one mode of oscillation.
(3) The T's along the portion of the G.(z) locus outside the
corresponding critical region provide a stable system.
A
One special condition exists when the relay dead zone D becomes
zero and the relay is considered to be ideal. From the physical view-
point the relay will provide a corrective output whenever there is
a signal at its input, no matter how small this signal may be. Under
this condition some types of oscillation disappear in the nonlinear
system, and some can only appear for several discrete values of p.
For instance, when D = 0, the sustained oscillation of the types, T. =
4T and ▲ 1 can occur only in the system when the phase angle
of e(t) is a multiple of ±7/2. This fact can be observed by referring
to Fig. 12-37 in which the critical regions for A = 1 and 2 are
shifted to
- along the -180° deg axis when D becomes zero.
Evidently, the critical region for A= 2 degenerates into a region.
which is bounded by two vertical straight lines with the left- and
the righthand boundaries located at -225 deg and -135 deg,
respectively. The critical region for A = 1 degenerates into a single
vertical line along the -180 deg axis. For T. = 6T and D = 0, self-
sustained oscillation of the type A = 1 can no longer exist. This is
due to the period of 6T of the sinusoidal e(t). This e(t) will produce
more than two-relay corrective signals in either direction during

Sec. 12.5
489
NONLINEAR SAMPLED-DATA CONTROL SYSTEMS
=
----
one period, whereas A = 1 specifies that only one positive and one
negative relay correction can be made during each period T..
Referring to Fig. 12-39, the critical region for A = 1 is a closed
region; when this closed region is shifted to ∞ along the -180
deg axis, it is no longer expected to enclose any portion of the
G₁(2) locus for T. 67. Similarly, the critical region for A = 3
becomes a vertical strip centered along the -180 deg axis with its
left and right-hand boundaries located at the -210 deg and -150
deg lines, respectively. The critical region for A 2 is narrowed
down to a vertical line along the -180 deg axis. For T. = 8T, the
sustained oscillations are characterized only by A3 and A = 4,
and those of ▲ = 1 and 2 are eliminated.
=

An interesting comparison can be made of the results that are obtained in
this example by use of the sinusoidal analysis with describing function to
those that are computed in the same system by the state transition method
in Sec. 12.2. In Sec. 12.2 the same relay sampled-data system is analyzed by
means of the difference equation approach. Figures 12-4 and 12-5 show the
four different modes of oscillations of the system when the system is subjected
to various initial conditions. The amplitudes of oscillation of c(t) are tabulated
in the last column of Table 12-3. Whereas the difference equation approach
gives the solution for only a particular set of initial conditions, the deter-
mination of the possible modes of oscillations under various initial conditions
is extremely difficult. However, the describing function method introduced in
this section does point out that for the system under study with T = 1 sec.
and D = 0.1, only the four modes of oscillations listed in Table 12-3 are
possible under any initial conditions.
=
The results in Table 12-3 show that the amplitudes of self-sustained
oscillation which are predicted by the two different methods are quite close,
except for mode 2 when Te 4 sec and ▲ = 2. Note that both the difference
equation and the discrete describing function methods deal with the system
response at the sampling instants only. Furthermore, the describing function
method assumes that the input to the sampler is sinusoidal and that only the
fundamental component of the relay output is considered to be significant.
In reality, the oscillations in a nonlinear system are seldom sinusoidal.
Therefore, the accuracy of the sinusoidal analysis depends entirely on how
much the output c(t) differs from a sine wave. In fact, the amplitudes of

492 NONLINEAR SAMPLED-DATA CONTROL SYSTEMS
Chap. 12
oscillation listed in Table 12-3 which are determined by the discrete describing
function are extremely close to the results obtained by Chowt using the
conventional describing function method.
Nonlinear Systems with More than One Synchonized Sampler
One distinct advantage of the discrete describing function method
is that it can be applied to a nonlinear system with more than one
synchronized sampler. Figure 12-43 shows the block diagram of a
nonlinear sampled-data system with two samplers synchronized to
open and close at the same time. The closed-loop transfer function
of this multi-sampler system is

Nonlinear Systems Without Zero-Order Hold-Finite Pulse Width
Considerations
The nonlinear system studied in the preceding sections is con-
sidered to have a zero-order hold following the sampler. If the zero-
order hold is absent and if the sampling duration of the sampler
can be assumed to be infinitesimal, the describing function technique
presented here can still be applied. Instead of plotting G₁(z), which
includes the transfer function of the hold, only the locus of G(z)
in the gain-phase plot need be plotted using the same critical regions.
However, in some cases, without a hold device a finite pulse
+ C. K. Chow, op. cit.

Sec. 12.5
NONLINEAR SAMPLED-DATA CONTROL SYSTEMS 493
width has to be considered. In this case, an approximation can be
made if the narrow pulses with width p are approximated by flat-
topped pulses. The sampler can then be represented by an ideal
sampler followed by a fictitious hold device which holds the sampled
signal for p second only and then drops to zero instantaneously until
the next impulse comes along. The transfer function of G₁(s) is
written
G₁(s)
――

(12-135)
(12-136)
A discrete describing function utilizing the z-transformation has
been introduced for a relay-type nonlinearity for sampled-data systems.
The critical regions which are defined by the describing function
are used to study the condition of self-sustained oscillations by
investigating the relative position of the G₁(z) loci and the critical
regions. The effect of varying the sampling time T on the system
stability is shown clearly on the G₁(z) loci. As has been shown,
the method can be applied to multisampler system as well as to
systems without hold devices. We might note that the discrete
describing function method can also be applied to other types of
amplitude dependent nonlinearities such as saturation with dead zone.
